Everything (⌘F surface)

How to work

The two taxonomies

The system stack: model → posttraining → scaffold → tools → product → deployment → company → institution. Every capability claim, obligation, and incident attaches at one of these levels, and naming the level is half of most analyses.

The claim ladder: measurement → inference → forecast → assurance → recommendation. Label what you read. Never let a document ride a measurement into a recommendation without showing the rungs between.

The five work moves

  1. Evidence brief — six lines: claim, method, population, uncertainty, what it can't establish, the decision it could support.
  2. Closed-book reconstruction — shut the resource, rebuild the central diagram or equation, then check.
  3. Claim grading — extract consequential claims, grade the evidence, name the author's institutional position, state one unresolved crux.
  4. Ledger — running claims file for anything you'll cite later; every entry sourced and scoped.
  5. Perturb one variable — change one consequential thing, predict the output, run it, explain the difference.

Each core reading names its move. Attempt before answers, always.

The rubric

Mechanism · evidence · transfer · policy judgment, each 1–4, pass at 3-of-4. The anchors (what a 2 versus a 3 looks like) are on the rubric page, and the acceptance test on each artifact is the bar.

Build: it runs; the acceptance test passes; one deliberately-broken variant behaves as predicted.

Memo: recommendation up front; every strong sentence survives the source-scope-uncertainty audit; the strongest counterargument appears at full strength; a named principal could act on page one.

Pace rules

  • Weeks are sequence positions, not calendar weeks.
  • No catch-up debt. Resume points forward, missed weeks stay missed.
  • Fast-pass is a first-class move: every week opens with its pass task; demonstrate it and skip.
  • After any gap ≥2 weeks, re-enter through the tutor's refresher quiz plus the phase cold-start summary.
  • Designed exit points with standalone value: W18, W28+R7, W45+S. Stopping at one is an outcome, not a failure.

The standing practices

Six, cadenced by position rather than date; full spec in DEPTH.md §5: forecast log, replication, genre gym, adversarial practice, publishing + diet swap, human contact.

Two counting rules: a quarter with no external anchor didn't count, and a quarter with no named-human exchange didn't count either.

Any memo-type artifact is written as if it might publish, since 6–8 per year will and the flagship goes through IFP editorial as work product. The minister-explanation drill is standing: any week's mechanism, three sentences, no jargon lost, no mechanism lost.

Foundation gateway

Read

  • The Python tutorial (docs.python.org) — chapters 3–5, plus 6 and 8 if imports and tracebacks read as noise, and the NumPy quickstart. Repair for check 1; redo the pass task afterward without copying.
  • Virtual environments and packages (Python tutorial ch. 12) and Software Carpentry's git-novice (lessons) — repairs for checks 2 and 3, an evening each. git-novice stops short of branching; learn that leg from the pass task.
  • The claim ladder (How to work) — repair for check 8. Grade five claims from one system card against it, asking what decision each could and could not support.

Do

Attempt each pass task cold: fresh kernel, closed book, nothing copied. Pass and move on. Fail and take the matching repair route above, then repeat the task, still cold. Nothing in Read is required unless its check fails.

  1. Python and notebooks. In a fresh notebook, load a small CSV, clean one column, compute a grouped statistic, draw a labeled chart, and rerun the whole notebook from a clean kernel without errors.
  2. Files, environments, and reproducibility. Create an isolated environment, install pinned dependencies, save a requirements file, and reproduce your check-1 result in a new directory from that file alone.
  3. Version control. Create a repository, commit a working analysis, branch, make a change, inspect the diff, merge it, and recover an earlier version without deleting current work.
  4. Tensor shapes and matrix multiplication. Predict, then verify, the output shapes for a batch of token embeddings pushed through three projection matrices and an attention score product.
  5. Linear transformations, rank, and projections. Explain a matrix as a transformation, compute the rank of a small example, and show concretely what a projection preserves and what it discards.
  6. Probability and Bayes. Solve a base-rate problem, compute an expectation and a variance, and explain why a posterior depends on both the evidence and the prior.
  7. Derivatives and the chain rule. Differentiate a two-layer scalar computation by hand, label each local derivative, and verify the result numerically.
  8. Evidence discipline. Take one published AI claim and separate the measurement, the inference, the forecast, the assurance, the recommendation, and the missing evidence.

Done when all eight are demonstrated, passed cold or repaired and then passed cold, and recorded: one line in progress.yaml, or tell the tutor and it writes the line.

Take away

  • The gateway routes; it does not grade. A cold pass and a repair are equally good outcomes. The only bad state is an undemonstrated "I'm sure I know this."
  • Recognition is not demonstration. "I took a course on this" and "the video made sense" are the kinds of evidence this gateway exists to replace. Cold conditions are the conditions you will need these under in W13, W18, and every memo after.
  • Checks 4–7 have no external repair route because W2–W5 are the repair material. A fail means taking those positions at full speed instead of fast-passing them: W2 and W3 for checks 4 and 5, W4 for check 6, W5 for check 7. Redo check 7 afterward with a different nonlinearity, to prove it wasn't memorized.
  • Checks 1–3 re-check formally in W6, which reruns check 2 as its opening diagnostic. Repair earlier only when a gap blocks the week you are in.
  • Check 8's move, asking what decision a claim could and could not support, is the habit every artifact downstream is graded on. W1 runs it again on documents you have already read.
Check yourself
  1. You pass six checks cold but fail 1 (Python) and 6 (probability). What happens next, in what order, and what will count as evidence that each has finally passed?
  2. Why do checks 4–7 route into W2–W5 rather than to an external course?
  3. Where does check 8's repair route point, and what is the move once you're there?

Answers. (1) Nothing gets recorded yet. Check 1's repair starts now, the Python tutorial plus the NumPy quickstart, but its formal re-check waits for W6, where the pass task runs again cold and uncopied. Check 6 resolves first: don't fast-pass W4. What counts as evidence is doing the task cold, meaning fresh kernel, closed book, no copying; course history and familiarity are recognition, the failure mode the gateway screens out. (2) Those weeks are the repair material; routing elsewhere duplicates the content and loses the pass tasks that verify it. A math fail here is a signal to take those sequence positions at full speed, not a prerequisite failure. (3) The claim ladder in How to work, applied to five claims from one system card, asking of each what decision it could and could not support. W1 then runs the same grading move on the two scenario documents you re-read there.

Calibration

Read

  • AI 2027 (ai-2027.com) — core, the race branch. Mark every load-bearing assumption, then read titotal's critique and the Futuresearch six-month scorecard.
  • Situational Awareness (situational-awareness.ai) — core, same drill. Note what kind of document it is: one dominant scenario with a program backward-chained from it. You read it a third time in S1.
  • Carlsmith, "Is power-seeking AI an existential risk?" (arXiv:2206.13353) — the six power-seeking premises. Write them out with your probabilities before rereading his, then multiply through.

Do

Fill Matrix v0 (the matrix, kept in matrix/matrix.md in the repo): the ~20 cells you believe you already know, with sign, conditional, and crux. Expect confident signs and empty conditionals. Leave the gap; it is your before-photo.

Write two dossiers (the seed files sit in dossiers/ in the repo), your highest-credence threat model first and your lowest second. Each gets the causal pathway in your own words with your probabilities, the strongest published critique at full strength, cruxes with credences, and one falsifiable 12-month prediction.

Log the Carlsmith sheet in cruxes.md and ten forecasts in forecasts/log.md, both in the repo: sub-12-month Metaculus AI questions, with the reference class and base rate written down for each.

Done when all of it is dated and committed, every dossier crux carries a credence and a named piece of evidence that would move it, and every forecast entry names its reference class. No credence, no pass.

Take away

  • Two taxonomies run through everything and live permanently in How to work: the system stack (model / posttraining / scaffold / tools / product / deployment / company / institution) and the claim ladder (measurement / inference / forecast / assurance / recommendation).
  • Every technical week sharpens one taxonomy; every policy artifact is graded against both.
  • Familiarity is not reconstruction. You can recite "misaligned power-seeking AI could disempower humanity." Until you can write that argument as premises with probabilities and say which premise you'd bet against, you hold a conclusion rather than a position.
  • Six months in, the Futuresearch scorecard puts reality at roughly 60–70% of AI 2027's scenario pace.
  • You arrive post-BlueDot, post-AI-2027, so nothing here explains what a scaffold is. The gap that fluency leaves is rebuilding the arguments with your own numbers.
  • "It depends" is the disease this week treats. A crux with no number and no named observable is a preference.
  • Decision: define which systems fall within a frontier reporting or oversight regime. EU Article 51's presumption, SB 53's 10²⁶ FLOP plus revenue test, and the rescinded EO 14110 lines are all fights about where in the system stack obligations attach and which claim types trigger them. R1 gives you the instruments; S4 gives you why the thresholds are decaying.
Check yourself
  1. Name a live governance obligation that attaches at each of: model, deployment, company. Why does the level matter?
  2. A system card states: "Claude scored X% on our internal cyber eval; we assess it does not meaningfully uplift attackers." Decompose into claim-ladder types.
  3. What's your credence that your dossier-1 threat model's distinguishing crux resolves in its favor within 24 months, and what would you observe next quarter if it were on track?

Answers. (1) Model: EU Art. 51 systemic-risk classification, keyed to training compute. Deployment: SB 53 transparency reports at or before deployment. Company: whistleblower duties and corporate-structure commitments. The level decides who is regulated when a fine-tune, a scaffold, or an open release changes hands, and the same capability at a different level can escape the regime entirely. (2) The score is a measurement of a benchmark, with a construct-validity question attached; "does not meaningfully uplift" is an inference stacked on an assurance; and the sentence quietly functions as a recommendation to deploy. Nothing in it is a forecast, since it says nothing about capability change over time. (3) No fixed answer. The test is whether you wrote a number and an observable. If you couldn't, redo dossier 1.

Linear algebra as transformations

Read

  • 3Blue1Brown, Essence of Linear Algebra (3blue1brown.com) — core: "Linear combinations, span, and basis vectors," "Linear transformations and matrices," "Matrix multiplication as composition," "Inverse matrices, column space and rank," "Change of basis." Draw where î and ĵ land for one 2×2 matrix, then verify in code.
  • Mathematics for Machine Learning (Deisenroth, Faisal, Ong; mml-book.com) — core, most of the week's load: Ch. 2, 2.1–2.7, with weight on 2.6 Basis and Rank; Ch. 3, 3.1–3.8. Rebuild the 3.8 projection derivation closed-book with every dimension labeled.
  • Dive into Deep Learning (d2l.ai) — core, at the keyboard: 2.1 Data Manipulation, 2.3 Linear Algebra, appendix 22.1 Geometry and Linear Algebraic Operations. Predict the output shape before every change you make to a tensor example.

Do

One fresh notebook, four sections, every figure generated by your code. Before each rerun, predict the picture or the rank from the matrix entries alone, then look.

  1. Transformations. Push a grid of 2D points through at least four matrices, including a rotation, a shear, and a rank-1 collapse, plotting before and after each. Compose two and confirm the order matters. Check every rank claim with np.linalg.matrix_rank.
  2. Basis change. One vector's coordinates written in two bases, with the round trip shown.
  3. Rank. A full-rank and a rank-deficient 3×3, with a plot of what the deficient one collapses, plus a 3D-to-2D projection.
  4. A tiny embedding space. About 20 words as hand-built feature vectors (five or so dimensions: animacy, size, whatever you choose), projected to 2D and plotted with labels.

Done when you can point to two words the section-4 plot places close together and show, from the full vectors, that a discarded dimension separates them. If no such pair exists, change the projection until one does, and write down what you dropped.

Skip if you can already explain P = [[1, 0], [0, 0]] as a transformation with a picture, state its rank, name what it keeps and what it throws away, and do the same for a rank-2 3×3 of your own construction.

Take away

  • A matrix's columns tell you where the basis vectors land, and linearity does the rest. Composition is one move after another, rank counts the dimensions that survive, and a projection flattens space onto a subspace. A neural layer is a learned choice of which directions to keep.
  • Knowing the recipe is not knowing the object. Row-times-column arithmetic tells you how to compute Ax, not what A does. An explanation that never says where space goes is bookkeeping, and W13's projections will read as symbol soup.
  • The projection you draw this week is the Q/K/V projection with learned entries. W12's embeddings are the rows of one such matrix, and W3 asks which directions of a transformation carry the action.
  • Decision: certify or challenge a technical briefing's description of embeddings, projections, or model shrinkage. Article 51's 10²⁵ FLOP presumption assumes big capabilities require big models, and distillation is the standard route around that assumption.
  • The Hamburg DPA's discussion paper Large Language Models and Personal Data (15 July 2024; English PDF) argues that storing an LLM is not processing under GDPR Article 4(2) "because no personal data is stored in LLMs".
  • The EDPB's Opinion 28/2024 (17 December 2024) takes the contrary position: a model trained on personal data cannot in all cases be treated as anonymous, and anonymity must be shown case by case against extraction and inference attacks.
  • The disagreement is technical before it is legal. The operative question in both is what the map discarded and what test would show it.
Check yourself
  1. P = [[1, 0], [0, 0]]: what does it do to the plane, what is its rank, and what does it destroy, recoverably or not?
  2. A is 4×3 and B is 3×2. What does AB map from and to, what is the largest rank AB can have, and why can composing maps never raise rank?
  3. Two analysts plot the same 20 word vectors in 2D and get visibly different pictures; neither made an error. What happened, and which claims should you trust?
  4. Attention computes XW_Q, XW_K, XW_V, the same X through three learned matrices. From this week alone: why push the same vectors through three different projections instead of using X directly?

Answers. (1) It flattens the plane onto the x-axis, (x, y) → (x, 0). Rank 1: one dimension survives. The y-coordinate is destroyed unrecoverably, since infinitely many inputs share each output. (2) AB maps R² to R⁴, read right to left with B first. Its rank is at most 2, the minimum of the dimensions and ranks involved: B's image is already at most a 2D subspace, and A can move that subspace but cannot add dimensions to it. Transformations collapse space; none invent it. (3) They projected onto different 2D subspaces, and each picture shows what its projection preserves. An orthogonal projection never increases distances, so separation visible in either plot is real, while closeness may be an artifact of a pair the map merged. Anything beyond that gets checked in the full-dimensional space. (4) A projection selects which directions of a space to expose. Three different matrices let one vector show three faces, three learned answers to "which features of this vector matter right now," and that is the mechanism W13 builds on.

Singular values and low rank structure

Read

  • Mathematics for Machine Learning, Ch. 4: Matrix Decompositions (Deisenroth, Faisal & Ong; mml-book.com) — core: 4.1–4.2 and 4.4–4.6, with 4.3 Cholesky optional. Write the SVD statement with every dimension attached, plus Eckart–Young, closed-book before checking.
  • "We Recommend a Singular Value Decomposition" (David Austin, AMS Feature Column; ams.org) — optional: the geometric picture on small 2×2 examples, ending in compression and noise reduction. Read it before the build if Ch. 4 lands as symbol-pushing.
  • LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models (Hu et al., 2021; arXiv:2106.09685) — optional: the abstract, §4.1 (the ΔW = BA parametrization), §7.2 (what the optimal rank r turns out to be). Read it as evidence that the update a fine-tune needs is approximately low rank.

Do

Truncated-SVD image compression, one notebook, one image. Load a grayscale photo as a matrix, compute its SVD, reconstruct at a sweep of ranks, and plot two curves: the singular value spectrum, and reconstruction error against rank. Find the elbow. Then reconstruct from the bottom k singular directions instead of the top k, predicting what you will see before you look.

Write it up as a visual explainer plus your compression case: one page walking rotation → scaling → rotation on your own example matrix, then the spectrum plot, three reconstructions (well below, at, and above the elbow), and the error-vs-rank curve. Close with the minister drill, the full mechanism in three sentences with no jargon lost.

Done when your error-vs-rank plot shows the elbow and you can say in one sentence what the discarded singular values contained, pointing at your bottom-k reconstruction as the demonstration.

Skip if you can already compute the SVD of a 2×2 matrix by hand, state Eckart–Young from memory, and explain the elbow in an error-vs-rank plot.

Take away

  • A rank-k approximation keeps the k directions with the largest singular values and discards the rest. Eckart–Young says the truncated SVD is the best rank-k approximation there is, in both Frobenius and spectral norm.
  • That promise is minimal reconstruction error, in a norm. It says nothing about which directions any particular task depends on.
  • Small singular values are not necessarily unimportant ones. Discarded directions are low energy, not low relevance. "We compressed it and nothing important was lost" is a claim to check, not a corollary of the math.
  • Attention (W13) is built from low-rank projections and sends you back here if this week is shaky. A LoRA fine-tune (W24) is a rank-r update to a frozen weight matrix, which works only when the needed update has a fast-decaying spectrum.
  • Decision: assess a low rank compression claim used in an assurance case. Evaluations attach to a checkpoint, and EU AI Act Article 55 obliges providers of systemic-risk GPAI models to run model evaluations and adversarial testing.
  • What ships is routinely a compressed variant: quantized, pruned, distilled, or low-rank-factorized. A reconstruction-error bound is a measurement; "capabilities unchanged" is an inference that only re-running the evaluations on the deployed variant supports.
  • The UK AI Security Institute describes its own access as API access to particular model checkpoints, sometimes "with different safeguards to those implemented on the publicly available version of the model" (Frontier AI Trends Report). The wedge is per-variant evaluation written into the documentation requirement.
Check yourself
  1. Every matrix has an SVD, but not every matrix has an eigendecomposition. Why, and how do the singular values of A relate to the eigenvalues of AᵀA?
  2. State what Eckart–Young guarantees about the rank-k truncation. A vendor writes: "the deployed model is a compressed version of the evaluated model; capabilities are unchanged." Which claim-ladder rung is that sentence standing on, and what should you demand?
  3. Your image is 512×512 and you keep rank 50. How many numbers do you store versus the original, and, from your own notebook, what visual content lived in the values you dropped?
  4. A fine-tune replaces W with W + BA, where B is m×r, A is r×n, r tiny. What has to be true about the needed update for this to work, and what observation would falsify it for a given task?

Answers. (1) Eigendecomposition needs a square matrix with a full basis of eigenvectors; the SVD exists for any m×n matrix because AᵀA is symmetric positive semidefinite, hence always orthogonally diagonalizable. The singular values of A are the square roots of the eigenvalues of AᵀA. (2) Guaranteed: among all matrices of rank ≤ k, the truncated SVD minimizes approximation error in Frobenius and spectral norm. Not guaranteed: preservation of any downstream behavior, since a direction with tiny norm contribution can be the one a task depends on. The vendor sentence reads as a measurement but is an assurance resting on an unstated inference. Demand the evaluations re-run on the deployed variant. (3) Rank-k storage is k(m+n+1) numbers: 50 × (512+512+1) = 51,250 versus 262,144, roughly a fifth. The bottom-k reconstruction answers the second half: fine texture, grain, and sharp edge detail, not large-scale structure. (4) The update the task requires must lie approximately in a few directions, a fast-decaying singular value spectrum for the true ΔW. Falsification: task performance keeps climbing materially as you raise r, or the SVD of a full fine-tune's weight change shows a flat spectrum.

Probability, likelihood, and information

Read

  • Probability for Computer Scientists (Piech, Stanford; chrispiech.github.io) — core, the bulk of the week: Part 1 Core Probability and Part 2 Random Variables. Complete the embedded problems; they are why this text was chosen.
  • Dive into Deep Learning (d2l.ai) — core: 2.6 Probability and Statistics, then appendix 22.7 Maximum Likelihood and 22.11 Information Theory. Show closed-book that minimizing cross-entropy over a corpus is maximizing likelihood.
  • Deep Learning (Goodfellow, Bengio & Courville; deeplearningbook.org) — optional if D2L felt thin: Ch. 3 Probability and Information Theory, Sec. 5.5 Maximum Likelihood Estimation. Put every modeling assumption into your claims file in plain language, one line each.

Do

Retake gateway check 6 first: one base-rate problem, one expectation and variance computed by hand, and a written answer to why a posterior needs both evidence and a prior. Repair only what failed; Piech's problems are the repair material.

Then a short notebook. Pick a three-outcome distribution p and a model q. Compute H(p), H(p, q), and the average NLL of samples drawn from p and scored under q. Verify that H(p, q) ≥ H(p) with equality only at q = p, and that the sampled NLL converges to H(p, q) as you draw more. Then move q's probability mass around while keeping its argmax fixed, and watch cross-entropy change while greedy-choice accuracy doesn't. Write one sentence on what that means for reading a loss curve as a capability trend.

Then the derivation set, closed-book and then checked: (1) Bayes' theorem from the definition of conditional probability; (2) the reduction of H(p, q) to −log q(y) when p is one-hot at the observed token y, and the corpus average of that as the language-model training loss; (3) perplexity as e^(cross-entropy), with the branching-factor reading written out. Finish with a one-page memo addressed to someone citing a perplexity improvement as a capability claim (a lab comms post or eval summary is the right target), recommendation up front, per the memo rubric in How to work.

Done when the memo names three distinct things a perplexity number cannot establish, each pinned to a property of the definition rather than a vibe. Three restatements of "it might not generalize" fail.

Skip if gateway check 6 passed clean on the first attempt: read D2L 22.11 only, then write the memo cold.

Take away

  • Cross-entropy loss is the negative log probability the model assigned to the token that actually came next, averaged over a corpus, so training on it is maximum likelihood estimation and nothing more exotic.
  • Perplexity is e^(cross-entropy): the effective number of tokens the model is choosing among, uniformly, at each step.
  • Lower cross-entropy is not closer to truth. The loss sees one thing, q(observed token), and it is computed against a corpus rather than the world. A model that perfectly fits a corpus of falsehoods achieves minimal loss on it.
  • Lower loss means better compression of a distribution, which on the claim ladder is a measurement of fit. "The model got smarter" is an inference needing its own evidence.
  • Every loss curve is entropy-of-the-data plus a model-specific KL term, and only the KL part is the model's to reduce. The irreducible floor belongs to language, not the architecture, and W20 builds its scaling curves on that decomposition.
  • When the condition being screened for is rare, false positives swamp true positives, so a flag mostly means "flagged." Any screening regime needs a base-rate-aware second stage.
  • Decision: choose what likelihood, confidence, and calibration evidence a deployment standard should accept. Loss and perplexity are the cheapest evidence a developer can supply, and they will be offered wherever EU AI Act Article 55's systemic-risk model evaluations or NIST AI RMF-style measurement requirements leave "evaluation" underspecified.
  • Such a number measures fit to a reference distribution, in nats per token. Converting it into a capability claim takes a validated link from loss to task behavior, which is what W20's scaling laws attempt to supply. Article 51's presumption and SB 53's FLOP test inherit that dependency.
Check yourself
  1. An audit test flags a dangerous capability in 99% of models that have it and 5% of models that don't; one submitted model in a thousand has it. A model is flagged. What is the probability it has the capability, and what does the answer imply for screening regimes?
  2. Show that when the target distribution is one-hot at the observed token, cross-entropy reduces to the negative log likelihood of that token. For fixed p, what is the minimum of H(p, q) over q, where is it attained, and what does the gap H(p, q) − H(p) equal? A model averages 2.3 nats per token: what is its perplexity, in words?
  3. Two models post identical perplexity on the same eval corpus. Give two mathematically distinct reasons you cannot conclude they are equally capable.
  4. A model assigns probability 0.9 to a token. Distinguish three claims someone might build on that number: the probability statement itself, a confidence claim, and a calibration claim.

Answers. (1) P(capable | flag) = (0.99 × 0.001) / (0.99 × 0.001 + 0.05 × 0.999) ≈ 0.019, about 2%. When the condition is rare, false positives swamp true positives, so a screening regime needs a base-rate-aware second stage. (2) H(p, q) = −Σ p(x) log q(x); with p one-hot at y every term vanishes except −log q(y). The minimum over q is H(p), attained only at q = p, and the gap is KL(p‖q) ≥ 0. Perplexity = e^2.3 ≈ 10: on average the model is as uncertain as if choosing uniformly among ten tokens at each step. (3) Any two of: identical averages hide different error distributions, and the tokens that decide task success are a vanishing fraction of the mean; different tokenizers make per-token losses averages over different event spaces, so the numbers aren't in common units; behavior under a decoding rule depends on rankings, including near-ties, which equal average log mass does not pin down. (4) The probability statement is a fact about the model's output distribution. The confidence claim, that 0.9 should govern how strongly anyone believes the token is right, is an inference about the world. The calibration claim, that across many 0.9-assignments about 90% prove correct, is an empirical property of a set of predictions, measurable and frequently false; W11 is where you measure it.

Automatic differentiation

Read

  • Karpathy, "The spelled-out intro to neural networks and backpropagation" (karpathy.ai/zero-to-hero) — core, ~2.5 hours of video: the scalar Value object through the multilayer perceptron. Code alongside, pausing to predict each derivative before the backward step.
  • Parr & Howard, "The Matrix Calculus You Need for Deep Learning" (arXiv:1802.01528) — core: Sec. 3 partial derivatives, 4.1 the Jacobian, 4.5 the chain rules, 5 gradient of neuron activation, 6 gradient of the neural network loss. Rework the displayed examples closed-book with dimensions beside every factor.
  • PyTorch, "Automatic Differentiation with torch.autograd" (tutorial) — core, a sitting. Inspect .grad at every leaf of a small computation and check the numbers against your own engine.

Do

Build a scalar autograd engine: a Value class with +, *, **, and one nonlinearity, a topological-sort backward(), and gradient accumulation at shared nodes, plus a test file and a half-page mechanism note (three sentences, no jargon lost, no mechanism lost).

Then break it deliberately. Swap gradient accumulation (+=) for plain assignment (=) in your backward pass, rerun a function that reuses a subexpression, and explain which path's contribution vanished.

Done when your engine's gradients match central finite-difference numerical gradients to within 1e-6 on three test functions, at least one composing your nonlinearity and at least one reusing a shared subexpression, the same Value feeding two paths. Know why the bar is 1e-6 and not 1e-12 before you claim a pass.

Skip if you can already differentiate a two-layer scalar computation by hand, label every local derivative, and verify the result numerically (gateway check 7).

Take away

  • Reverse-mode differentiation answers one question in one backward sweep: how does this single scalar loss change with respect to everything upstream. The forward pass stores each node's local derivative; the backward pass multiplies them along every path and adds where paths merge.
  • Cost is a small constant multiple of the forward pass whether the model has ten parameters or a trillion. That is what makes training deep networks feasible at all.
  • Autodiff is not costless symbolic differentiation. It never builds a derivative formula. It computes exact derivatives of the program you actually ran, and it pays in memory: every intermediate activation stays alive until its backward step needs it.
  • That is why training takes several times the memory of inference, why activation checkpointing exists (W22), and why "just fine-tune it on a laptop" usually fails as a memory claim before it fails as a FLOPs claim.
  • This mechanism sits under every training claim downstream: W8's forward and backward propagation, W9's vanishing and exploding gradients, W13's attention derivatives, W15's GPT training loop.
  • Decision: evaluate claims about training mechanism, compute, and reproducibility. Compute-threshold regimes regulate a number someone has to count: EU AI Act Article 51's 10²⁵-FLOP presumption, with the AI Office writing the measurement guidance, and SB 53's 10²⁶-FLOP test.
  • The standard estimate of ~6 FLOPs per parameter per training token exists because the backward pass costs roughly twice the forward pass, a fact about this mechanism rather than a convention.
  • Whoever drafts the counting rules decides whether backward-pass FLOPs, discarded runs, and fine-tuning count toward the threshold, and each choice moves real models across the line.
Check yourself
  1. One Value in your graph feeds two downstream operations. What must its gradient equal after backward(), which rule of calculus says so, and what symptom does = instead of += produce?
  2. Why does reverse mode deliver every parameter's gradient for a small constant multiple of one forward pass, while forward mode would need one pass per parameter, and what resource does reverse mode pay in instead?
  3. For g: ℝⁿ→ℝᵐ, f: ℝᵐ→ℝ, and L = f(g(x)): write the shape of each Jacobian in the vector chain rule and the shape of their product. What object is that product?
  4. Your finite-difference check disagrees with your engine in the fourth decimal place. Which one is exact, why, and what two error sources afflict the other?

Answers. (1) The sum of the gradients arriving along each path, by the multivariable chain rule (total derivative). With =, whichever branch the topological order visits last overwrites the other's contribution, so gradients go silently wrong when and only when subexpressions are shared, the bug the third test function exists to catch. (2) Reverse mode propagates one scalar's sensitivity backward, so each node computes ∂L/∂(itself) once, reusing stored local derivatives, and the number of inputs never enters the cost. Forward mode carries one directional derivative per pass: a million parameters, a million passes. The bill is memory, because every activation a local derivative needs stays live until its backward step. (3) ∂f/∂g is 1×m, ∂g/∂x is m×n, and the product is 1×n, the gradient ∇L as a row vector over the inputs. (4) The engine is exact to floating point, since it multiplies exact local derivatives of the operations actually executed. Finite differences carry truncation error, O(h²) for central differences, plus cancellation when subtracting nearly equal numbers; shrink h too far and roundoff grows. That trade-off is why the acceptance bar is 1e-6.

Numerical computing and PyTorch fluency

Read

  • PyTorch, "Learn the Basics" (tutorial) — complete: tensors, datasets, data loaders, transforms, model construction, autograd, optimization, saving. Rebuild the final example in a fresh file with the tutorial closed.
  • NumPy quickstart (quickstart) — core if you failed gateway check 1: end to end, with extra weight on broadcasting and indexing (start with the absolute beginners guide if it moves too fast). Write the shape you expect before running any array expression.
  • The Python tutorial (docs.python.org) — core if you failed gateway check 1: chapters 3–5, adding 6 and 8 only if imports and tracebacks still read as noise.
  • Python tutorial ch. 12, "Virtual Environments and Packages" (docs) — core if you failed gateway check 2. Keep the commands; they become the pinned-environment block of your notebook template.
  • Software Carpentry, "Version Control with Git" (lesson) — core if you failed gateway check 3: "Setting Up Git" through "Exploring History", plus "Conflicts". It stops short of branching, so close that gap with the pass task.
  • Dive into Deep Learning (d2l.ai) — optional: 2.1–2.5 and the Builders' Guide, 6.1–6.7. Your perturbation grid comes from here.

Do

Run the W0 gateway checks 1–3 cold before opening any resource: the fresh-notebook CSV analysis, the pinned-environment rebuild in a new directory, and the commit–branch–merge–recover cycle. Pass a check and skip its repair resource above; fail one and that resource is core for you this week.

Then work through Learn the Basics, rebuild its final example closed-book (model, dataloader, training loop, save and load), and commit one repo with three pieces:

  1. Tensor challenge: a dozen tasks you write and then beat against the clock (indexing, broadcasting, reshaping, reductions, device moves, dtype casts), each with the expected shape written as a comment before the cell runs. Then the perturbation grid: change device, precision, batch size, and seed, recording which outputs should and should not change, one line each on why.
  2. Notebook template: pinned environment, seeds set in one visible place, sections for data / model / train / eval, and a final cell that asserts the headline result.
  3. Debugging checklist: ordered by what you check first (shape → dtype → device → grad → data pipeline), one line per item on how to check it.

Done when the notebook reruns clean from a fresh kernel, and your checklist, followed literally from top to bottom, catches a shape bug deliberately planted in your challenge by someone else. Have the tutor plant it.

Take away

  • A tensor is a shape, a dtype, and a device, and nearly every mysterious PyTorch bug is a mismatch in one of the three. Fluency is predicting all three attributes of every intermediate result before the cell runs.
  • A seed is not reproducibility. Nine things must hold: code, data version, preprocessing, split, seed, precision, hardware-relevant numerical behavior, dependencies, and configuration. torch.manual_seed(0) pins one of them, and some GPU kernels stay nondeterministic even then.
  • Floating-point addition is not associative, and different hardware reduces in different orders. The right bar for a reproduction is agreement within a stated tolerance; bit-identity across devices is a requirement no vendor can meet.
  • Broadcasting right-aligns shapes and stretches every size-1 dimension to match its partner. Getting the answer without the rule means you memorized an example.
  • OMB's AI acquisition memo (M-25-22) and NIST's evaluation standards work shape what documentation an eval contractor must file, and someone writes what "reproducible" means into the contract line.
  • The nine-item list is what lets an official reject a vendor appendix that says "random seed fixed at 42" and nothing else, and write a requirement vendors can meet instead. W36 turns this into a measurement-science argument.
Check yourself
  1. Tensors of shape (32, 1, 512) and (8, 512) are multiplied elementwise. What is the result's shape, and what rule produced it?
  2. You set torch.manual_seed(0), rerun on the same machine, and the loss curve still differs. Name three legitimate causes from the nine-item list.
  3. The same script produces GPU losses differing from CPU in the sixth decimal place. Bug or expected, and what does that imply about the right acceptance bar for a reproduction?
  4. Training crashes with a shape mismatch on batch 47 but not batch 1; separately, the first layer's .grad is None after backward(). What does each symptom point at?

Answers. (1) (32, 8, 512). Broadcasting right-aligns the shapes, treats (8, 512) as (1, 8, 512), and stretches every size-1 dimension: 1→8 in the middle, 1→32 in front. (2) Any three of: nondeterministic GPU kernels (the atomics use_deterministic_algorithms disables), dataloader shuffling and worker seeding driven by a different generator, a changed dependency version, a precision or dtype difference, a silently updated data file or preprocessing step. The seed pinned the RNG stream, not the computation. (3) Expected, the hardware-relevant numerical behavior item; the bar is agreement within a stated tolerance rather than bit-identity. (4) Batch 47 points at the data pipeline, since the model already survived 46 batches: look for a data-dependent shape (variable-length sample, partial final batch, collate surprise). A None grad means the parameter never entered the graph, from a severed forward path (a .numpy() round-trip, an op under torch.no_grad(), requires_grad=False) or a layer defined in __init__ and never called in forward. An optimizer never handed the parameter also freezes training but leaves .grad populated, so rule it out second.

Statistical learning and generalization

Read

  • Understanding Deep Learning, Chs. 5, 8, 9 (Prince, udlbook.github.io/udlbook) — 5.1 and 5.7 (maximum likelihood, cross-entropy), 8.2–8.5 (sources of error, double descent, hyperparameters), 9.1–9.2 (explicit and implicit regularization). Redraw 8.2's noise/bias/variance figures with every axis labeled, then check.
  • Dive into Deep Learning (d2l.ai) — 3.6 Generalization, 3.7 Weight Decay, 4.6 Generalization in Classification, 4.7 Environment and Distribution Shift, 5.5 Generalization in Deep Learning, 5.6 Dropout. Slow down on 4.7; your build runs on it.
  • Deep Learning, §§5.2–5.5 (Goodfellow, Bengio & Courville, deeplearningbook.org) — optional. Capacity, validation sets, bias and variance, maximum likelihood. List every way information can leak across splits as you read, and keep the list.

Do

Start from D2L 3.7's high-dimensional linear regression. Sweep weight decay over several orders of magnitude and plot training and validation loss per setting. Then rebuild the pipeline with one manufactured leak, letting duplicated examples straddle the split. Predict the validation score's move before you rerun.

Then the generalization-claim audit. Take one benchmark score from a real system card; GPT-4's 86.4% on MMLU is the canonical target. In one page, name the training population, the evaluation population, and the deployment population the score is offered as evidence about. Grade both transitions with the claim ladder, then hunt for contamination, construct mismatch, and selection.

Done when the audit names the train, evaluation, and deployment populations explicitly, and identifies at least one leakage or shift channel the original report does not mention.

Skip if you can already write the noise/bias/variance decomposition from memory, name three distinct leakage channels, and explain in two sentences why a clean held-out score can still fail to support a deployment claim.

Take away

  • Empirical risk on a sample estimates expected risk on the distribution that sample came from, and nothing else. Every generalization claim is indexed to a population, and carrying the score to new users or higher stakes is a separate inference.
  • A held-out test set is not universally representative. "It generalizes, we tested on held-out data" assumes same distribution, no leakage across the split, and no tuning against the test set. None of the three is checkable from the score alone.
  • Test error decomposes into noise (irreducible), bias (what the model class can't represent), and variance (sensitivity to the training sample). Weight decay attacks variance and pays in bias, the right trade when variance dominates.
  • Double descent (UDL 8.4): past the interpolation point, test error falls a second time, driven by the training procedure's implicit regularization (9.2). "More parameters, therefore more overfitting" holds in the classical regime only.
  • A leak's size and its disqualifying force are different properties. Normalization statistics computed before splitting barely move a synthetic benchmark and still belong on the ledger.
  • Medical regulators already demand the population argument. The FDA/Health Canada/MHRA Good Machine Learning Practice principles (October 2021) call for datasets representative of the intended patient population, so results can be "reasonably generalized to the population of interest."
  • EU AI Act Article 55 requires that systemic-risk models be evaluated but says nothing about whom the evaluation population must represent. Frontier models have no GMLP equivalent, and system cards pick their own benchmarks.
Check yourself
  1. Training loss 0.02, validation loss 0.31. Say what each number estimates, and name the two further assumptions under which the validation number transfers to deployment.
  2. Decompose test error into noise, bias, and variance. Which term does weight decay attack, what is the price, and when is the trade worth it?
  3. Name three distinct leakage channels and identify the one your build manufactured. Why couldn't a reader of the final score detect it?
  4. A system card reports 86.4% on MMLU. Name the three populations of your audit and the question each transition raises.

Answers. (1) Training loss is empirical risk on the sample the optimizer fit, biased downward by the fitting itself; validation loss estimates expected risk on the distribution the data came from. Transfer needs two more assumptions: the validation set stayed untouched (no leakage, no repeated tuning against it), and the deployment distribution matches the data distribution. Both are routinely false. (2) Noise is irreducible, bias is systematic error from the model class, variance is sensitivity to the training sample. Weight decay attacks variance at the price of added bias, which is worth it when variance dominates: flexible model, limited data. (3) Any three of: preprocessing statistics computed on the full dataset before splitting; duplicates or near-duplicates straddling the split; repeated hyperparameter selection against the test set; temporal leakage; public benchmark items sitting in a web-scale training corpus. The build manufactured the second. The final score is one number carrying no record of pipeline order, and the leak lives in the process. (4) Training population: the partly undisclosed web-scale corpus. Evaluation population: MMLU's multiple-choice items, public since 2020. Deployment population: the queries of actual users. The first transition raises contamination (were the public items in the corpus?); the second raises shift and construct validity (does multiple-choice exam recall measure what deployment demands?). The 86.4% measures the middle population; everything else is inference.

Multilayer networks and learned representations

Read

  • Karpathy, makemore Parts 2–3 (karpathy.ai/zero-to-hero) — Part 2 "MLP" and Part 3 "Activations & Gradients, BatchNorm", paired with the build. Pause before every cell and predict the tensor shape.
  • Dive into Deep Learning, Ch. 5, Multilayer Perceptrons (d2l.ai) — 5.1 through 5.4: activation functions, implementation, forward and backward propagation, numerical stability and initialization. Derive the one-hidden-layer forward and backward pass with every shape attached before checking against 5.3.
  • Understanding Deep Learning, Chs. 3–4 (Prince, udlbook.github.io/udlbook) — optional. Ch. 3 Shallow neural networks (3.2, universal approximation) and Ch. 4 Deep neural networks (4.5, shallow vs. deep). Reproduce one hidden-unit figure and write down which interpretations it does not justify.

Do

Code along with makemore Part 2 and build the Bengio-style character MLP: embedding table, hidden tanh layer, softmax head, training loop. Then train with embedding dimension 8 instead of 2, project the embedding table to 2D using your W3 move (top two singular directions), and plot the characters. Retrain with a different seed, re-plot, keep both plots.

Assemble one notebook: the MLP with the validation loss you report, the two seeds' embedding plots side by side, one hidden-layer activation histogram, and an interpretation warning label. The label is one paragraph on what the plot shows and what it does not: what the projection preserves, what the seed comparison established, and which claims about "meaning" need causal evidence you lack.

Done when the label carries the asymmetry of W2's answer 3 correctly: separation in the plot is trustworthy, because an orthogonal projection never increases distances, while closeness may be an artifact. Check it against that answer line by line, and cite no cluster as proof of shared meaning.

Skip if you can already derive the backward pass for a one-hidden-layer tanh network unaided and say what a saturated unit does to its gradient and why.

Take away

  • A learned representation is what optimization left behind. You chose the architecture, the data, and the loss; gradient descent chose everything else, under no obligation to make it legible. "The model represents X" is a claim about a trained artifact, never a design fact.
  • No unit owes you a stable human meaning. A hidden unit that fires on X in your probe set is correlated with X there. Units are routinely polysemantic, apparent meaning shifts across seeds and checkpoints, and a 2D embedding plot carries every caveat W2 attached to projections.
  • Two stacked linear layers collapse: W₂(W₁x) = (W₂W₁)x, so depth without a nonlinearity buys nothing. The universal approximation theorem (UDL 3.2) is a statement about the nonlinear network.
  • The tanh gradient is 1 − a², which is ≈ 0 at ±1. Saturated units stop learning while training carries on around them, and the usual culprit is initialization scale putting pre-activations too large at step zero (D2L 5.4).
  • The seed comparison separates the two kinds of structure: what survives a retrain belongs to the data, what vanishes belonged to the run.
  • Anthropic's 2024 "Scaling Monosemanticity" work reported extracting millions of features from Claude 3 Sonnet, including features tracking deception- and safety-relevant concepts. Interpretability of this kind is regularly offered as a future basis for auditing.
  • Two questions survive scaling: is the evidence correlational (a probe set) or causal (ablate or steer the feature and watch behavior change), and is the feature stable across training runs? W39 turns these into a grading rubric.
Check yourself
  1. For y = W₂ tanh(W₁x + b₁) + b₂ with input dimension d, hidden width h, output dimension k: write every shape in the forward pass, then derive ∂L/∂W₁. Which W2 and W5 facts does the derivation reuse?
  2. Part 3 shows a histogram of tanh activations piled up at ±1. What does that do to learning in those units, mechanically, and what upstream choice usually caused it?
  3. Your projected embedding plot puts two characters close together. List what that observation can establish, what it cannot, and the test that separates structure the data produced from structure the run produced.
  4. A lab tells a regulator that unit 402 of its model "represents deception." Give two reasons the claim as stated is under-supported, and one experiment that would strengthen it.

Answers. (1) W₁ is h×d, b₁ ∈ Rʰ, z = W₁x + b₁ ∈ Rʰ, a = tanh(z) ∈ Rʰ, W₂ is k×h, y ∈ Rᵏ. Backward: ∂L/∂a = W₂ᵀ(∂L/∂y); ∂L/∂z = (1 − a²) ⊙ ∂L/∂a; ∂L/∂W₁ = (∂L/∂z)xᵀ. It reuses W5's chain rule through the computation graph and W2's shape discipline, since every factor's dimensions are checkable before you compute anything. (2) Gradients through saturated units vanish (1 − a² ≈ 0 at ±1), so those units stop learning while the rest of training continues. Initialization scale is the usual cause, with pre-activations too large at the start (D2L 5.4); Part 3's histograms are the diagnostic. (3) It establishes that the two are near in the projected space, and, if the pairing survives the seed comparison, that the data pushes them together (in makemore, characters that substitute in similar contexts). It cannot establish closeness in the full 8-dimensional space, since the projection may have merged them, nor that the network treats them alike in any causal sense. The test is retraining with a new seed and re-plotting. (4) The evidence is correlational, since firing on a human-chosen probe set does not show the unit influences behavior; and polysemanticity plus instability mean the unit may fire for several unrelated things and may not survive retraining, as the seed test showed in miniature. To strengthen it, intervene: ablate or activate the unit and measure behavior change on held-out cases. Standards for that evidence are W39's business.

Optimization and trainability

Read

  • Dive into Deep Learning, the trainability sections (Zhang, Lipton, Li & Smola, d2l.ai) — Ch. 12: 12.1, 12.3–12.6, 12.10 Adam, 12.11 Learning Rate Scheduling; plus 5.4 Numerical Stability and Initialization, 8.5 Batch Normalization, 8.6 ResNet. Write the SGD, momentum, and Adam updates side by side from memory, then check against 12.4, 12.6, and 12.10.
  • Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition (He, Zhang, Ren & Sun, 2015, arXiv:1512.03385) — §1 with Figure 1 (the degradation problem), §3.1–3.3, §4.1 with Figure 4 and Table 1. Shut the paper and trace one residual block forward and backward with explicit shapes, through the shortcut too, then check against §3.2.
  • Understanding Deep Learning (Prince, udlbook.github.io) — optional. 6.1–6.5 fitting models, 7.4–7.5 backpropagation and initialization, 11.3–11.6 residual networks and batch normalization. Read for failure mechanisms rather than recipes.

Do

Take one small net (an MLP or small CNN on Fashion-MNIST is plenty) and train it four ways: plain SGD, SGD with momentum, Adam, and Adam with a decay schedule. Same data, same batch size, same epoch budget, only the optimizer changes. Plot the four loss curves on one set of axes. Then set the learning rate 10× too high and write down the curve you expect before you run it.

Then write a controlled ablation report, one page plus curves. Pick one intervention (batch normalization, a residual connection, or a learning-rate schedule) and train the same net with and without it, every other hyperparameter frozen, at least three seeds per arm. Report per-arm mean and spread of the final metric, overlay the curves, and give a verdict.

Done when the report states whether the intervention's effect exceeds seed-to-seed noise, with the numbers shown, so a reader can check the verdict against your table without rerunning anything. Keep the code; W10 points the same harness at statistics.

Skip if you can already write the Adam update from memory, explain ResNet Figure 1 without notes, and state what a residual connection changes about the gradient path.

Take away

  • A deep network is trainable when gradients arrive at every layer with usable scale. Momentum and Adam reshape the step, schedules resize it over time, initialization sets the scales at step zero, normalization holds them during training, and residual connections give the gradient a path it cannot lose.
  • None of that changes what the network could express, only whether optimization can find it.
  • Residual connections don't help by making the model bigger. An identity shortcut adds no parameters (ResNet §3.2 says so explicitly), and Figure 1's 56-layer plain net posts higher training error than the 20-layer one. Capacity was never the bottleneck.
  • With y = F(x) + x, every block gets a short route to the loss, and "learn a change to your input" (including no change) becomes the easy default.
  • Backpropagation multiplies roughly one Jacobian per layer, so a per-layer scale factor s compounds as s⁵⁰ over 50 layers: slightly below 1 vanishes, slightly above 1 explodes. Initialization sets s near 1 at step zero; batch normalization re-standardizes activations throughout training so drift can't compound.
  • Momentum keeps an exponential moving average of past gradients and cancels SGD's ravine oscillation. Adam keeps two averages, of the gradient and of its square, giving each parameter its own step scale.
  • EU AI Act Article 53(1)(a) and Annex XI require general-purpose model providers to document "training methodologies and techniques, the key design choices including the rationale and assumptions made".
  • "We adopted X for improved training stability" asserts an ablation result. Annex XI's rationale-and-assumptions line is the hook that lets a reviewer ask how many seeds and what the spread was.
Check yourself
  1. A fixed learning rate can be simultaneously too small and too large over one training run. How, and what does a schedule change?
  2. In a 50-layer plain net, why does the initialization scale decide whether gradients vanish or explode, and what does batch normalization change about that picture?
  3. ResNet Figure 1 shows a 56-layer plain net with higher training error than a 20-layer one. Why does that rule out overfitting, and how do residual connections fix the real problem?
  4. Your single-seed ablation shows batch normalization adding 1.4 points of accuracy. What must the report show before "batchnorm helps this net" is a supported claim?

Answers. (1) Early in training, large steps make fast progress, so a small fixed rate wastes the budget; late in training, minibatch noise means large steps bounce around the minimum instead of settling into it. A decaying schedule serves both regimes in sequence, which no constant rate can do. (2) Backpropagation multiplies roughly 50 layer-Jacobians, so a per-layer scale factor s compounds as s⁵⁰, vanishing below 1 and exploding above it; initialization sets s, and variance-preserving initialization puts it near 1 at step zero. Batch normalization re-standardizes activations at every layer throughout training, so scale drift can't compound and training is far less sensitive to the initial choice. (3) Overfitting predicts low training error with high test error; here training error itself is worse, so the deeper net can't fit the data it has, with capacity to spare. With y = F(x) + x the shortcut passes gradients straight through (the identity term's derivative is the identity), so every block has a short path to the loss, and doing nothing means driving F toward zero rather than learning an identity map through stacked nonlinear layers. (4) Both arms rerun over multiple seeds, per-arm mean and spread reported, and the between-arm gap shown against the seed-to-seed spread. If 1.4 points sits inside seed noise, the claim isn't supported.

Experimental craft

Read

  • Evan Miller, "Adding Error Bars to Evals" (Anthropic, 2024, arXiv:2411.00640) — §2 analysis framework, §3 variance reduction, §4 comparing models (unpaired vs. paired), §5 power analysis. Write the standard error of an eval score and of a paired difference from memory, then check §2.1 and §4.2.
  • D2L §22.10, Statistics (d2l.ai) — 22.10.1 evaluating estimators, 22.10.2 hypothesis testing, 22.10.3 confidence intervals, with runnable code for all three. Rerun the CI code at a tenth of the sample size and watch what moves and what doesn't.
  • Full Stack Deep Learning 2022, Lectures 2–3 (fullstackdeeplearning.com/course/2022) — optional. Development Infrastructure & Tooling, then Troubleshooting & Testing. The tooling walkthroughs have aged; watch for the habits, not the tools.

Do

Point your W9 ablation harness at statistics. Run one configuration across several seeds and compute the mean, the CLT standard error, and a 95% CI. Then implement Miller's paired comparison between two configurations: a CI on the per-seed differences, not two CIs eyeballed for overlap. Finish with a power calculation: how many seeds to detect a 1-point effect at 80% power?

Then re-run the W9 ablation as a preregistered mini-study. Write down before anything runs: the hypothesis, with a direction and the minimum effect size you'd care about; the number of seeds, chosen by a power argument you show; and the replication criterion. Run it, report the effect with its CI, and close with a one-paragraph limitations section.

Done when a reader who is not you can rerun the study from the report alone (code reference, seeds, environment, exact metric) and decide from the replication criterion whether they replicated, without asking you. Commit the hypothesis-and-criterion section before the first run and cite that commit in the report.

Skip if you can already produce the correct CI given a score, an n, and a clustering structure, and say when a paired design beats an unpaired one.

Take away

  • An eval score is the sample mean of a random variable: questions drawn from an unseen super-population, answers drawn from the model's sampling distribution. That framing buys CLT standard errors, paired comparisons, and power analysis.
  • A two-point gap can be noise or decisive, and the number alone can't tell you which. Unpaired, two models' error bars on a 500-question eval usually swallow two points whole. Paired on identical questions, Var(A−B) = Var(A) + Var(B) − 2Cov(A,B), and the shared variance cancels.
  • Clustered items (five questions per document, shared templates) make the effective sample size far smaller than the nominal one, so the naive standard error is too small and you end up overconfident. Miller's examples show inflation of more than 3×.
  • Lowering the sampling temperature shrinks variance by measuring a different model (§3.3). Precision bought that way isn't precision about the system you deploy.
  • NIST's Center for AI Standards and Innovation has pre-deployment testing agreements with five frontier labs and more than 40 completed assessments, and in March 2026 signed an MOU with GSA to bring evaluation science into federal AI procurement.
  • The UK AI Security Institute tests leading systems before and after public release, on API access to particular model checkpoints that it notes may carry different safeguards from the public version (Frontier AI Trends Report).
  • When such a score gates a deployment or a purchase, write four things into the funding terms: a standard error on every headline number, paired designs for any model-vs-model claim, seeds and sampling parameters disclosed, and a power analysis showing the eval could have detected the effect.
Check yourself
  1. A model scores 78% on 500 independent questions. Compute the 95% confidence interval. Now suppose those 500 are 100 documents × 5 questions each: what breaks, and in which direction does it bias you?
  2. Why can a paired comparison detect a difference that the two models' separate CIs can't? Point at the formula.
  3. W9 gave you a seed-to-seed SD of about 0.8 points, and you care about 1-point effects. Roughly how many seeds per configuration for 80% power at α = 0.05?
  4. A colleague's ablation changed the optimizer, the schedule, and the data order at once, ran one seed, and credits the optimizer for a 3-point gain. Name both distinct failures.

Answers. (1) SE = √(0.78 × 0.22 / 500) ≈ 1.85 points, so 78% ± 3.6, on the assumption that questions are independent draws from the super-population. With 100 documents × 5 questions, the within-document questions are correlated, the effective sample size is well under 500, and the naive SE comes out too small, leaving you overconfident. Clustered standard errors (Miller §2.2, Appendix A) are the fix, and his examples show inflation of more than 3×. (2) Var(A−B) = Var(A) + Var(B) − 2Cov(A,B). On identical questions the outcomes are positively correlated, so the covariance term shrinks the variance of the difference, sometimes far below what the separate error bars suggest; overlapping CIs therefore do not imply no difference. (3) Two-sample sketch: n ≈ 2(z_{α/2} + z_β)² σ²/δ² = 2 × (1.96 + 0.84)² × 0.64 / 1 ≈ 10 seeds per arm. A paired design needs fewer, which is the practical argument for pairing. (4) Confounding, since three factors changed and the gain can't be attributed to one; and no uncertainty, since one seed leaves 3 points possibly inside seed-to-seed noise. No CI, no claim.

Calibration, shift, and causal claims

Read

  • On Calibration of Modern Neural Networks (Guo et al., ICML 2017, arXiv:1706.04599) — §2 Definitions (reliability diagrams, ECE), §3 Observing Miscalibration, §4 temperature scaling; skip the method-comparison tables. Draw an overconfident model's reliability diagram and write the ECE formula from memory, then check against §2.
  • WILDS: A Benchmark of in-the-Wild Distribution Shifts (Koh et al., 2021, arXiv:2012.07421) — the two shift settings, plus Camelyon17, iWildCam, and CivilComments. Not the leaderboard, not the baseline tables. Keep a ledger per dataset: the shift, the deployment population, and what an i.i.d. test number would have overstated.
  • Causal Inference: What If, Ch. 1, A Definition of Causal Effect (Hernán & Robins, free PDF at miguelhernan.org/whatifbook) — individual and average causal effects, and the closing contrast between causation and association. Write E[Y | A=1] and E[Y^{a=1}] from memory, plus one sentence on why they differ in general.
  • Holistic Evaluation of Language Models (Liang et al., TMLR 2023, arXiv:2211.09110) — optional, and read the paper rather than the live leaderboard. The multi-metric argument and the targeted evaluations. Pick one scenario and state which of this week's three failure modes its metrics would and would not detect.

Do

One notebook, one classifier, two failures made visible. Plot a small image classifier's reliability diagram and compute ECE, then fit temperature scaling on a held-out split and re-plot. Evaluate the same model on a shifted test set and plot accuracy and ECE, clean versus shifted. Predict first whether the clean-fitted temperature survives the shift. D2L 4.7 is the worked reference.

Then redesign one published evaluation so its claim survives a named distribution shift. Pick one attached to a real claim: a system-card number, a medical-AI accuracy figure, a policy-document leaderboard result. One page: the original claim and its population; the deployment population and the shift in WILDS' terms; your redesign (test-split construction, per-subgroup reporting, calibration); what the original number overstated.

Done when the redesign names the shift, names the new population, and states in one sentence what the original number would have overstated. If you can't fill in the overstatement, you picked an evaluation that was already honest; pick again.

Skip if you can already sketch a reliability diagram for an overconfident model, name WILDS' two shift settings with a dataset each, and explain in two sentences why a calibrated model can still be wrong on every case that matters.

Phase II closes here. The gate asks you to defend a calibration plot, a shifted evaluation, and one correctly labeled causal claim; it is on the gates page.

Take away

  • Calibration is a frequency promise on a distribution: of all the times the model says 80%, about 80% are right. It is a property of the score on a population, never of a single answer.
  • Temperature scaling is one scalar dividing the logits, fitted by NLL on a held-out split. It can buy calibration after the fact while leaving softmax ordering, accuracy, and ranking untouched, which tells you how little calibration certifies.
  • A calibrated model can still be wrong on every case that matters. Calibration aligns stated confidence with observed frequency where it was measured. It guarantees nothing per case, and it does not survive shift; recalibration is owed after any change of population.
  • WILDS' two settings decide what the headline number should be. Domain generalization (Camelyon17's sixth hospital) calls for performance on held-out domains; subpopulation shift calls for worst-group performance, because the average is the number that hides it.
  • E[Y | A=1] compares disjoint subsets of the population, and E[Y^{a=1}] compares the same population under two counterfactual treatments, so the first tracks who got treated rather than what treatment does. They coincide under exchangeability.
  • EU AI Act Article 15 requires high-risk systems to achieve "an appropriate level of accuracy" with the metrics declared in the instructions for use, and to perform consistently "throughout their lifecycle".
  • Operationalizing that means naming accuracy on which population, calibrated by which measure, re-verified under which shifts. Camelyon17's hospital-transfer failure is what "consistent throughout the lifecycle" has to rule out.
Check yourself
  1. Your model's reliability diagram sags below the diagonal everywhere. Name the one-parameter fix, the data you fit it on, and two things it cannot repair.
  2. A tumor classifier trained on five hospitals deploys to a sixth; a toxicity filter matches training proportions overall but fails on comments mentioning specific demographic groups. Which WILDS setting is each, and what should the headline number be?
  3. A vendor says: "our model is well calibrated, so its confidence scores can be trusted for triage." Give two distinct failures this sentence leaves open.
  4. "Hospitals using our system had 12% fewer adverse events." Which claim-ladder rung is that, and what design would move it up one?

Answers. (1) Temperature scaling: a single scalar dividing the logits, fitted by NLL on a held-out validation split, never the test set. It cannot repair which cases are wrong, since softmax ordering is unchanged and accuracy and ranking stay fixed, and it does not hold under shift; your build's perturbation showed the clean-fitted temperature failing on the shifted set. (2) The sixth hospital is domain generalization, so report performance on held-out domains. The demographic failure is subpopulation shift, so report worst-group performance, because the average hides it. (3) Per-case: a calibrated 90% is wrong one time in ten, and calibration doesn't say which time. Population: calibration was measured on some distribution and the triage population is a shift away, and calibration is among the first properties shift breaks. (Construct validity, confidence about the wrong quantity, also accepted.) (4) A measurement of an association, being ridden toward a causal recommendation. Moving up requires an intervention-grade design, a randomized rollout across hospitals or a credible quasi-experiment, plus an explicit exchangeability argument; Ch. 1's notation is what lets you state what's missing.

Tokenization and the language modeling objective

Read

  • Karpathy, "Let's build the GPT Tokenizer" (karpathy.ai/zero-to-hero) — at the keyboard: BPE from scratch, training a tokenizer, the GPT-2 and GPT-4 vocabularies, and the closing tour of model failures traceable to tokenization. Afterward implement the merge loop from memory, then check it against the video's code.
  • Stanford CS336, Lecture 1 "Overview, tokenization" + the BPE-tokenizer portion of Assignment 1 (Basics) (cs336.stanford.edu) — the lecture, then the assignment portion alongside the build. Change the vocabulary size in your Assignment 1 tokenizer and predict what happens to tokens-per-word before you rerun it.
  • Petrov et al., "Language Model Tokenizers Introduce Unfairness Between Languages" (arXiv:2305.15425) — the premium measurements: token counts for the same content across languages, and the consequences for cost, latency, and context window. Write a six-line evidence brief separating cost effects from performance claims.
  • Dive into Deep Learning (d2l.ai) — optional. 9.2 Converting Raw Text into Sequence Data, 9.3 Language Models (perplexity), 15.1 Word Embedding (word2vec), 4.1 Softmax Regression. Run its tokenization code on two languages and one technical corpus and keep the outputs.

Do

Code along with Karpathy's first makemore video: the count-based bigram, then the neural one (a single linear layer into softmax), and watch them land on the same loss. Before the first training step, predict the loss of uniform guessing (−log(1/27) for a 27-character vocabulary) and check how close the untrained network starts.

Then implement the BPE merge loop and train a small tokenizer on a corpus of your choosing. Retrain it at the same vocabulary size on a different-language corpus, predict which token counts move and in which direction, then measure.

Put the results in one notebook. Take the same short text (a parallel passage, not separate texts) in at least four languages spanning two scripts, run it through two tokenizers (a production GPT tokenizer via tiktoken, and your own), and tabulate tokens-per-sentence with each language's premium relative to English. Alongside it: both bigram models with their final losses, and three samples from each.

Done when the table shows tokens-per-sentence for the same content in 4+ languages, and directly beneath it one sentence states the API-cost implication using your own numbers ("at per-token prices, this request in ⟨language⟩ costs N× the English price"), where a reader can recompute N from the table alone.

Skip if you can already derive the autoregressive objective with every shape attached and say in two sentences why identical content is not identical cost across languages.

Take away

  • A language model is one probability distribution over token sequences, factored by the chain rule: p(x₁…x_T) = ∏ p(x_t | x_<t). Training minimizes the average negative log probability of the next token, which is W4's cross-entropy. Perplexity is that loss exponentiated.
  • A token is not a word, and it is not neutral. BPE is compression fit to a training corpus: strings the corpus saw often become single tokens, everything else gets shredded toward bytes.
  • Petrov et al. measure up to a 15× spread in tokenization length for the same translated content, and 4×+ gaps survive even byte-level encoding. APIs bill, rate-limit, and window by the token, so "per-token" pricing is per-language pricing.
  • The embedding matrix is V×d, one row per token; the output matrix is d×V, one column per token; softmax turns its logits into the distribution the loss scores. Sometimes the two are tied into one shared matrix, never interchangeable in role.
  • The same multiplier shrinks the effective context window. "128K context" is not one product across languages.
  • Any administration buying per-token API access for citizen-facing services (the EU institutions, operating in 24 official languages, are the sharpest case) is signing a contract with an undisclosed per-language price multiplier.
  • The tractable ask is disclosure, not redesign: publish tokens-per-character on a standard parallel corpus for every language in scope. The procurement framing is an application, not a documented case.
Check yourself
  1. Write the autoregressive factorization of p(x₁…x_T) and the training loss for one sequence. Which W4 quantity is the per-token loss, and what does perplexity mean in its terms?
  2. Your count-based bigram and your SGD-trained neural bigram converge to the same loss. What is the neural bigram, and why must the two agree?
  3. A BPE tokenizer trained mostly on English shreds Amharic into near-byte fragments. Name the mechanism, and the three consequences Petrov et al. draw at fixed per-token pricing and fixed context length.
  4. "The model handles spelling questions badly because it can't see letters." What is right in that sentence, and what does it get wrong about where the limitation lives?

Answers. (1) p(x₁…x_T) = ∏_t p(x_t | x_<t), an exact identity rather than an approximation; the loss is the average of −log p(x_t | x_<t), the cross-entropy between the one-hot next token and the predicted distribution, and perplexity is exp of that average, the model's uncertainty as an effective number of equally likely choices. (2) A softmax regression from a one-hot of the current token: one V×V weight matrix whose row i holds the logits for what follows token i. Counting computes the conditional MLE directly, gradient descent on cross-entropy converges to the same MLE, so they meet at the same loss; "neural" buys nothing until contexts get too numerous to count, which is what W8's MLP was for. (3) BPE merges are earned by corpus frequency, so scripts the corpus underrepresents never earn merges and their text stays near byte level, multiplying the token count. Consequences: higher API cost for identical content, higher latency (more sequential tokens to process and generate), and a smaller effective context window. (4) Right: the model receives token IDs and then embedding rows, never characters, so a spelling question asks about the internal structure of atoms it cannot decompose. Wrong: locating the limit in the model. It lives in the tokenizer, a replaceable pipeline choice, and character- or byte-level tokenization would expose the letters at the price of far longer sequences.

Attention

Read

  • Attention Is All You Need (arXiv:1706.03762) — §3.2, masks, positional encoding, Table 1. Recompute every dimension for d_model=512, h=8 before you look at theirs.
  • 3Blue1Brown, "Attention in transformers" (lesson) — 30 min. Watch it first if the equations look dead. Then find your Q·K product inside Bycroft's running model.
  • Karpathy, "Let's build GPT" (video) — optional, self-attention section only. Predict each result shape before it runs.

Do

Implement scaled dot-product attention from scratch, no nn.MultiheadAttention. Single head, then batched, then causal mask, then multi-head. Drop the √d scaling and watch the softmax saturate.

Write the shape trace: 4 sequences × 128 tokens through embedding → Q/K/V → scores → mask → softmax → values → output projection.

Done when your implementation matches torch.nn.functional.scaled_dot_product_attention on random inputs, causal mask included.

Take away

  • Attention is content-based routing. Each position asks a question (query), every position advertises what it has (key), and the answer is a weighted average of what they carry (value). The weights are a softmax over dot products.
  • √d keeps the dot products' variance near 1. Without it, large d pushes the softmax toward one-hot and the gradients toward zero.
  • The mask sets future scores to −∞ before the softmax, so normalization only ever sees the past.
  • K and V cache across generated tokens; Q doesn't. That makes long context a memory problem, which is what drives serving cost in W23.
  • An attention map is not a chain of thought. It shows one routing operation, in one head, in one layer, picked for display by a human. Ablate the head and the output may not move. This is the most common technical error in policy writing about transformers, and the reason attention pictures are weak evidence in an EU AI Act Article 53 filing. W39 covers what counts instead.
Check yourself
  1. In Q·Kᵀ/√d, what are the dimensions of each factor for one head with sequence length n and head dimension d?
  2. Why mask before the softmax rather than zeroing weights after?
  3. A colleague says "the model attended to the liability clause, so it considered it." Give two distinct reasons that inference fails.
  4. Which of Q, K, V must be recomputed per generated token, and what does that imply about long-context cost?

Answers. (1) Q is n×d, Kᵀ is d×n, product n×n: every position scored against every position. (2) Zeroing after would leak probability mass to the future before renormalization; −∞ before gives exact zeros and correct normalization. (3) Attention weight is correlation with a routing step, not causal influence on the output — ablating the head may change nothing; and there are dozens of heads across dozens of layers, so a human chose which one you saw. (4) Only the new token's Q; past K and V are cached. Per-token cost grows with context through the cache.

The transformer block

Read

  • CS336 Lecture 3, "Architectures, hyperparameters" (cs336.stanford.edu) — norm placement (pre vs post), LayerNorm vs RMSNorm, gated feedforwards, position information, and the d_ff/d_model and head-count ratios. Rebuild the block diagram from memory with norm placement marked, then check the slides.
