  • Karpathy, "Let's build GPT" (karpathy.ai/zero-to-hero) — the back half of the video: multi-head assembly, the feedforward layer, residual connections, layernorm, scaling up. Predict how the parameter count moves before you add each component, then print it.
  • The Llama 3 Herd of Models (Meta, 2024, arXiv:2407.21783) — optional. §3.2 Model Architecture and Table 3. Keep a ledger entry per stated deviation from the vanilla dense transformer, with the reason the paper gives.

Do

Assemble the block around your W13 attention: LayerNorm → causal multi-head attention → residual add, then LayerNorm → two-layer GELU MLP (d → 4d → d) → residual add. Stack 12, put token and position embeddings in front, a final LayerNorm and a tied unembedding behind. That is GPT-2 small's architecture. Delete the residual adds in every block, predict what happens to gradient norms at block 1 before you run it, then restore them and check.

Draw the result on one page: the block plus embeddings and unembedding, every tensor labeled with its shape, every parameter matrix drawn where it acts and counted, and a closed-form parameter formula in V, n_ctx, d, and L whose terms sum to the model total. Mark which single operation crosses positions.

Done when your formula reproduces GPT-2 124M within 1%, embeddings included, and the page states whether the unembedding is tied to the token embedding. (Tied, it comes to 124,439,808; if you're near 163M you counted the embedding twice.)

Skip if you can already draw the block cold with every tensor shape attached and produce that formula.

Take away

  • A decoder block does four things: causal attention moves information between positions, an MLP transforms each position in place, residual connections add both results to a stream running unbroken from embedding to logits, and normalization keeps each sub-block's inputs at a usable scale. Layers write increments to that stream rather than replacing the representation.
  • Attention is not most of the model. In GPT-2 each block's MLP carries roughly twice the parameters of its attention (~4.72M against ~2.36M). Attention is the only operation that moves information between positions; everything else is per-position computation.
  • Pre-norm won because post-norm puts the normalization on the residual path, so every gradient passes through every norm and deep stacks needed warmup tricks. Pre-norm leaves an identity path from the loss to any block. Llama 3 keeps the placement with RMSNorm.
  • Untying the unembedding adds a second V×d matrix, 50257×768 ≈ 38.6M, pushing GPT-2 small near 163M. A disclosure that omits tied-or-not is ambiguous by roughly 30% at this scale.
  • Policy relevance: the EU AI Act's Annex XI, Section 1, item 1(d) requires general-purpose AI model providers to document "the architecture and number of parameters," kept as technical documentation under Article 53(1)(a) for the AI Office and national authorities on request.
  • Block type, dimensions, and count let an evaluator reconstruct memory footprint, FLOPs per token, and serving cost, and catch a disclosed architecture that doesn't sum to the disclosed parameter count. The same line buys nothing about training data, posttraining, or behavior; it supports compute accounting (W21), not safety evaluation.
Check yourself
  1. Trace a sequence of n token vectors through one pre-norm block of GPT-2 small (d=768, 12 heads): list the operations in order and give the tensor shape after each.
  2. Which operation is the only one that mixes information across positions, and what does that imply about what the MLP can and cannot do?
  3. Where do most of a block's parameters live? Give both per-block counts from your formula.
  4. Why does tied-or-not matter when someone discloses "number of parameters"?

Answers. (1) x is n×768 → LayerNorm → QKV projection (n×2304, split into Q, K, V of n×768 each, then into 12 heads of n×64) → causal attention per head (n×64) → concat (n×768) → output projection → residual add → LayerNorm → MLP up-projection (n×3072) → GELU → down-projection (n×768) → residual add. In and out are the same shape, which is what lets you stack it. (2) Attention only; the MLP applies the same weights to every position independently, so it can transform what a position holds but cannot move anything between positions. (3) The MLP: ~4.72M per block (768×3072 and 3072×768 plus biases) against ~2.36M for attention (QKV projection 768×2304, output projection 768×768, plus biases). (4) The gap between 124.4M tied and ~163M untied is about 30%, so a parameter count without that flag can't be checked against an architecture disclosure.

Build and train a compact GPT

Read

  • Karpathy, "Let's build GPT: from scratch, in code, spelled out" (karpathy.ai/zero-to-hero) — the full lecture, spread across the week: bigram baseline, self-attention, the block, scaling up to the char-level model. The next day, rewrite the model class from memory and diff it against your build-along copy.
  • nanoGPT (Karpathy, github.com/karpathy/nanoGPT) — model.py, train.py, and config/train_shakespeare_char.py. Write an evidence brief on the README's reported best validation loss: method, population, what it can't establish.
  • Stanford CS336 (cs336.stanford.edu) — optional, the deeper path; it replaces the build rather than adding to it. Assignment 1 "Basics" only: BPE tokenizer, Transformer LM, training loop, minimal LM. Use the provided tests to locate your errors instead of copying reference code.

Do

Build alongside the video with an editor open: pause, type, predict shapes before he prints them. Then read nanoGPT, tracing one batch from token input to logits through model.py before executing anything. Then train: prepare.py on shakespeare_char, train.py with config/train_shakespeare_char.py, sample.py, logging train and validation loss at checkpoints (CPU: --device=cpu --compile=False; Apple Silicon: --device=mps). Rerun on 1% of the data, predicting train loss, val loss, and sample quality first, and keep the diverging curves.

Ship three things that make one claim. The working model: code, config, seed, environment. The training record: train and validation loss curves with checkpoints marked, exact config, and compute used, reproducible under W10's standard. A one-page model card: data description, training procedure, held-out loss against a stated naive baseline, samples plus the memorization check, compute, limitations.

Done when best validation loss on the char-level Shakespeare config is at or below 1.4697, the nanoGPT README's reported figure. A run on different hardware within a few hundredths also passes, and on CPU the README's reduced config "gets us a loss of only 1.88", which passes too. Either way the model card states which run you did and why your numbers differ from the README's.

Skip if you can show a compact GPT you have already trained with a training record and model card that clear that test.

Take away

  • "The model works" is a measurement claim with an anatomy: what the data was, how it was split, held-out loss against a stated baseline, samples, a memorization check, compute used, and what the bundle cannot establish. That anatomy is a model card.
  • Plausible samples are not evidence. A model that memorized its 1 MB corpus produces gorgeous Shakespeare; so does a model that generalized. Only held-out loss and a search for generated strings inside the training data tell them apart.
  • The baseline is arithmetic, not vibes: cross-entropy against the uniform distribution over the 65-character vocabulary is −ln(1/65) = ln 65 ≈ 4.17 nats per character. A unigram character-frequency baseline is the next-toughest comparison.
  • Some train-validation gap is routine. It becomes a finding when val loss stalls or rises while train loss keeps falling, which on a 1 MB corpus is a model memorizing outright.
  • A compact-model demo occupies the measurement rung of the claim ladder, and better samples don't move it. A fitted trend across model sizes does (W20).
  • Policy relevance: EU AI Act Article 53 with Annex XI obliges GPAI providers to document training data, training process, and compute (Section 1, points 2(b)–(d)). The reader of that filing is looking at this week's model card at ten orders of magnitude more compute. Ask for the curve across scales and treat its absence as the answer.
Check yourself
  1. The acceptance number is 1.4697. Loss against what baseline, and what would a model that knows nothing about Shakespeare score on the 65-character vocabulary?
  2. Your train loss sits well below your validation loss. When is that gap routine, and what turns it into a finding on a 1 MB corpus?
  3. Your samples read as convincing Shakespeare. Name the two checks that must accompany them before the model card may say "works," and what each rules out.
  4. A startup demos a compact model and claims the architecture "scales straight to frontier performance." Which claim-ladder rung is the demo on, and what evidence moves it up one?

Answers. (1) Cross-entropy against the uniform distribution: −ln(1/65) = ln 65 ≈ 4.17 nats per character, so 1.47 means the model concentrates probability far beyond chance; a unigram character-frequency baseline is the tougher next comparison. (2) Routine because the model is fit to the training set; it becomes a finding when val loss stalls or rises while train loss keeps falling, since a corpus this small can be memorized outright, which is what the 1%-data rerun shows you. (3) Held-out validation loss against a stated baseline, which rules out memorization dressed up as competence, and a memorization check searching generated strings in the training corpus, which rules out verbatim regurgitation that a good loss alone won't flag; samples constrain neither. (4) Measurement: one model, one scale, one number. Moving to forecast requires a fitted trend across multiple scales (W20's scaling-law evidence), and without it the sentence is a forecast riding on a measurement.

In-context learning and emergence

Read

  • Language Models are Few-Shot Learners (Brown et al., 2020, arXiv:2005.14165) — §1–2 with Figure 2.1's zero/one/few-shot definitions, Figure 1.3's aggregate curves, §3.9.1 arithmetic, §5 limitations. Write an evidence brief on "GPT-3 learns tasks from demonstrations in context" and say what the few-shot results cannot establish.
  • Wei et al., "Emergent Abilities of Large Language Models" (TMLR 2022, arXiv:2206.07682) — the definition and the catalogue of claimed emergent abilities. Read it before the rebuttal below.
  • Schaeffer, Miranda & Koyejo, "Are Emergent Abilities of Large Language Models a Mirage?" (arXiv:2304.15004) — the alternative explanation and its three tests. Grade both papers for strongest claim, evidence grade, and institutional position, then name the crux neither resolves.
  • In-context Learning and Induction Heads (Olsson et al., Anthropic, 2022, transformer-circuits.pub) — optional. The argument overview and the six lines of evidence. Rank the six by causal strength before the authors do.
  • International AI Safety Report 2026 (internationalaisafetyreport.org) — optional. §1.2 "Current capabilities" (pp. 26–31) with §1.3 "Capabilities by 2030" (pp. 32–43). Read it asking which capability claims would survive the Schaeffer treatment.

Do

Manufacture the metric artifact with numbers you control. Take a smooth per-token accuracy curve (p rising 0.5 → 0.95 across ten model "scales") and score a 10-token exact-match task at each scale (accuracy = p^10). Plot both curves on one figure: one is a gentle ramp, the other an emergence poster. Re-score with token edit distance and watch the cliff dissolve. Predict where the exact-match "emergence point" moves when answers are 5 tokens instead of 10, then check.

Then write the comparative memo. Three candidate explanations for broad capability: scale (bigger models represent more), data coverage (the corpus already contains the tasks), and in-context learning as meta-learning (prediction forces task inference). Grade each against this week's assigned evidence: what it explains, what it fails to predict, and which observation would discriminate it from the other two. The memo rubric from How to work applies: whichever side of Wei/Schaeffer you take, the other paper's best exhibit appears at full strength.

Done when the memo takes an explicit position on the Wei/Schaeffer disagreement, names the specific metric choice that drives it, and says in one sentence which metric should govern capability claims and why.

Skip if you can already take a benchmark curve with an apparent discontinuity, name a metric transformation that could manufacture it from smooth underlying improvement, and say what continuous measurement you'd request to check.

Take away

  • A model trained to predict the next token over a task-diverse corpus is trained on thousands of tasks at once, because predicting text well requires performing whatever task the text embodies. The breadth lives in the data; prediction is the extractor.
  • Few-shot prompting installs nothing at inference. No gradient moves, the demonstrations only condition the forward pass, so the capability was already latent and the demonstrations locate it.
  • A sharp jump on a benchmark is not evidence of a sharp change inside the model. Exact-match on a k-token answer scores roughly p^k when per-token accuracy is p: 0.8^10 ≈ 0.11 rising to 0.9^10 ≈ 0.35 triples a benchmark score off a 12% per-token gain. Most published emergence plots never show the continuous version.
  • Wei et al. define an ability as emergent when it is absent in smaller models, present in larger ones, and not predictable by extrapolation. Schaeffer et al. answer that metric choice and undersized test sets manufacture the sharpness.
  • Policy relevance: evals-gated scaling and if-then commitments presume dangerous capabilities arrive measurably and incrementally. EU AI Act Article 51 presumes "high impact capabilities" and systemic risk above 10^25 FLOP of training compute, and Recital 111 concedes that a model's full range of capabilities "could be better understood" only after it reaches the market.
  • If Schaeffer is right, continuous metrics buy back forecastability and the case shifts toward better-instrumented eval-based triggers. If Wei is right about even a few decision-relevant capabilities, a compute threshold has the virtue of firing on inputs, before the surprise.
Check yourself
  1. In GPT-3's few-shot setting, what is and isn't updated when the model "learns" from demonstrations, and why does that distinction change what the results are evidence for?
  2. Name Schaeffer et al.'s three tests against the emergence claim.
  3. Olsson et al. give six lines of evidence linking induction heads to in-context learning. Which is causally strongest, which weakest, and where does the case thin out?
  4. A minister asks: "Models keep surprising us, shouldn't a compute threshold trigger mandatory evaluation?" Give the two-sentence answer this week licenses.

Answers. (1) Nothing is updated: no gradient steps, the demonstrations only condition the forward pass, so the capability must already be latent from pretraining and the results are evidence of task location rather than task acquisition at inference. (2) Reanalyzing GPT-3 arithmetic under continuous metrics, a meta-analysis of BIG-Bench emergence claims, and deliberately inducing "emergence" in vision models by metric choice alone. (3) Strongest: direct ablation, an intervention rather than a correlation. Weakest: macroscopic co-occurrence, where the phase change and the in-context-learning improvement merely happen together. The ablation evidence comes from small models; for large models the case leans on continuity arguments, which the paper calls circumstantial. (4) "Much of the surprise so far is measurement artifact: under continuous metrics most capability growth is smooth, so well-instrumented evals can see capabilities coming. Keep the compute threshold as a cheap administrative trigger, but the safety work happens in the metric design of the evaluations it triggers; a threshold that fires theater evals bought you nothing."

The modern architecture family

Read

  • "Mixture of Experts Explained" (Sanseviero et al., Hugging Face, 2023, huggingface.co/blog/moe) — read before DeepSeek: sparsity, routing, load balancing and expert capacity, "When to use sparse MoEs vs dense models?". Draw the token → router → expert path and write the expert-capacity formula from memory, then check.
  • DeepSeek-V3 Technical Report (DeepSeek, 2024, arXiv:2412.19437) — §2.1.1 (MLA, cache compression), §2.1.2 (DeepSeekMoE and the auxiliary-loss-free load-balancing pitch), and Table 1. Skip §3's infrastructure and the multi-token-prediction objective. Write an evidence brief on Table 1's training cost and what it excludes.
  • The Llama 3 Herd of Models (Meta, 2024, arXiv:2407.21783) — optional, §3.2 only: the modern dense consensus, with RoPE, SwiGLU, RMSNorm, and GQA. Note what a frontier lab priced when it declined this week's headline idea.
  • Mamba (Gu & Dao, 2023, arXiv:2312.00752) — optional, §1 only. Two sentences in your notes on what a fixed-size recurrent state must give up that a cache of everything does not.
  • CS336 Lecture 4, "Attention alternatives and mixture of experts" (cs336.stanford.edu) — optional. Covers this week's MoE material and Mamba's question in one session.
  • FlashAttention is absent on purpose: it makes exact attention cheaper without changing the architecture, and the kernels arrive in W22.

Do

Do the KV-cache accounting with the GQA paper (Ainslie et al., 2023, arXiv:2305.13245) as your reference. For a 48-layer model with 40 query heads of dimension 128 at fp16, compute K and V cache bytes per token under multi-head, grouped-query (8 KV heads), and multi-query attention, then scale each to 128K tokens. Predict the GQA-8 shrink factor first. Then place multi-head latent attention on the same axis: it compresses K and V into one low-rank latent per token instead of dropping KV heads. Optional running version: retrofit GQA into your W15 model by sharing K/V projections across query-head groups, and verify the projection weights shrink by exactly the factor your formula predicts.

Then write the architecture decision memo. Four named deployment settings (edge assistant on-device, high-throughput API tier, long-context document analysis, on-prem fine-tune on a fixed GPU budget), each getting an architecture recommendation (dense or MoE, attention variant, rough size) justified by this week's mechanisms. House rule: no model may be described by a single parameter number, so every recommendation states its (total parameters, active parameters, cache per token) triple.

Done when each of the four recommendations opens by naming the single binding constraint it optimizes (device memory, FLOPs per token, cache size at target context, VRAM under fine-tuning), and a reader given only those four constraints can reproduce all four picks from this week's readings.

Skip if you can already take a spec sheet (layers, head count and dimension, KV heads, precision, expert count and size, experts per token) and produce cache-per-token, active versus total parameters, and the deployment constraint each choice relaxes.

Take away

  • Sparse experts buy capacity without paying for it per token: replace the feed-forward layer with many experts, route each token to a few, and total parameters decouple from active parameters. The price is a router that must be trained, load that must be balanced, and every expert resident in memory whether this token used it or not.
  • Total parameters no longer predict per-token compute. DeepSeek-V3 is 671B for memory, procurement, and headlines, and 37B for FLOPs per token, latency, and training cost. A rule that uses one number for both jobs is wrong by roughly 18× on one axis.
  • The KV cache scales with KV heads, not query heads, which is the whole of the GQA trick: 8 KV heads instead of 40 cuts the cache 5×, one head cuts it 40×. MLA takes the other route and compresses K and V into a low-rank latent.
  • Every amendment carries a bill. Routing is self-reinforcing, so it needs either an auxiliary balancing loss or DeepSeek-V3's per-expert bias adjustment. Llama 3 declined MoE outright "to maximize training stability." Pre-Mamba subquadratic models bought their speed by giving up content-based routing.
  • Policy relevance: Article 51 of the AI Act presumes systemic risk above 10^25 training FLOPs, a compute threshold rather than a parameter count. That choice is the only reason it survives MoE at all. A parameter rule cannot say whether DeepSeek-V3 is a 671B model or a 37B one, and both answers are defensible.
  • The erosion argument comes free: 6ND with 37B active parameters over 14.8T tokens puts V3's pre-training near 3×10^24 FLOPs, under the threshold at capabilities the threshold was drawn to catch. S4 takes up what threshold design does about that.
Check yourself
  1. For the build's config (48 layers, 40 query heads, head dimension 128, fp16), what is the KV cache per token under MHA, GQA-8, and MQA, and at 128K context?
  2. DeepSeek-V3: 671B total, 37B active. Which number governs FLOPs per token, and which governs the memory needed to serve it?
  3. Why does a learned router need a load-balancing mechanism at all? Name the classic fix and DeepSeek-V3's variation.
  4. In Gu & Dao's framing, what did pre-Mamba subquadratic models trade away relative to attention, and why does W13 make that trade legible?

Answers. (1) Per token: 2 (K and V) × 48 layers × KV heads × 128 dims × 2 bytes. MHA with 40 KV heads ≈ 983 KB/token, ≈ 129 GB at 128K tokens; GQA-8 ≈ 197 KB/token, ≈ 26 GB; MQA with one head ≈ 25 KB/token, ≈ 3.2 GB. (2) Active parameters (37B) set FLOPs per token, hence latency and training compute; total parameters (671B) set the weights that must be resident to serve, hence VRAM and hardware count. (3) Routing is self-reinforcing: favored experts see more tokens, train more, improve, and attract more, until the network collapses onto a few. The classic fix is an auxiliary loss pushing the router toward equal expert importance; DeepSeek-V3 balances without one, via per-expert bias adjustment, avoiding the gradient interference an extra loss term adds. (4) Content-based routing: earlier subquadratic models compress history into a fixed-size state and cannot select by content, which is the operation W13 showed attention performing. Mamba's selectivity tries to buy that back without the quadratic, and the standing trade is a compressed state against a cache of everything.

Decoding and inference behavior

Read

  • kipply, "Transformer Inference Arithmetic" (2022, kipp.ly/transformer-inference-arithmetic) — kv cache, capacity, latency, batch sizes, flops counting, with a 52B model on A100s as the running example. Rebuild the per-token KV-cache formula and the memory-bound-versus-compute-bound condition from memory before checking.
  • Hugging Face Transformers, generation docs (strategies guide, GenerationConfig) — greedy search, multinomial sampling, and beam search; then temperature, top_k, top_p, do_sample, num_beams, use_cache. Check the defaults your Hub models ship in generation_config.json.
  • CS336 Lecture 10, "Inference" (cs336.stanford.edu) — optional. The same arithmetic from the systems side, with the course's cost-accounting examples. Watch it after kipply rather than instead of it.
  • vLLM and PagedAttention (arXiv:2309.06180) are held for W23; here you only need to know the cache they page.

Do

The decoding grid: load a small instruct model (0.5–1B is plenty; your W15 GPT also works), fix one factual prompt and one open-ended prompt, and sweep temperature {0.2, 0.7, 1.0, 1.5} × top-p {0.5, 0.9, 1.0}, five samples per cell with logged seeds, plus a greedy row (do_sample=False) as baseline. Per cell, record correctness on the factual prompt and diversity and repetition on the open one. Predict the worst cell before you run it.

The KV-cache bill: take Llama-2-7B's config (32 layers, d_model 4096, fp16, 4096 context) and compute cache bytes per token, per full-context sequence, and for a batch of 8, against the ~13.5 GB the weights occupy. With grouped-query attention, divide by the head-group ratio. Write both parts up as the inference lab notebook: the grid with per-cell outputs and seeds, the cache worksheet, and a half-page note titled "What sampling settings must accompany any reported eval."

Done when the note names all three settings (temperature, the truncation rule, the protocol) and points at one pair of grid cells (same prompt, same weights, one setting changed, correctness changed) that a reader can reproduce from your logged seeds.

Skip if you can already take a config sheet (layers, d_model, precision, context length), produce the KV-cache bill, and name the three sampling settings an eval report must disclose.

Phase III ends here. Add the current-model dossier snapshot for one frontier model from the roster page: architecture, active and total parameters, attention and routing, context, precision, served inference configuration, with your own arithmetic on the KV-cache line. The phase gate defends a tensor-shape trace, a trained GPT, and this week's cache-and-settings arithmetic; it is on the gates page.

Take away

  • Temperature and nucleus sampling are two different surgeries on the same distribution. Temperature divides the logits before the softmax, so below 1 sharpens toward the argmax and above 1 flattens toward uniform. Top-p keeps only the smallest set of tokens whose probability mass reaches p, then renormalizes.
  • A decoding setting is not intrinsic capability. "The model scored 71%" is shorthand for "the model, under some temperature, some truncation rule, and some sampling protocol, scored 71%." Change a setting and the number moves, sometimes past a deployment threshold.
  • Long context is a memory problem. The KV cache grows linearly in tokens and in batch size, and at Llama-2-7B's shape it overtakes the ~13.5 GB of fp16 weights by batch 8 at full context. GQA divides the bill by the head-group ratio.
  • Batch-1 decoding is memory-bandwidth-bound: each step streams essentially all the weights to produce one token, about one flop per byte moved. The chip's flops-to-bandwidth ratio (~200 on kipply's A100 numbers) is roughly the batch size where compute becomes the binding constraint instead.
  • Policy relevance: EU AI Act Article 55(1)(a) requires providers of GPAI models with systemic risk to perform model evaluation "in accordance with standardised protocols and tools reflecting the state of the art", documented per Annex XI and enforced by the Commission's AI Office. A standardised protocol that omits sampling settings isn't one.
  • The disclosure line is four items in one sentence: temperature, truncation rule, protocol, seeds. Developer system cards print them at the developer's discretion, which is the other live venue.
Check yourself
  1. What does temperature do to the next-token distribution, what does top-p do, and which settings recover greedy decoding?
  2. For a 32-layer, d_model-4096 model in fp16: KV-cache bytes per token? Per 4096-token sequence? What happens at batch 8, relative to the weights?
  3. A 2,000-token prompt is processed far faster than 2,000 generated tokens, despite similar total flops. Why?
  4. An eval report says "Model A 71%, Model B 69%," settings unreported. Give two distinct reasons the comparison is uninterpretable.

Answers. (1) Temperature divides the logits before the softmax: T < 1 sharpens toward the argmax, T > 1 flattens, T → 0 is greedy in the limit. Top-p keeps the smallest token set whose cumulative probability ≥ p and renormalizes over it. Greedy is do_sample=False with num_beams=1; top_p=1.0 turns truncation off. (2) 2 tensors (K, V) × 2 bytes × 32 layers × 4096 = 524,288 bytes ≈ 0.5 MB per token; × 4096 tokens ≈ 2.1 GB per sequence; × 8 ≈ 17 GB, at which point the cache outweighs the ~13.5 GB of fp16 weights, as W13's answer 4 predicted. (3) Prefill processes all prompt tokens in parallel, so one weight-read serves 2,000 tokens of work and their K and V go straight into the cache, while decode is serial and pays a full weight-read per generated token: same flops, different arithmetic intensity. (4) Settings are part of the measured system, and since a settings change alone can flip correctness, a 2-point gap can be a configuration artifact rather than a capability difference; and without the protocol (greedy or sampled, samples per item, seeds) you cannot compute either number's variance, so there is no error bar and no significance claim (W10).

Training data

Read

  • Dolma: an Open Corpus of Three Trillion Tokens for Language Model Pretraining Research (Soldaini et al., Allen Institute for AI, ACL 2024, arXiv:2402.00159) — the source inventory, the pipeline stage order, the content filters, and the open toolkit. Redraw the pipeline closed-book, one sentence per stage on why it sits where it does.
  • The FineWeb Datasets: Decanting the Web for the Finest Text Data at Scale (Penedo et al., Hugging Face, arXiv:2406.17557) — "Building FineWeb" (ablations, filter stack, MinHash dedup) and "FineWeb-Edu" (the rubric, the classifier, the threshold). Grade the claim "more deduplication is better" against their own ablation.
  • Cosmopedia: how to create large-scale synthetic data for pre-training Large Language Models (Ben Allal et al., Hugging Face, March 2024, huggingface.co/blog/cosmopedia) — prompt curation and the benchmark decontamination protocol behind 25B Mixtral-generated tokens. Write the evidence brief on "synthetic data sidesteps the consent problem."
  • DataComp-LM: In search of the next generation of training sets for language models (Li, Fang, Smyrnis, Ivgi et al., arXiv:2406.11794) — optional. A controlled curation benchmark: fixed data pool, fixed training recipes, model-based filtering the decisive knob. Capability-side evidence only; nothing on consent, provenance, or deletion.
  • CS336 Lectures 13 and 14, "Data" (cs336.stanford.edu) — optional. Sources and datasets, then filtering, deduplication, mixing, and synthetic data; Assignment 4 turns raw Common Crawl into pretraining data. Watch after the papers.

Do

Stream a few thousand documents from the FineWeb dataset on Hugging Face (streaming, so you never download 15T of anything). Implement two heuristics from this week's papers, a Gopher-style repetition rule and a C4-style terminal-punctuation rule, plus exact paragraph dedup. Predict each filter's removal rate before running it, then inspect ten removed documents and find at least one false positive.

Audit yourself for contamination. nanoGPT's prepare.py split your W15 Shakespeare corpus by position: first 90% train, last 10% val, no overlap check across the boundary. Take every 8-word n-gram from the val split and count verbatim hits in train. Predict the count first, then keep the number.

Then write a two-page data-governance plan for a hypothetical pretraining run, in the form you would hand an auditor. Six sections: sources, with the provenance record each document carries (origin URL, crawl date, license or opt-out status); the filter stack, each filter paired with the ablation that justifies it; dedup level and scope, with a position on FineWeb's global-dedup trap; PII handling; the eval-decontamination protocol (which eval sets, what n-gram size, what happens on a hit); and the documentation the pipeline emits, mapped to Annex XI's data fields and the Article 53(1)(d) public summary.

Done when the plan names one concrete contamination channel your own W15 Shakespeare run had, with the overlap count your build measured, and points to the specific check in your protocol that would catch that same channel in a frontier run.

Skip if you can already take a public dataset card (Dolma's works), name the pipeline stage behind each documented choice, the Annex XI field it would populate, and one thing the card leaves unauditable.

Take away

  • "Data quality" has only an operational definition: a filter is good if models trained on the filtered corpus score better. A serious corpus is therefore a stack of ablation results, one experiment per stage.
  • The filter is increasingly a model. FineWeb-Edu's classifier and Cosmopedia's generator put one model's judgment upstream of the next model's corpus, and those judgments become corpus law that no human wrote or can read off.
  • Dedup intensity is not monotone in quality. Global MinHash across all 96 Common Crawl snapshots removed up to ~90% of the oldest snapshots, and the surviving 10% was worse than what was removed, so FineWeb dedups per snapshot.
  • Provenance is not a copyright field. Without a per-document record of where text came from you cannot trace an eval-set leak, honor a deletion request or a machine-readable opt-out, excise a poisoned source, or audit representation. It is captured at collection or it is gone.
  • The decision this bears on is what pretraining data documentation to require. EU AI Act Annex XI Section 1 point 2(c), implementing Article 53(1)(a), obliges GPAI providers to document the "type and provenance of data and curation methodologies," data-point counts, how data was obtained and selected, and measures to detect unsuitable sources and identifiable biases.
  • Article 53(1)(d) adds a public "sufficiently detailed summary" of training content, per the AI Office template published 24 July 2025. Counts and source categories are cheap to produce after the fact; provenance cannot be retrofitted, and a requirement written without that distinction buys either theater or non-compliance.
  • Article 53(1)(c)'s duty to honor machine-readable text-and-data-mining reservations under Article 4(3) of Directive (EU) 2019/790 is the live fight over whether scraping for training needs permission at all. R5 takes it up.
Check yourself
  1. Name three engineering operations (not legal duties) that break when the pipeline keeps no per-document provenance.
  2. FineWeb deduplicates each Common Crawl snapshot independently. What did the global-dedup ablation show, why, and what does that do to "deduplicate harder" as a universal recipe?
  3. FineWeb-Edu's filter is a classifier. What new dependency does that insert into the corpus, and what five things should an auditor now demand to see?
  4. Your W15 run: name its contamination channel precisely, and the frontier-scale check that would catch the same channel.

Answers. (1) Any three of: tracing an eval-set leak back to its source; executing a deletion or opt-out, since you cannot remove what you cannot locate and honoring a TDM reservation requires the origin URL; excising or reweighting a source discovered to be poisoned or low-quality; choosing dedup scope per source; auditing representation across sources. (2) Global MinHash across all 96 snapshots removed up to ~90% of the oldest crawls, and the surviving 10% was worse than what was removed (more ads, keyword lists, malformed text), because dedup eliminated large clusters of stable, often good content while small scattered clusters of junk survived. Per-snapshot dedup trained better models, so dedup intensity is a knob to ablate rather than maximize. (3) The corpus now depends on Llama-3-70B-Instruct's judgment of "educational," distilled into a classifier. The auditor asks for the scoring rubric and prompt, the annotation sample, the classifier itself, the threshold (3), and the validation figures (F1 82%); without those, "filtered for quality" is unfalsifiable. (4) Position-based splitting with no overlap check: prepare.py takes the first 90% of one document as train and the last 10% as val, and Shakespeare repeats strings verbatim across that boundary (speaker headers, stock phrases), so validation loss partly scores memorized strings; your build counted the hits. The frontier check is train-eval decontamination run before training, n-gram overlap between corpus and every eval set (Cosmopedia's version: 10-gram overlap then sequence matching at 0.5), hits logged and removed.

Scaling laws and algorithmic efficiency

Read

  • Kaplan et al., "Scaling Laws for Neural Language Models" (arXiv:2001.08361) — §1.2, §2.1 (C ≈ 6NBS), §3, §6 Optimal Allocation, §6.3 "Contradictions and a Conjecture." Grade the fit: strongest claim, evidence grade, the crux for trusting it outside its regime.
  • Hoffmann et al., "Training Compute-Optimal Large Language Models" (arXiv:2203.15556) — §3.1–3.4 and §4, read after Kaplan. Grade it the same way, then name the training-setup variable that separates the two.
  • Ho et al., "Algorithmic progress in language models" (Epoch AI, arXiv:2403.05812) — the headline halving estimate, the augmented-scaling-law method, the compute-versus-algorithms attribution. Write the evidence brief before you deploy the number anywhere.
  • Meta, "Introducing Meta Llama 3" (blog) and "The Llama 3 Herd of Models" (arXiv:2407.21783) — §3.2.1 Scaling Laws, plus the blog's token arithmetic. Keep a ledger of every token count; the artifact and S4 need them.
  • CS336 Lectures 9 and 11, "Scaling laws" (cs336.stanford.edu) — optional, the fitting craft taught live. Watch after the papers.

Do

Transcribe (model size, tokens, loss) points from Chinchilla's IsoFLOP profiles (§3.2) and fit L(N, D) = E + A/N^α + B/D^β. Hold out the largest compute budget, predict its valley, then check. Derive the allocation rule your fit implies along the C ≈ 6ND constraint and plot it against Kaplan's N ∝ C^0.73, from your W15 GPT's budget up to 3.8×10^25 FLOPs. Drop the two largest budgets, refit, and watch α, β, and the implied allocation move.

Ship a notebook: sourced data points, the fitted form with the held-out extrapolation check, one figure showing both allocation rules with Meta's actual choices (405B, 15.6T) and (8B, 15T) marked, and a closing paragraph on where the two prescriptions diverge.

Done when the notebook prints each law's recommended (N, D) at 3.8×10^25 FLOPs, Llama 3's stated budget, beside Meta's actual choices, and the closing paragraph names which regime current frontier practice follows (Kaplan's allocation, Chinchilla's, or deliberate over-training) with one cited token count as evidence.

Skip if you can already write down Kaplan's and Chinchilla's prescribed (N, D) allocations for a given budget, name the training-setup variable that separates them, and say which regime Llama 3's 8B followed.

Take away

  • Compute-optimality answers one question: at a fixed training-FLOP budget, which split between parameters and tokens minimizes final training loss. It says nothing about capability or inference cost, which is where most of what policy wants from these laws lives.
  • Matching learning-rate schedules to token budget moved the answer from "grow the model" to "grow both, equally." A fit has a regime, and the prescription can flip inside one architecture family. W38 extrapolates these curves into forecasts.
  • "Chinchilla-optimal" is not how frontier models are trained. Twenty tokens per parameter (Chinchilla's own 1.4T ÷ 70B) is a training-loss optimum, not a business optimum. Llama 3's 8B took 15T tokens, roughly 75× the ~200B Meta calls Chinchilla-optimal, because training compute is paid once and inference compute per token served.
  • In L(N, D) = E + A/N^α + B/D^β, E is the irreducible entropy of the text distribution, W4's H(p). Only the two power-law terms are the model's KL to reduce.
  • The decision this bears on is how much capability a training-compute threshold can bear, and for how long. Article 51's 10^25 presumption (administered by the AI Office) and California SB 53's 10^26-plus-revenue test both assume capability tracks training compute. Inside a fitted regime that holds remarkably well.
  • Some Kaplan trends span more than seven orders of magnitude, but three things decay the assumption: algorithmic progress (Epoch's ~8-month effective-compute halving), distillation (capability crosses the line while training compute stays under it), and inference-time scaling (W28's, and invisible to a training-FLOP metric). S4 designs the repair.
Check yourself
  1. A lab quadruples its training compute. What does Kaplan's §6 tell it to do with the extra budget, what does Chinchilla tell it, and what training-setup variable explains the disagreement?
  2. Llama 3's 8B trained on 15T tokens against a Chinchilla-optimal ~200B. Chinchilla's math says that compute would have bought lower loss in a bigger model. Why was Meta right anyway?
  3. Article 51 presumes systemic risk at 10^25 training FLOPs. Using Epoch's central estimate, roughly how much does the capability meaning of that fixed line decay over three years, and what do the CI bounds do to your answer?
  4. "Our fitted law says the next 10× of compute brings capability X, so regulate now." Name the two distinct weak links, and the claim-ladder rung the sentence skips.

Answers. (1) Kaplan: put nearly everything into model size (N ∝ C^0.73, D ∝ C^0.27), stopped before convergence. Chinchilla: double parameters and tokens together. The variable is the learning-rate schedule; cosine cycle length must roughly match training steps, and Kaplan's runs reused schedules unmatched to each token budget, overstating loss at intermediate horizons and biasing the fit toward big-model, data-light optima. (2) Compute-optimal prices training and nothing else. Serving cost scales with N on every token forever, so a small model trained ~75× past its optimum is the cheaper route to a given capability at deployment, which is what the Herd paper says. The optimum moved because the objective did (W18's arithmetic). (3) Halving every 8 months gives 36/8 = 4.5 halvings ≈ 23×, so three years on roughly 4×10^23 physical FLOPs plus current algorithms buys what 10^25 bought while the number stands still. The CI of 5–14 months puts the three-year decay anywhere from ~6× to ~150×: certain in direction, wildly uncertain in rate. (4) Fit-to-regime: the law holds under the data, schedule, and architecture it was fit on, and Kaplan→Chinchilla shows the prescription can flip. Loss-to-capability: loss measures fit to a distribution (W4), and task metrics can move discontinuously against smooth loss (W16). The sentence rides a measurement straight to a recommendation without stopping at forecast.

Compute and hardware

Read

  • How To Scale Your Model, Parts 1, 2, and 12 (rooflines, TPUs, GPUs) — rooflines and ridge points, TPU anatomy, GPU anatomy and the H100/B200 spec tables. Rebuild the roofline diagram closed-book and derive the ridge point.
  • AI Chips: What They Are and Why They Matter (Khan & Mann, CSET, April 2020, cset.georgetown.edu) — transistor economics, the cost model, fab concentration, the GPU/FPGA/ASIC taxonomy. It is a 2020 document; grade which claims survived six years.
  • LBNL, 2024 United States Data Center Energy Usage Report (doi.org/10.71468/P1WC7Q) and RAND, AI's Power Requirements Under Exponential Growth (rand.org) — skim for the numbers you will cite from a ledger, then write the evidence brief on one projection.
  • Epoch AI, Data on Notable AI Models (epoch.ai/data/notable-ai-models) — optional as reading, mandatory for the artifact. Read the estimation methodology and the Confident/Likely/Speculative tiers before you defend your own numbers against theirs.

Do

Build the spec-sheet roofline ledger. Tabulate peak dense BF16 FLOP/s, HBM capacity and bandwidth, interconnect bandwidth, and TDP for H100 and B200 (add a TPU generation if you like). Compute each chip's ridge point, place three workloads you already own on the plot (a dense training matmul, W18's batch-1 decode, prefill), and check that the chips agree about which side each lands on. Halve the assumed utilization in a 6ND-to-wall-clock conversion and watch the estimated training time move.

Then estimate one named frontier model's training run, with explicit sensitivity ranges: training FLOPs by both routes (6ND from architecture; accelerator-hours × peak FLOP/s × utilization), accelerator count, wall-clock time, energy (chip TDP through node overhead and PUE to facility MWh), and cost. Close with a sensitivity table, one row per assumption (utilization, precision, token count, retries, PUE, $/GPU-hour).

Done when your point estimates bracket at least one published independent estimate from Epoch's database, and the sensitivity table identifies which single assumption dominates the spread.

Skip if you can already read the ridge point off an accelerator spec sheet, place a dense training matmul and batch-1 decode on the correct sides of it, and produce a 6ND estimate with a stated utilization for one named model.

Take away

  • Every workload has an arithmetic intensity (FLOPs performed per byte moved) and every chip has a ridge point (peak FLOP/s divided by memory bandwidth). Which is bigger decides whether you are buying compute or buying bandwidth.
  • An H100 delivers ~990 dense BF16 TFLOP/s against 3.35 TB/s of HBM, a ridge near 300 FLOPs per byte. B200 roughly doubles both numbers and the ratio barely moves. That keeps W18's batch-1 decode memory-bound on every chip you will ever audit.
  • A public training-compute estimate is not a measurement. It is a reconstruction from unknowns (token count, active parameters, precision, utilization, failed runs), each worth a factor. Epoch labels its own estimates Confident (within 3×), Likely (within 10×), or Speculative (within 30×). A bare point estimate has silently claimed the top tier.
  • The reference anchors: LBNL puts US data centers at 176 TWh and 4.4% of US electricity in 2023, projected to 325–580 TWh and 6.7–12% by 2028. RAND projects 68 GW of global AI data-center demand by 2027 and single training runs approaching 1 GW by 2028.
  • TWh and GW are different quantities. Energy summed over a year answers consumption-share and emissions questions; instantaneous power is what an interconnection queue allocates. Conflating them is the most common quantitative error in data-center policy writing.
  • The decision this bears on is how to define training-compute reporting: who measures, in what units, verified how. Three enacted regimes denominate obligations in FLOPs (the EU AI Act, California's TFAIA, and New York's RAISE Act, amended March 2026 to track California and effective January 2027), and none fully specifies the measurement.
  • Article 51 presumes systemic risk above 10^25 training FLOPs, documented to the AI Office under Annex XI. California's SB 53, signed September 29, 2025, defines a frontier model as one trained on more than 10^26 operations including subsequent fine-tuning and other material modifications, cumulative and self-measured.
  • The rule the statutes haven't written: the unit (cumulative FLOPs at a stated precision, pretraining-only or lifetime, where the laws disagree), the route (logged accelerator-hours cross-checked against 6ND), and the audit. Accelerator-hours are invoiced; utilization is self-reported, and a 6ND figure that cannot reach the disclosed compute is a catchable inconsistency.
Check yourself
  1. Define arithmetic intensity and ridge point. Compute the H100's ridge from ~990 BF16 TFLOP/s and 3.35 TB/s, and say what it means for a workload sitting below it.
  2. Name the two independent routes to a training-FLOP estimate and the inputs each requires. Which could an auditor verify, and from what records?
  3. Public estimates of a closed model's training compute can spread across an order of magnitude. Name three distinct unknowns that contribute, and say where your artifact's dominant one showed up.
  4. LBNL reports 176 TWh; RAND projects 68 GW. Why are these not the same kind of number, and which one does a grid interconnection queue care about?

Answers. (1) Arithmetic intensity is FLOPs performed per byte moved to or from memory; the ridge point is peak FLOP/s ÷ memory bandwidth, the intensity at which a chip stops being bandwidth-bound. 990e12 / 3.35e12 ≈ 295 FLOPs per byte. Below the ridge a workload is memory-bound: more FLOP/s won't help, more bandwidth or higher intensity via batching will. (2) Architecture route: 6ND, needing active parameters per token and training tokens, both from disclosure. Hardware route: accelerator count × wall-clock × peak FLOP/s × utilization, needing cluster records plus a utilization figure. The hardware route is auditable from accelerator-hour logs and invoices; utilization is its soft spot, which is why cross-checking the two routes is the audit. (3) Token count (rarely disclosed), active-versus-total parameters under MoE (W17's two-questions-one-name problem), precision and its matching peak-FLOP/s definition, utilization, and failed-run overhead. Which dominates is empirical, and your sensitivity table answers it; for most hardware-route estimates it is utilization, the one factor in the product with no invoice or spec sheet behind it. (4) TWh is energy consumed over a year; GW is the instantaneous draw the infrastructure must serve. An interconnection queue allocates capacity, so it cares about GW at peak, while LBNL's TWh answers consumption-share and emissions questions. They are linked only through load factor.

Distributed training

Read

  • The Ultra-Scale Playbook (Hugging Face nanotron, playbook) — "First Steps: Training on one GPU" through "5D parallelism in a nutshell," plus Appendix A0 on collectives; skip "Diving in the GPUs" except Flash Attention. Rebuild the 5D table closed-book: what each parallelism shards, communicates, on which link.
  • CS336 Lectures 7 and 8, "Parallelism" (cs336.stanford.edu) — the same material taught live, with Lecture 5 "GPUs, TPUs" as background.
  • FlashAttention (Dao et al., 2022, arXiv:2205.14135) — tiling, exactness, memory complexity, benchmark methodology. Write the evidence brief: separate the kernel-level speedup claims from the end-to-end training claims.
  • ZeRO (Rajbhandari et al., 2019, arXiv:1910.02054) — optional. §3 "Where Did All the Memory Go?", the three partitioning stages, §7's communication analysis. Shut the paper and rebuild the memory-accounting table, one row per stage.
  • The Llama 3 Herd of Models, §3.3 (Meta, 2024, arXiv:2407.21783) — optional. §3.3.2 parallelism plan, §3.3.3 collectives, §3.3.4 "Reliability and Operational Challenges." Grade the claim: what accounting turns 419 unplanned interruptions into "over 90% effective training time"?

Do

Work Sasha Rush's LLM-Training-Puzzles (github.com/srush/LLM-Training-Puzzles, runs in the linked Colab): eight puzzles on training across many devices. The cluster is simulated; the reasoning is not. Take as many of the eight as the week allows, in order, and before each one write down which constraint you expect to bind, memory or communication, then check against the visualizer. The real-GPU version is the distributed-training half of CS336 Assignment 2 (Systems).

Then write the parallelism plan: one page of architecture-and-bottleneck analysis for a stated model on a stated cluster. Default case is a 70B-parameter dense transformer on 8 nodes of 8 × 80 GB GPUs, fast intra-node link, slower inter-node link, with your two bandwidths stated as assumptions. Compare two strategies (say pure ZeRO-3 data parallelism against tensor-parallel-8 within nodes × pipeline × data across them): memory per GPU, per-step communication volume on each link, which link saturates first, the plan you'd pick, and the two assumptions that would flip your answer.

Done when the analysis computes memory per GPU under both strategies (weights, gradients, optimizer states, and an activations line with its recomputation choice stated) and for each strategy names the communication link that saturates first, with the per-step byte count that says why.

Skip if you can already take a model config and a cluster spec and produce memory per GPU under two parallelism strategies with the saturating link named for each.

Take away

  • Every parallelism strategy is one move on a different tensor: shard something (optimizer states, gradients, parameters, activations, layers, experts) across devices, and pay for it with a collective on some link. Memory freed, communication owed. That is why they compose: 5D parallelism is five instances of the same trade at once.
  • Accelerator count is not delivered compute. A device count is a purchase order; a training run is that hardware times a parallelism plan, kernel efficiency, interconnect, and uptime. Both directions leak: ZeRO and FlashAttention raise what fixed hardware can train, and operational reality lowers it.
  • Llama 3's team logged 466 job interruptions across a 54-day pre-training snapshot on 16,384 GPUs, 419 of them unplanned, roughly one every three hours. Synchronous training makes any single device's failure everyone's failure.
  • Tensor parallelism communicates activations several times per layer in both directions, so it stays inside a node on the fastest link. Data parallelism communicates gradients once per step and tolerates slower links. Pipeline parallelism sends only stage-boundary activations and pays in bubbles instead.
  • The decision this bears on is whether reported accelerator access corresponds to usable training capacity. The live authority is Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security, whose advanced-computing export controls license chips by per-device performance metrics: a rule that holds device capability fixed while the software term moves fast, outside the regime entirely.
  • When a briefing says an actor "has N accelerators," the questions that matter are interconnect, parallelism plan, utilization, and uptime. Any threshold pegged to device counts decays at the rate of systems software, which S4 takes up as a design problem.
Check yourself
  1. Mixed-precision Adam, Ψ parameters: account for the 16 bytes per parameter, then say what ZeRO-1, ZeRO-2, and ZeRO-3 each add to the sharding.
  2. Data, tensor, pipeline: what does each shard, what does each communicate, and why does tensor parallelism rarely cross a node boundary?
  3. How does FlashAttention speed up attention without approximating it, and name a run condition under which a faster attention kernel barely moves end-to-end throughput.
  4. Your job runs on 512 GPUs at a fixed global batch size and you double to 1,024. Name three mechanisms by which step time can fail to halve.

Answers. (1) 2Ψ fp16 weights + 2Ψ fp16 gradients + 12Ψ optimizer states (fp32 master weights, momentum, variance, 4Ψ each) = 16Ψ bytes. ZeRO-1 partitions the optimizer states across data-parallel ranks; ZeRO-2 adds the gradients; ZeRO-3 adds the parameters, driving per-GPU model-state memory toward 16Ψ/N at the price of modestly higher communication volume. (2) Data parallelism shards the batch, not the model, and all-reduces gradients once per step, overlappable with the backward pass and tolerant of slower links. Tensor parallelism shards weight matrices inside each layer and communicates activations several times per layer forward and backward, traffic frequent enough to need the intra-node link. Pipeline parallelism shards layers into stages and sends stage-boundary activations point-to-point: cheap communication, but it pays in bubbles that no schedule fully eliminates. 1F1B's real purchase is activation memory, since its bubble matches AFAB's. (3) It tiles Q, K, V blocks through on-chip SRAM and recomputes attention in the backward pass instead of writing and rereading the n×n matrix in HBM: same math, exact result, far fewer slow-memory accesses. If step time is dominated by inter-node gradient synchronization or pipeline bubbles, attention is a thin slice of the bill and the speedup vanishes into the binding constraint. (4) Any three of: per-GPU batch shrinks, dropping arithmetic intensity toward the memory-bound regime; collective communication cost grows with device count and rides the slowest link; synchronization means the whole step waits on stragglers; deeper pipelining adds bubble fraction; failure probability rises with N, and each interruption costs lost work since the last checkpoint plus detection-and-restart overhead.

Inference systems and economics

Read

  • "How To Scale Your Model," Part 7 "All About Transformer Inference" (scaling book) — prefill/decode arithmetic intensity, throughput and latency tricks, "Designing an Effective Inference Engine," quantization, Appendix D "Speculative Sampling." Rebuild closed-book the batch size where decode flips to compute-bound.
  • Kwon et al., "Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention" (SOSP 2023, arXiv:2309.06180) — §3 (memory waste under contiguous allocation), §4 (PagedAttention, the block-table KV manager, scheduling and preemption), §6 (the 2–4× throughput result). Write the evidence brief on that 2–4×.
  • "How To Scale Your Model," Part 8 "Serving LLaMA 3-70B on TPUs" (applied inference) — memory sizing, the compute-bound batch threshold in bf16 versus int8, prefill handling, "Visualizing the Latency Throughput Tradeoff." Redo one worked problem with quantized weights, predicting the new batch threshold first.
  • CS336 Lecture 6 "Kernels, Triton," with the FlashAttention/Triton half of Assignment 2 (cs336.stanford.edu) — optional. The kernel level, where "memory movement dominates" becomes code you can profile.

Do

Two bookkeeping exercises, one sitting, no GPU. The waste ledger: sample 200 synthetic requests with mixed lengths (prompts 100–2,000 tokens, outputs 50–1,000, output lengths unknown in advance), compute total KV memory reserved under contiguous max-length preallocation versus 16-token paged blocks, and report the fraction of reserved memory holding actual token state. The batching simulator: Poisson arrivals over the same request mix, a fixed KV memory budget, two schedulers, static batching (requests admitted and retired as a group) against continuous batching (a finished sequence's slots free immediately). Measure throughput and tail latency, then double the variance of output lengths, predict which scheduler degrades, and run it.

Then price a deployment. A federal agency stands up a document-processing service on one open-weights model from the roster, and you owe them capacity and cost. Price three workload shapes on the same model and hardware: short chat (~500-token prompts, ~200-token outputs, interactive latency), long-context RAG (~30,000 tokens of retrieved records per request, ~500-token outputs, interactive latency), and overnight batch (the same document jobs, no latency target). For each shape give the binding constraint, the achievable batch size under a stated KV budget, prefill and decode priced separately, and $/token plus requests-per-hour-per-chip, with every assumption tabled.

Done when the budget's own arithmetic shows why the long-context shape dominates cost, so that a reader can point to the line where its per-request KV cache caps batch size below the compute-bound threshold while prefill flops scale with context; it names the two levers that cut that cost most, each tied to a specific term in that line; and a reader who accepts your assumptions can recompute all three $/token figures.

Skip if you can already take a model config, a chip spec sheet, and a workload description and produce a $/token estimate with the binding constraint named.

Phase IV closes here. Add the current-model dossier: pick one frontier model from the roster page and write its hardware-and-deployment snapshot, meaning accelerator, HBM and interconnect fabric, parallelism scheme, achieved utilization, rack power and cooling, and cost uncertainty. Tie every scale claim to a delivered system rather than a nominal accelerator count. The phase gate, on the gates page, defends the data, compute, hardware, and serving arithmetic of one frontier deployment as a single connected account.

Take away

  • Serving cost is a property of the workload, not just the model. Prefill is compute-bound, decode is memory-bound, and the KV cache decides how many requests share each weight-read. Prompt-to-output ratio, context length, concurrency, cache reuse, and the latency target set $/token; parameter count only sets the scale.
  • Parameter count is not a price. "A 70B model costs ten times a 7B model to serve" fails twice. The same model on the same hardware spans roughly a hundredfold range in per-token cost along the latency-throughput curve, and a long-context workload can make the smaller model the expensive one by drowning it in KV cache.
  • Paging was worth 2–4× throughput because contiguous allocation left real token state in as little as 20.4–38.2% of KV memory, and continuous batching recovers the rest at the scheduler without touching any model math.
  • Quantization and speculative decoding exploit the compute left idle by memory-bound decode. Quantization shrinks the bytes moved and can shift outputs; speculative sampling spends flops to bank several tokens per weight-read and provably preserves the target distribution.
  • The decision this bears on is how much serving capacity a deployment needs, and whether to build the infrastructure that follows. The venue is the state public utility commission, where utilities file data-center demand forecasts to justify new generation and transmission.
  • LBNL's congressionally mandated 2024 United States Data Center Energy Usage Report (December 2024), projecting data-center load doubling to tripling by 2028, is the standing reference. Every megawatt in those filings is tokens served times joules per token, so every one embeds assumptions about batching, quantization, workload shape, and utilization.
  • Efficiency cuts cost per token, and cheaper tokens have so far meant more tokens rather than less demand. Both halves belong in any forecast you defend, including the IFP report's figures.
Check yourself
  1. API price sheets charge several times less for input tokens than output tokens. Ground that ratio in this week's arithmetic.
  2. Contiguous max-length KV allocation wastes memory two ways: name both, say which PagedAttention eliminates outright, and then say what sits idle under static batching with mixed output lengths and why continuous batching recovers it.
  3. Quantization and speculative decoding both accelerate decode. One can change the model's output distribution and one provably doesn't. Which is which, and why does the memory-bound regime make both work?
  4. Same model, same chips: rank short chat, long-context RAG, and overnight batch by $/token, name each shape's binding constraint, and say which single workload parameter the ranking is most sensitive to.

Answers. (1) Prefill processes the whole prompt in parallel at high arithmetic intensity, compute-bound, one weight-read amortized over thousands of tokens. Decode streams the weights once per generated token, memory-bandwidth-bound, so each output token buys far more hardware time. The price ratio is the arithmetic-intensity gap, discounted by whatever batching claws back. (2) Internal waste: slots preallocated for the maximum possible output that the actual output never uses, plus reserved-for-future slots holding nothing yet. External fragmentation: gaps between differently sized contiguous allocations too small to serve a new request. Paged blocks eliminate external fragmentation outright and cap internal waste at less than one block per sequence. Under static batching, finished sequences' slots sit idle until the longest member of the batch completes; continuous batching admits a waiting request into freed slots at the next step, which is scheduling only, so it composes cleanly with paging. (3) Quantization changes the stored weights and KV representation, so logits shift and outputs can differ, a usually small quality risk traded for fewer bytes moved. Speculative sampling drafts tokens cheaply and verifies them under an acceptance rule that preserves the target distribution: same outputs statistically, faster wall clock, extra flops spent. Both pay off because memory-bound decode leaves compute idle. (4) Overnight batch is cheapest: no latency target, so batch size climbs to the compute-bound threshold. Short chat sits in the middle: small per-request KV allows respectable batching, but the latency target stops short of the threshold, so it is bandwidth-bound. Long-context RAG is most expensive: a ~30k-token KV cache per request caps the batch far below threshold, so decode is memory-capacity-bound while prefill flops scale with context. The ranking is most sensitive to context length, which moves both the KV cap and the prefill bill at once.

LoRA fine-tuning

Read

  • LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models (Hu et al., 2021, arXiv:2106.09685) — §§1–3, §4.1–4.2 (h = W₀x + BAx, the α/r scaling, which matrices get adapted), §5.5, §7.1–7.3. Closed-book: write the modified forward pass with every dimension attached and both initializations, then check.
  • Hugging Face PEFT, LoRA guide (docs; conceptual guide) — the common LoraConfig fields (r, lora_alpha, target_modules, bias, modules_to_save), merge_and_unload(), the initialization options. Map every field you set to the paper section that justifies it.
  • QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs (Dettmers et al., 2023, arXiv:2305.14314) — optional. The abstract and the three innovations (4-bit NormalFloat, double quantization, paged optimizers). Write an evidence brief on Guanaco's 99.3% of ChatGPT: who judged, on what population.

Do

Fine-tune a small open-weight model onto one narrow behavior a regex can judge. Base: Qwen3-1.7B or SmolLM3-3B (both Apache 2.0, instruct variant). Behavior: every answer ending with a one-line TL;DR:, every answer being exactly three bullets, a fixed refusal sentence for one benign topic. Two constraints: the base model must do it in under one held-out prompt in five, and the judge must be a committed script rather than your eye.

Order of operations: (1) write ~60 held-out prompts plus the scoring rule and commit them; (2) build a few hundred training examples exhibiting the behavior, recording where every example came from; (3) train with PEFT, LoraConfig at r=8, target_modules on the attention projections; (4) score held-out completions pre- and post-tune. Then retrain at r=1, predicting first whether the delta survives. Table 6 says rank 1 sufficed for {W_q, W_v} on their tasks.

Ship three pieces as one claim. The adapter: weights, config, seed, training data, code. An adapter card to W15's model-card standard: base model and exact revision, adapter config (r, α, target modules), training-data description with provenance, procedure, compute used, limitations, and the behavior delta as the headline measurement. The delta report: pre- and post-tune rates on the held-out set with counts, the commit hash of the preregistered prompt set, the scoring rule, three failure cases inspected by hand. Say plainly that the bundle establishes nothing about behavior off the measured distribution.

Done when the target behavior appears in >80% of held-out completions post-tune and <20% pre-tune under the committed mechanical rule, and the prompt set's commit predates the first training run. Both facts are checkable from your repo history.

Skip if you can already show a LoRA adapter you trained, with a prompt set committed before training and a measured behavior delta clearing that bar.

Take away

  • The unit of evidence for a fine-tune is a preregistered before/after delta: a held-out prompt set committed before training, a mechanical scoring rule, and the behavior's rate on both sides of the run. Falling loss, curated transcripts, and vibes are the small-scale version of the assurance language later phases teach you to refuse.
  • A small update is not a small change. §7.3 shows the learned update doesn't echo the base model's dominant directions; it amplifies directions the pretrained weights barely used, by a factor around 21.5 at rank 4 in their GPT-3 measurements. Parameter count measures the cost of a change, not its size.
  • B = 0 at step zero means ΔW = BA = 0, so training starts at the pretrained model and the first gradient step is a measured move from a known baseline. After training, BA merges into W₀ and the deployed model is a plain dense layer, so no inference latency.
  • LoRA cuts trainable parameters and optimizer states (~10,000× on GPT-3), GPU memory (~3×), and per-task storage. It leaves arithmetic per training token roughly untouched: forward and backward still run through the entire frozen base. A memory-and-storage win, not a FLOPs win.
  • Policy: which event turns a downstream fine-tuner into a regulated provider. The Commission's GPAI guidelines (C(2025) 5045 final, 18 July 2025, §3.2) make a downstream modifier the provider of a model, inheriting Article 53 duties.
  • The indicative criterion is modification compute exceeding one-third of the original's training compute, or one-third of 10²³ FLOPs when the original is unknown (one-third of 10²⁵ for a systemic-risk model).
  • Your run sits about six orders of magnitude below that fallback line while flipping a behavior outright, so a compute-indexed trigger will essentially never fire. The live question is whether safeguards survive modification at all, which W27 takes up.
Check yourself
  1. LoRA initializes A with random Gaussians and B at zero. Why must B start at zero, and why does the finished adapter add no inference latency?
  2. Table 6 shows rank 1 already competitive when adapting {W_q, W_v}. What does that say about the update the task needed, and what r=1 result of your own would have falsified W3's low-rank premise for your behavior?
  3. Your delta is <20% → >80%. Name the two distinct failure modes that committing the prompt set before training closes.
  4. Estimate your fine-tune's training compute with W21's 6ND rule and compare it to the Commission's fallback modification threshold. What does the ratio imply?

Answers. (1) With B = 0 the adapter contributes nothing at step zero, so training departs from a known baseline instead of a random perturbation you would have to train back out; h = W₀x + BAx = (W₀ + BA)x, so BA merges after training. (2) The update those tasks required had very low intrinsic rank, a few directions carrying the adaptation, W3's fast-decaying spectrum made empirical. Falsification: your delta collapsing at r=1 and climbing materially with r, evidence that your task's update needs more directions than the low-rank bet assumes. (3) Post-hoc prompt selection, which is transcript curation by another name, and contamination of the held-out set by the training corpus; the commit fixes the eval population before the corpus exists to leak into. (4) Roughly 6 × 1.7×10⁹ × 10⁶ ≈ 10¹⁶ FLOPs for a million training tokens through a 1.7B base, against a fallback line of a third of 10²³ ≈ 3×10²², about six and a half orders of magnitude above you, while the behavior flipped. A compute-indexed trigger classifies essentially every behavior-changing fine-tune as too small to regulate, so whatever protection it was meant to provide has to come from somewhere else.

Supervised tuning and human feedback

Read

  • The RLHF Book (Lambert, rlhfbook.com) — read in order: Instruction Fine-Tuning, Preference Data, Reward Modeling. Closed-book, redraw the pipeline with the artifact each stage produces.
  • Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback (Ouyang et al., 2022, arXiv:2203.02155) — §3.4 human data collection, §5.2 "Who are we aligning to?", §5.3 limitations, then skim §4.1. Write an evidence brief on the §4.1 headline: population, who judged, what it cannot establish.
  • The RLHF Book, "The Nature of Preferences" (ch. 10) — optional. Where preference-as-supervision came from, utility theory to reward functions. Read it if "whose preferences" still sounds rhetorical rather than technical.
  • ARENA, Ch. 2 Reinforcement Learning, §[2.4] RLHF (learn.arena.education) — optional. Implement RLHF end to end, PPO applied to language-model finetuning. Queue it after W26; the PPO prerequisite is §[2.3].

Do

Blind-label 30 chosen/rejected pairs sampled from Anthropic/hh-rlhf. Write down your predicted agreement rate first, then compute your actual rate against InstructGPT's 72.6%. Sort your disagreements into three bins: I'm right, the label is defensible, ambiguous. Keep the ambiguous count.

Draw the pipeline map on one page: base model → demonstration collection → SFT → preference collection → reward-model training → policy optimization → evaluation gate → deployed policy. Give each stage its input artifact, output artifact, and who touches it, including the loop nobody draws, where the deployed model generates the next round's comparison data.

Add a risk register beside it, one row per risk, four columns: the stage that creates it, the mechanism, one symptom observable from outside, where in the pipeline it could be caught. Your ambiguous-bin count is the register's first evidence entry.

Done when every register row ties its risk to the single stage that introduces it and names one observable symptom, and sycophancy appears as a row, attributed to the stage you argue creates it, with the reward-model amplification step stated explicitly.

Skip if you can already draw the pipeline from data collection to deployed policy from memory, name the artifact each stage produces and the failure mode it introduces, and say what a ~73% inter-annotator agreement rate does to the ceiling of any reward model trained on those labels.

Take away

  • The reward model is a proxy, and optimization is not neutral toward proxy error. A policy trained to score well searches out the reward model's systematic mistakes: verbosity rated as quality, agreement as helpfulness. That amplification sits behind sycophancy and reward hacking (W42).
  • Preference labels are not readouts of human values. A label is one contractor's choice between two completions under written instructions, a rubric, and time pressure. InstructGPT's own training labelers agreed with each other 72.6 ± 1.5% of the time.
  • Every pipeline then decides what to do with the disagreeing tail: majority-vote it away, average over it, or model it. Most make that choice silently.
  • Bradley-Terry fits a sigmoid over a score difference, so a reward model never learns absolute quality. Scores rank completions within a prompt; comparing raw scores across prompts or models is a category error.
  • Every stage here is a data pipeline in W19's sense, except the thing being filtered is human judgment. Chat templates are part of it: the same weights become a different artifact under a different template.
  • Policy: the documentation requirement is whose preferences, collected under what instructions, aggregated by what rule. The EU AI Act's Annex XI data fields ("type and provenance of data and curation methodologies," Article 53(1)(a)) were drafted with pretraining corpora in view; whether they reach preference labels is the open drafting question.
  • No current instrument lets an outside party check whether deployed behavior matches the spec given to annotators. OpenAI's Model Spec discloses the target but not the data. Later reading on aggregation: Stanford's CS329H and Conitzer et al., 2024.
Check yourself
  1. Why doesn't reward-model error wash out as noise when you optimize a policy against it hard?
  2. What can a trained reward model tell you about a single completion in isolation, and what can't it?
  3. A deployed assistant flatters users and over-agrees even when it costs accuracy. Trace sycophancy through your map: which stage creates it, which amplifies it, and why doesn't the evaluation gate catch it?
  4. InstructGPT's training labelers agreed 72.6 ± 1.5% of the time, and the 1.3B InstructGPT beat the 175B GPT-3 in human evaluations. What does the first number do to "ground truth," and what does the second result fail to establish?

Answers. (1) Optimization is a search, not a survey. Random label noise partly cancels, but the RM's systematic errors define directions where score exceeds quality, and gradient pressure moves the policy along them. Goodhart's law with a learning rate. (2) Nothing calibrated. The loss only constrains score differences between completions of the same prompt, so scores carry rankings, not absolute quality. (3) Created at preference collection, where raters systematically prefer agreement and flattery. Amplified at policy optimization, where the RM inherits the bias and the policy searches out what the RM over-rewards. The gate misses it because its judges, human raters or an LLM judge trained on similar preferences, share the bias. (4) On ~27% of comparisons two screened labelers disagree, so ~73% is roughly the ceiling on matching a held-out labeler, and the pipeline picks an aggregation rule, usually silently. The 1.3B result is a preference rate on OpenAI's own API prompt distribution, judged by labelers from the same hiring filter. It shows posttraining moves preference share at 100× less scale. It does not show greater capability, or that preferences from outside that pipeline would agree.

Reinforcement learning foundations

Read

  • Spinning Up in Deep RL (Achiam, OpenAI, spinningup.openai.com) — Parts 1–3: key concepts, the algorithm taxonomy, intro to policy optimization. Effectively frozen since 2020, so the code targets the pre-Gymnasium gym API. Next day, re-derive the simplest policy gradient closed-book.
  • RLHF Book, Ch. 6 "Reinforcement Learning" (Lambert, rlhfbook.com/c/06-policy-gradients) — the role of RL in RLHF, "Policy Gradient Algorithms" through "Value Functions and PPO," and "Double Regularization." Stop before GRPO; W28 picks it up. Source the objective r = r_θ − β·D_KL now.
  • Sutton & Barto, Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction, 2nd ed. (full text) — optional reference. Ch. 3 Finite Markov Decision Processes when the MDP formalism goes by too fast, Ch. 13 Policy Gradient Methods for the theorem with proofs. Reach for it on demand.

Do

Port Spinning Up's simplest policy gradient (1_simple_pg.py, 128 lines of PyTorch) to Gymnasium and train it on CartPole-v1 (+1 per surviving step, truncated at 500). The port is small: step now returns five values with a terminated/truncated split, and reset returns (obs, info). Add reward-to-go, run at least three seeds, log per-episode return, and measure the random-policy baseline rather than assuming it.

Then swap in a subtly different reward that pays +1 only while the cart sits in the right half of the track. Write down the behavior you expect, retrain, and watch what the optimizer finds.

Write it up in one page plus one figure: environment and algorithm named, config and seeds stated, learning curves across seeds with the measured baseline marked, the perturbed-reward result with predicted versus observed behavior, and a closing paragraph connecting proxy error to learned behavior.

Done when the figure shows mean episode return across at least three seeds with per-seed curves or a spread band, the random-policy baseline measured over the same number of episodes, and the final mean clearing that baseline by more than the seed-to-seed spread.

Skip if you can already write the policy-gradient estimator and the RLHF objective r = r_θ − β·D_KL from memory with every symbol named, and say when PPO's clipping does nothing at all.

Take away
