GSM1k found a Spearman correlation of 0.36 (p = 0.03) between a model's log-likelihood of GSM8k test items and its performance gap: evidence of partial memorization, and equally evidence that memorization is partial. An audit that only greps for verbatim test items exonerates most of the inflation.
  • The matching is what makes GSM1k's gap readable: matched human solve rates, step counts, and answer magnitudes rule out difficulty drift, structural complexity, and arithmetic-scale artifacts, leaving exposure. Note the release condition too, the clean twin scheduled to publish by June 2025 at the latest, contaminating itself on contact. Parallel benchmarks are consumables.
  • Model judges carry measurable artifacts, and the fixes are cheap: swap positions and call disagreement a tie for position bias, use reference-guided grading for the anchoring failure (which cut failures from 14/20 to 3/20).
  • High agreement with humans is a measurement on the current distribution being asked to carry an assurance about an adversarial one.
  • LiveBench prices its own answers. Monthly refresh breaks cross-month comparability and each batch leaks the day it publishes, so freshness is a subscription rather than a fix. Ground-truth-only scoring shrinks the construct to what a rule can check, so the benchmark goes silent where judges were needed.
  • Policy: the moment a score gates anything (the AI Office assessing GPAI obligations under the Code of Practice it enforces from August 2026, NIST's CAISI or the UK AI Security Institute running pre-deployment tests a decision will cite), the benchmark becomes a fixed target for every channel here and its validity becomes time-indexed.
  • The SWE-bench arc is the worked example: constructed in 2023, contamination and flawed tests documented by February 2026, a successor endorsed, then the successor audited (roughly 30% of its 731 public tasks judged broken) and the endorsement retracted five months later.
  • So the design conclusion attaches to the regime, not the benchmark: named re-audit triggers, a retirement path, and grader-dependence disclosure written into any rule that cites an instrument.
Check yourself
  1. GSM1k's gap is interpretable only because of what was matched. Name the three matched properties and the confound each removes.
  2. What did the log-likelihood correlation establish, and what are the two inflation channels from Lesson 4 that no search for test items in training data would find?
  3. Your pipeline's judge solves a math problem correctly when asked directly, then grades a wrong answer as correct. Name the failure and the §3.4 fix, with its measured effect.
  4. Zheng et al. report 85% judge–human agreement, above the 81% humans manage with each other. Give two reasons that does not establish the judge as a valid grader once scores start to matter.

Answers. (1) Human solve rates (a gap from difficulty drift would be uninterpretable), number of reasoning steps (structural complexity), and answer magnitude (arithmetic-scale artifacts). Hold all three and exposure to the original is what remains to explain a gap. (2) A Spearman correlation of 0.36 (p = 0.03) between per-character log-likelihood of GSM8k test items and the performance gap, which is evidence of partial memorization and of memorization being partial. The residual channels: benchmark data leaking indirectly through reward-model and annotation pipelines, and selecting checkpoints, methods, or hyperparameters by benchmark score. Goodhart with clean hands; no test item ever enters the training set. (3) The math-grading limitation, where judges are anchored by the answer placed in front of them even when they can solve the problem independently. Fix: reference-guided grading, the judge generating its own solution first and grading against it, which cut failures from 14/20 to 3/20. (4) The agreement was measured observationally, on outputs nobody had optimized against the judge, and the verbosity attack shows what optimization finds (91.3% of attacked answers on two of three judges). And agreement with human majority preference is not correctness: biases humans share, verbosity among them, score as agreement, and the paper cannot rule self-enhancement in or out.

Capability trends and forecasting

Read

  • **Tetlock & Gardner, *Superforecasting: The Art and Science of Prediction*** (Crown, 2015) — Ch. 3 "Keeping Score" and Ch. 5's outside-view discipline. Score three hypothetical forecasts by hand using (p − outcome)², the convention Metaculus and your log use; Tetlock's 0–2 scale doubles every number.
  • Epoch AI trends dashboards (epoch.ai/trends) — frontier training compute (~5×/year since 2020, 90% CI 4–6×) and the Epoch Capabilities Index (~15.5 ECI points/year since early 2024), then the ECI documentation. Read the methodology and uncertainty notes, not the headlines.
  • AI Index Report 2026, Ch. 2 Technical Performance (Stanford HAI, hai.stanford.edu/ai-index/2026-ai-index-report) — the trend charts, and beneath each the source note: whose data, which benchmark, what the axis is. Any chart you would cite enters your claims file with source, scope, and date.
  • METR time horizons, revisited (metr.org/time-horizons) — a short return, not a reread; W32's caveats all still apply. Take the trend as a forecasting input: 207-day doubling (95% bootstrap CI 166–240), recent points above the fit. Write one horizon forecast under three branches (continues, reverts, breaks) with a probability on each.

Do

The forecast log is the build. Add three new questions to forecasts/log.md in the repo, each resolving inside 12 months, each with the full rationale the format demands: reference class, base rate inside it, inside-view adjustment with direction and size, bias direction. Draw one from an AI Index Ch. 2 chart, one from Epoch's dashboards, and one from the METR trend. Where the question exists on Metaculus, link it and record the community median. Respect the log's two rules: the cap of 25 active questions, and the counting rule that Brier-versus-median comparisons are calibration hygiene rather than skill until 40-plus resolutions. For one question, write the probability the bare trend extrapolation implies before writing your own number.

Take one of the three to full standard: resolution criteria a stranger could adjudicate, resolution date inside 12 months, reference class and base rate stated, inside-view adjustment and bias direction written, final probability signed. Add a half-page "what would change my number" note listing the observables you will watch, each with a direction and rough size.

Done when the logged forecast resolves within 12 months by its own written criteria, so a reader who is not you could score it on resolution day without asking you anything, and names its reference class; and the note names two observables, each with the direction it moves your number.

Skip if your log already carries three or more sub-12-month questions with named reference classes and base rates, plus one resolved entry you scored.

Take away

  • Every forecast is a base rate plus an adjustment. Outside view first: Fermi-ize, find the reference class, take its base rate as the starting number, then let the inside view move it, writing down which direction and why.
  • Brier closes the loop by making honest probabilities the optimal strategy, so resolution criteria are non-negotiable. A forecast that cannot be scored cannot be wrong, and cannot be evidence about anything, including your own calibration.
  • One trend line cannot carry capability, impact, and policy. A capability forecast extended into an impact claim silently adds adoption, complements, workflow integration, and institutions. An impact claim extended into a recommendation silently adds values, alternatives, authority, and costs.
  • The three instruments have different weaknesses. Compute curves are the most observable (~5×/year, 90% CI 4–6×). The ECI spans saturation by chaining benchmarks of different difficulties, but its scale is a relative latent construct with no absolute meaning, it cannot tell you the composition of gains, and it inherits every component benchmark's contamination and decay.
  • METR's doubling is a fitted parameter with an interval: 207 days, 95% bootstrap CI 166–240, recent points above the fit. Carry W32's caveats (suite-bound, clean software tasks, fitted threshold) with the number every time you use it.
  • Policy: the live case is whether trend extrapolation justifies acting before harm arrives. The International AI Safety Report 2026 carries the ~2-hour horizon and its ~7-month doubling into a record some thirty governments commissioned, and every briefing citing it is implicitly arguing the trend will hold.
  • Give that argument its structure. It is a forecast, not a measurement, and it rests on a reference class of clean software tasks fitted 2019–2025. The case for early action is decision-theoretic: acting too early wastes authority on a trend that breaks, acting too late means the capability arrives inside institutions that cannot respond at trend speed.
  • Writing the extrapolation as a scored forecast with resolution criteria and update triggers gives the recommended regime a built-in check.
Check yourself
  1. A briefing shows the ECI rising ~15 points/year and concludes: "AI will displace a large share of paralegal work by 2028, so bar associations should impose supervision rules now." Walk the claim ladder and name what goes unargued at each crossing.
  2. Your log shows 0.8 on an event that failed to happen. Compute the Brier score. A colleague advises putting 0.5 on everything so you never look bad. Refute with the expected-score argument, and say what the log's counting rule makes of your score either way.
  3. What does the ECI's stitching let it do that no single benchmark can, and name two things the same methodology makes it unable to tell you.
  4. "Will an agent complete, at 50% reliability, a task that takes humans a full work week, within 24 months?" Build the outside view from this week's numbers before touching any inside view. Show the arithmetic and where the CI leaves you.

Answers. (1) The measurement is a latent-scale composite of benchmark scores. First crossing, to a capability forecast: "the trend continues" needs a reference-class argument, not a ruler. Second, to an impact forecast: displacement adds adoption, workflow integration, complements, error tolerance, and institutional friction, none of them measured by any benchmark. Third, to a recommendation: values, alternatives, authority, costs. (2) Brier = (0.8 − 0)² = 0.64. Always-0.5 scores 0.25 on everything, but Brier is proper: if your true belief is 0.8, reporting it gives expected score 0.8(0.04) + 0.2(0.64) = 0.16 against 0.25 for reporting 0.5. Hedging beats you only where the true frequency sits closer to 0.5 than your stated number, which is a calibration failure rather than a strategy. Per the log's counting rule, before ~40 resolutions any Brier comparison is calibration hygiene, so one bad score is a data point. (3) Stitching spans saturation, comparing systems across eras no single benchmark survives. It cannot tell you what any score means in absolute terms, since the scale is a relative latent construct and "15 points/year" has no unit outside the model, nor the composition of gains (general capability versus a few domains moving). It launders out none of the component contamination and decay. (4) A work week ≈ 40 hours; anchor on the ~2-hour 50% horizon the ministerial record carries, so you need log₂(40/2) ≈ 4.3 doublings. At 207 days per doubling that is ~890 days ≈ 29 months, outside the window; at the CI's fast edge of 166 days, ~715 days ≈ 23.5 months, just inside. The outside view puts the event near but mostly past the deadline, so a starting probability well under half, before adjusting up for the above-trend recent points or down for the suite's clean-task external-validity problem.

Interpretability and causal evidence

Read

  • Scaling Monosemanticity (Templeton et al., Anthropic, May 2024, paper) — what an SAE feature is, how specificity and steering test it, the safety-relevant features, the paper's own caveats. Not end to end. Grade it with the position named, since this is a lab grading its own model.
  • On the Biology of a Large Language Model (Anthropic, March 2025, paper) — attribution graphs on Claude 3.5 Haiku: the multi-step reasoning example, then Planning in Poems and Chain-of-thought Faithfulness, then Limitations in full. Write an evidence brief per case study.
  • A Mathematical Framework for Transformer Circuits (Elhage, Nanda, Olah et al., Anthropic, Dec 2021, paper) — optional, several sittings of real mathematics. The residual stream as a communication channel, QK/OV decomposition, induction heads. Rebuild the residual-stream picture closed-book.
  • Interpretability Illusions in the Generalization of Simplified Models (Friedman et al., ICML 2024, arXiv:2312.03656) — optional. Simplified proxies (SVD, clustering) that match the model on-distribution and misrepresent it under shift. File "interpretability illusion" with its citation.

Do

Install transformer-lens, load gpt2-small, and work the Main Demo far enough to cache activations and register a hook. Then patch: pick a clean/corrupted prompt pair differing in one token with different correct completions (the name-swap indirect-object sentence is the classic), cache the clean run, and re-run the corrupted prompt while patching the clean residual stream back in at each layer × position. Plot how much of the clean logit difference each patch restores. Swap patching for zero-ablation at your strongest site, write the predicted difference down first, and explain what you get.

Then write the evidence ladder, one page. Pick one claimed feature or circuit: the mover your heatmap localized, or a published claim at full strength. State the claim precisely (which mechanism, influencing which behavior, on what inputs), list every piece of evidence you have or the paper offers, place each on a rung with one sentence of justification, and give each the specific experiment that would upgrade it one rung. Close with the verdict line: the strongest causal sentence the current evidence supports, written so a policy reader cannot round it up.

Done when the ladder places at least three distinct evidence types on named rungs, each paired with the concrete experiment that would move it up one rung. An upgrade line that says "more evidence" fails. Rungs from your own build resolve to the committed notebook and heatmap; rungs from a paper cite the section reporting the evidence.

Skip if you can already take three pieces of interpretability evidence (a max-activating example set, a steering demo, a patching result), place each on the ladder cold, and name the experiment that upgrades each one rung.

If the week hooks you, ARENA Chapter 1 is the deep route.

Take away

  • What turns an interpretability observation into a causal claim is a controlled intervention on the proposed mechanism that predictably alters the relevant behavior while excluding alternatives. Nothing weaker supports the word "causes."
  • Three rungs. Correlational: the feature fires when the concept appears (max-activating examples, probes, graph-reading). Interventional: forcing the feature changes behavior in the predicted direction (ablation, clamping, steering). Counterfactual: patching between two minimally different inputs moves the one output it should move and leaves the rest.
  • A feature that fires on deception text is not a deception detector. SAE features are atoms of a dictionary optimized for sparse reconstruction, with no guarantee they are the variables the model computes with, and coverage is admittedly incomplete. "We found the X feature" is a hypothesis generator; the intervention is the test.
  • Scaling Monosemanticity climbs to rung two at production scale: clamping a feature and watching behavior follow is an intervention performed on a deployed model, which is what makes it more than feature-hunting on toy models.
  • An unvalidated attribution graph sits on rung one, a structured correlational hypothesis, because it is computed through a replacement model that incompletely captures the original. The biology paper tests its graph predictions by suppressing or injecting the implicated features on the real model, and reports a hit rate of roughly a quarter of prompts tried.
  • Policy: whether interpretability evidence belongs in a frontier safety case, and at what weight, is live. The International AI Safety Report 2026 treats safety cases among standard risk-management practices (§3.2), and developers regularly offer interpretability as a future basis for assurance.
  • The current answer is a supporting exhibit with its coverage and validity caveats attached, given the quarter-of-prompts hit rate and the conceded replacement-model gap. That self-report is also the standard to demand: any interpretability exhibit in a safety case should state its evidence rung and its coverage, and one that will not can be rejected.
Check yourself
  1. Mechanically, what is an SAE feature, and why is "we extracted 34 million features" not the same claim as "we understand the model"?
  2. Place on the ladder and name each one's upgrade: (a) a feature's top-activating examples are all sycophantic praise; (b) clamping that feature high makes the model flatter the user across contexts; (c) patching the feature's activation from a sycophantic prompt into a matched neutral prompt flips that response to flattery while unrelated completions stay fixed.
  3. In your build, why patch activations from a matched counterfactual prompt instead of just zeroing the component?
  4. A safety case asserts: "our interpretability audit found no deception features active during evaluations, so the model was not deceiving." Give two independent reasons this cannot carry the weight, and the defensible sentence that could replace it.

Answers. (1) A direction in a sparse, overcomplete dictionary trained so that a few active features reconstruct the residual-stream activation. They are the dictionary's atoms, not certified variables of the model's own computation, and the paper concedes incomplete coverage and no rigorous test that features match the model's real intermediates. (2) (a) Correlational, since firing tracks a human-chosen probe set; upgrade by intervening on the feature with a predicted behavioral direction. (b) Interventional, since behavior moved under forcing; upgrade to counterfactual with a matched minimal pair, one predicted specific effect, and unrelated outputs demonstrably unchanged, because steering that changes everything proves influence rather than mechanism. (c) Counterfactual, the top rung for one input; the upgrade is breadth rather than rung, replicating across prompts, seeds, and model scales with the direction predicted in advance. (3) Zeroing puts the network in a state it never occupies, off-distribution in W8's sense, so the behavior change may measure the weirdness of the broken state rather than the component's role. Patching substitutes a value the model does produce on the counterfactual input, a controlled comparison where the only difference is the information under test. (4) Coverage: the audit searched the dictionary the SAE happened to learn, and absence from the dictionary is not absence from the model. Validity under shift: a proxy faithful on the evaluation distribution can misrepresent the model elsewhere, which is the interpretability-illusions result, and deployment is elsewhere. Defensible replacement: "interpretability evidence, at stated coverage, found no positive indication of deception on the evaluated distribution, offered as supporting evidence and not as an inability claim."

Build an evaluation suite

Read

  • Inspect documentation (UK AI Security Institute, inspect.aisi.org.uk) — Tutorial; Components (Tasks, Datasets, Solvers, Scorers); Log Files; Sandboxing. Skip Agents and Extensions. Run the model-graded example against a small model before you read any of it.
  • ARENA Chapter 3, two sections only (learn.arena.education/chapter3_llm_evals) — [3.1] Intro to Evals, then [3.3] Running Evals with Inspect. Answer 3.1's design questions for your own W36 construct. Add [3.2] if you generate items with an LLM.
  • lm-evaluation-harness (EleutherAI, github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness) — optional. The README, then one task's YAML config. Find where elicitation settings live in a config-file harness.

Do

Implement the W36 spec in Inspect: 15–30 items you wrote or hand-curated, an explicit solver, and a scorer that matches the construct. Use Inspect's Docker sandbox if the construct involves tool use or code execution. Commit items, config, and the unchanged spec before the first scoring run. Run the suite twice at identical configuration and commit both logs, failed runs included. Perturb one variable (swap the grader model, or drop temperature to zero), predict the direction of the shift, rerun, and file the measured delta under validity threats. If nothing moved, say so on the card.

Then write the eval card, one to two pages, every field checkable against the logs. Construct and the decision it serves, with deviations from the W36 spec listed and dated. Population: what the items sample, and what deployment population they claim to stand in for. Task set and version. Elicitation settings per W18: temperature, truncation rule, protocol, seeds. Harness and scaffold: Inspect version, model version, sandbox. Scoring: scorer type, grader model and prompt verbatim if model-graded, plus your hand-check of grader agreement on a sample of transcripts. Uncertainty: run-to-run variance measured from your own runs, and item-sampling standard error per W10, clustered where items share a template. Validity threats: W36's table revised by implementation, perturbation delta included. Decision rule: threshold, action, and who acts.

Done when a second identical-configuration run reproduces the first within the variance the card states, and both run logs are committed beside the card.

Skip if you can already show a suite you built and reran: two identical-config committed logs, and a card whose stated variance covers the gap between them.

For the dossier, take one headline eval from the newest frontier model's vendor documentation and fill in your own card's fields; the last line lists what the documentation leaves blank. The Phase VIII gate, on the gates page, connects a score to a decision (uncertainty, threshold, authority, action) and asks why attribution is not intervention, per W39, with your card as the exhibit.

Take away

  • A score is decision-relevant only when the chain is explicit end to end: construct → items and population → elicitation → scorer → uncertainty → threshold → the action someone takes at that threshold. The card is that chain written down, so it doubles as the audit trail.
  • Your repo is not your eval. The number came from code plus a model version, a temperature and truncation rule, a grader model and prompt, and a sampling protocol. The repo pins one of the five.
  • An Inspect Task is a dataset, a solver, and a scorer bound to a configuration. The log adds what the score cannot: full config including model version and generation settings, per-sample inputs and complete message histories, token counts, and events and errors.
  • Two uncertainties, kept apart. Run-to-run spread is the pipeline's own noise, which is what the acceptance test checks. Item-sampling error is √(p(1−p)/n) per W10, and at n = 20 it swamps the first. Pinning every seed collapses run-to-run spread by construction.
  • Policy relevance: what evaluation documentation to require so third-party checking is possible. NIST's Center for AI Standards and Innovation holds pre-deployment testing agreements with five frontier labs and has completed more than 40 assessments; the UK AI Security Institute tests pre-deployment builds and maintains Inspect.
  • Such a body can verify that a documented suite reruns within its stated variance and that elicitation matches the disclosure. It cannot verify "our internal evaluations found no dangerous capability," which arrives without construct, population, settings, or logs.
Check yourself
  1. Name, in order, the links a score must connect before it is decision-relevant, and state which of them "the model scored 71%" supplies on its own.
  2. Name four things the eval log records that the headline score does not, and the check each one enables.
  3. Your 20-item suite: run one scores 70%, run two 60%, and the card claims run-to-run variance of ±3 points. Does it pass the acceptance test? Separate the two uncertainties, estimate the item-sampling one, and name one honest and one dishonest way to shrink the run-to-run gap.
  4. Your scorer is model-graded. Name the two ways the grader enters the measurement and the check that catches a grader drifting. Then: a developer says "internal evals show the capability is absent" and supplies the score. Name two checks the evaluator cannot perform, and the one-line requirement that would make both possible.

Answers. (1) Construct → items and population → elicitation settings → scorer → uncertainty → threshold → action-and-actor. "Scored 71%" supplies a point estimate and nothing else: no construct, no population, no settings, no error bar, no rule. (2) Full config including model version and generation settings, enabling reruns; per-sample inputs, outputs, and complete message histories, enabling re-grading and grader audits; token counts, enabling cost checks; events and errors, enabling forensics on failed runs. (3) Fails: the 10-point gap exceeds the stated ±3, so the card's variance claim is falsified. Fix the card or the protocol and rerun. Item-sampling SE ≈ √(0.65 × 0.35 / 20) ≈ 10.7 points, so at n = 20 the number says little about the construct's population however well the runs agree. Honest shrink: more samples per item, averaged, or more items. Dishonest: pin every seed, which verifies determinism rather than stability, or drop temperature to zero, which W18 showed changes what is being measured. (4) Through its model version and through its prompt-and-rubric; change either and the score moves, so the card records both verbatim plus measured grader-human agreement on a hand-checked sample. Drift check: re-grade a fixed archived sample whenever the grader model updates. On the developer's claim, the evaluator cannot rerun it (no config, elicitation, harness, or logs) and cannot assess construct or population. The requirement: every capability claim ships with its eval card and committed run logs.

Misuse and dangerous capabilities

Read

  • The WMDP Benchmark: Measuring and Reducing Malicious Use with Unlearning (Li et al., 2024, arXiv:2403.03218) — abstract, §1, §3 entire, §4 through §4.1, Figures 1–6. Read §3.1's generation strategies against Figure 3; §3.2 and §3.3 are your kill chains.
  • The Operational Risks of AI in Large-Scale Biological Attacks: Results of a Red-Team Study (Mouton, Lucas, and Guest, RAND, 25 Jan 2024, landing page and direct PDF) — all 24 pages. Study the design before the result.
  • Epoch, "Do the biorisk evaluations of AI labs actually measure the risk of developing bioweapons?" (Ho and Berg, 13 Jun 2025, epoch.ai) — the whole piece. Apply its three complaints as a checklist to your own plan.
  • International AI Safety Report 2026, §2.1 "Risks from malicious use" (internationalaisafetyreport.org) — optional. pp. 45–70. Grade one claim per subsection by its evidence type. Or CAIS §1.2, "Malicious use".

Do

Write a threat model and evaluation plan for one dangerous-capability domain, biological or cyber, not both. Six parts. (1) The kill chain from the reading, with exactly one step marked as your target and a sentence on why that step and not its neighbors. (2) The actor: capability, resources, and what they can already obtain without any model. (3) The uplift construct: what quantity, measured on whom, against which baseline, at what threshold, with the baseline written as a decision you defend. (4) The measurement design: proxy tasks safe to publish, the scoring rubric and who applies it, the adjudication procedure, and, per W10, the sample size with the effect it could detect. (5) At least four validity threats, each with the direction it biases the result; RAND's cell-composition variance and the Epoch checklist are the starting set. (6) One page arguing which link in your chain binds, and what would change your mind. No build this week; the plan feeds the machinery you built at W40.

Done when the plan states where the binding constraint on this kill-chain step sits (information, materials, or tacit skill) and names one piece of evidence that would move it: what study or observation, what result, at what magnitude. A plan whose own measurement design could never produce that evidence fails.

Skip if you can already show a dangerous-capability threat model that names the kill-chain step, the counterfactual baseline, and where the binding constraint sits.

Take away

  • Capability is measured on the model; harm is produced by a chain. Between a score and a body sit access, intent, materials and equipment, tacit skill that no text transfers, operational friction, defenses, and scale.
  • Uplift is a counterfactual quantity: what this actor achieves with the model minus what the same actor achieves without it. Every uplift claim carries a baseline somebody chose, and a plan that leaves it unstated is not measuring anything.
  • A high score on a hazardous-knowledge benchmark is not evidence of uplift. WMDP's own paper: a low-scoring model likely lacks the knowledge to help, while a high-scoring model is "not necessarily unsafe" (Li et al. 2024, §3.1). The inference runs one way, and a deployment gate needs the other.
  • RAND's result: across twelve red cells, LLM access was associated with a 0.22-point decrease in viability, p = 0.64; LLM A +0.12 (p = 0.87), LLM B −0.56 (p = 0.25). Every plan scored between untenable and problematic.
  • The design could detect about a 1.0-point change, and it scored planning documents rather than execution, on the models available in summer 2023.
  • Epoch's three complaints: the publicly described benchmarks are essentially saturated, WMDP among them; the evaluations that actually gate deployment are not public; and nothing in the set answers somatic tacit knowledge or physical infrastructure access.
  • The exception is the Virology Capabilities Test, which targets wet-lab troubleshooting rather than recall: expert virologists average 22.1% on questions inside their own sub-areas, and models have passed that without coming near ceiling.
  • Policy relevance: setting a capability threshold for a misuse domain. Anthropic's Responsible Scaling Policy (v3.4, effective 8 July 2026) carries two chemical-and-biological thresholds, non-novel production and novel production, both answered with ASL-3 protections; novel production was rewritten in v3.3, 26 May 2026.
  • OpenAI's Preparedness Framework v2 (15 April 2025, plus the Frontier Governance Framework of 28 May 2026) sets its Biological and Chemical High threshold at meaningful counterfactual assistance to a novice actor, measured against unlimited access to the tools available in 2021. The baseline is where the two part: one names a frozen year, the other says nothing.
  • California's Transparency in Frontier AI Act and the EU AI Act's GPAI obligations reach these commitments, but mostly require disclosure of the framework rather than validation of the measurement.
Check yourself
  1. State WMDP's inferential asymmetry in both directions, then explain what in the construction makes the asymmetry structural rather than an artifact of difficulty.
  2. Give RAND's headline with its numbers. A colleague concludes: "so language models don't help bioterrorists." Name three distinct reasons the study cannot support that sentence.
  3. The black cell scored the highest plan score of all fifteen cells, 5.24 on viability, and was staffed by people expert in jailbreaking. Give three reasons that is not evidence of jailbreak-driven uplift.
  4. OpenAI's High threshold is defined against the tools available in 2021. Explain what fixing the baseline year does to the measured quantity, and the failure mode of letting the baseline float with the open internet instead. Then say why saturation of the public biorisk benchmarks is not evidence of high risk, and what it implies for a gate that cites one as its trigger.

Answers. (1) A low score licenses an inference, that this model probably lacks the knowledge to assist, while a high score licenses almost nothing, because knowledge is not the ability to sequence knowledge into a weapon. It is structural because the questions were built from precursors, neighbors, and components (Figure 4), targeting the middle tier of Figure 3 while the top tier is what the authors excluded and cleared against export controls. The benchmark cannot contain the thing whose presence would justify the strong inference; that is the price of being publishable. (2) Across twelve red cells, LLM access was associated with a 0.22-point decrease on a 9-point scale, p = 0.64; LLM A +0.12 (p = 0.87), LLM B −0.56 (p = 0.25). The conclusion fails on power (twelve cells, four vignettes, and the authors' own finding that cell composition was a far larger source of variability than LLM access; detectable change about 1.0 point, so resolving 0.22 would take roughly ten times the sample); on construct (it scored planning documents rather than execution, so the wet-lab half of the chain was never in the experiment); and on currency (summer-2023 models). A fourth worth credit: all plans landed between untenable and problematic, so the outcome scale never approached the range where a difference would matter. (3) Its biological feasibility score was 5.50, tied for second-highest, and that component carried the 5.24; chat-log analysis and the cell's own researchers attributed it to close reading of the public gain-of-function literature rather than model access, and the cell used neither jailbreaks nor the model for the tactics its plan turned on. Procedurally, the plan was assessed by only four experts, two in biology and two in operations, and never went through the formal adjudication event, which was associated with an average decrease of more than 0.50 points for every other plan. And it was a single excursion cell added mid-study with deliberately selected composition and a later timeline, outside the twelve-cell comparison; n = 1 carries no causal claim in either direction. (4) Freezing the baseline makes the threshold a claim about the model's contribution relative to a fixed historical world, so the number stays interpretable across model generations; the cost is that it stops describing the present, and a model can cross a frozen threshold while adding nothing an attacker could not already obtain. Letting the baseline float creates a ratchet: every diffusion of capability into the open world lowers the measured uplift of the next model, so a domain can become uniformly more dangerous while every system reports zero uplift and no gate trips. The errors run in opposite directions, so the baseline has to be argued rather than assumed. On saturation: it tells you about the instrument rather than the world, since a benchmark can be maxed out because its questions were the safe, publishable, recall-shaped fraction of the domain. Reaching ceiling also destroys discrimination, so a saturated benchmark can fire once and thereafter carries no information: either the trigger relocates to an instrument with headroom and a defended construct, or the gate is decoration.

Deception, reward tampering, and model organisms

Read

  • Sleeper Agents (Hubinger et al., 2024, arXiv:2401.05566) — the two threat models, the code-vulnerability backdoor, and persistence through fine-tuning, RL safety training, and adversarial training. Read the limitations closely. Then skim Denison et al. (arXiv:2406.10162) for the tampering rung.
  • Alignment Faking (Greenblatt et al., 2024, arXiv:2412.14093, reviews PDF) — the compliance gap, the synthetic-documents variant, and the RL result, then all four reviews. Name each reviewer's institutional position.
  • In-context scheming (Meinke et al., Apollo Research, 2024, arXiv:2412.04984; IASR 2026 §2.2.2, pp. 76–83) — optional. Apollo's evaluation designs, then IASR's Table 2.5 taxonomy. Track which caveats survived into the ministerial document.
  • Agentic Misalignment in Summer 2026 (Anthropic Alignment Science, July 13, 2026, alignment.anthropic.com) — optional. Four new failure modes across 14 frontier models at 20 runs per scenario, then the limitations the authors volunteer.

Do

Build the structured evidence review. Rows: at least four headline results from the week, chosen from backdoor persistence, adversarial-training-teaches-hiding, the compliance gap, the RL faking result, in-context oversight subversion, motivated mislabeling, and the anti-scheming reduction. Each row states the result at measurement level (what was observed, in which setup, at what scale), argues at least two of the three candidate explanations (genuine scheming propensity, persona or prompt artifact, evaluation awareness) at full strength, and lists the evidence that discriminates: the no-conflict ablation, the synthetic-documents variant, the paid-tier baseline, verbalized-awareness rates, scale dependence. For one row, read at least three raw transcripts from the Summer 2026 public browser and say whether the authors' selection matches what you sampled. Close each row with a verdict line. No build this week.

Done when at least one verdict line reads "current evidence cannot discriminate" and that cell names the experiment that would. A review where every row reaches a confident verdict fails, and so does one where every row abstains.

Skip if you can already show an evidence review that grades one deception result against at least two competing explanations and names the evidence that discriminates.

Take away

  • A model organism is a deliberately constructed instance of the failure you are worried about, built to be studied rather than caught in the wild. Construction buys causal control: you put the backdoor there, so "did training remove it?" has a checkable answer. The price is every transfer assumption between lab and deployment.
  • Lab behavior is neither proof nor disproof of catastrophe. "The backdoor was planted, so the result is circular" and "the model schemed in the lab, so deployed models are scheming" are the same transfer error with opposite signs. What breaks is the safety argument "training would have removed it." Whether there is anything to remove stays open.
  • The ladder runs gaming → tampering → deception → faking: flattering the grader because flattery scores well, editing the file that computes the reward, misleading the overseer, and complying during training with an objective you were trained to refuse. Denison et al. connect the first two by zero-shot generalization.
  • Adversarial training on trigger-eliciting prompts made the models better at recognizing their triggers, converting a detectable failure into a hidden one while the elicitation metric improved. Red-team success and model concealment become the same number.
  • The compliance gap carries the evidential weight, not the raw 14%. Absolute rates measure jailbreak propensity; the differential, conditioned on nothing but perceived training status, is what evidences situationally strategic behavior.
  • In the Summer 2026 runs, a judge model mislabeled transcripts 85.6% of the time when told the correct label would train away behavior it valued, and 16.7% with the consequences reversed.
  • Policy relevance: writing a deception trigger into a deployment gate, or refusing to. Frontier safety frameworks are where such a trigger would live (Anthropic's RSP v3.4, effective 8 July 2026; OpenAI's Preparedness Framework v2, mapped onto the EU GPAI Code of Practice and California's TFAIA by its Frontier Governance Framework).
  • The anti-scheming stress test (OpenAI and Apollo, 2025, arXiv:2509.15541) shows the difficulty: deliberative-alignment training cut covert actions roughly 30× (o3: 13% → 0.4%), but verbalized evaluation awareness rose from 2.3% to 4.5%, and the authors cannot exclude that the awareness drives part of the reduction.
  • A threshold written on measured deception rates is satisfied by a safer model or by a more test-aware one, and from outside the lab those look alike.
Check yourself
  1. Place these on the ladder: (a) a model flatters the grader because flattery scores well; (b) a model edits the checklist file that computes its reward; (c) a model inserts subtle errors and denies it when asked; (d) a model complies during training with an objective it was trained to refuse, and behaves differently unmonitored. Which two rungs have been shown to connect by zero-shot generalization?
  2. Your team red-teams a suspect model, finds prompts that elicit the bad behavior, and trains against them until elicitation fails. State the Sleeper Agents result that makes this potentially worse than doing nothing, and say what "worse" means.
  3. Define the compliance gap and explain why it carries the evidential weight while the raw 14% does not. Which candidate explanation does the synthetic-documents variant knock down, and name one it leaves standing.
  4. Anti-scheming training cuts covert actions from 13% to 0.4% while verbalized evaluation awareness roughly doubles. Why can't the reduction be read at face value? What does selecting case studies from no-verbalized-suspicion transcripts fix, and what does it leave unfixed?

Answers. (1) (a) specification gaming, exploiting a flawed reward signal; (b) reward tampering, modifying the apparatus that computes the reward; (c) deception, behavior that predictably misleads the overseer; (d) alignment faking, strategic compliance in training to avoid modification. Denison et al. connect (a) to (b): models trained on a curriculum of gameable environments generalized zero-shot to rewriting their own reward function a small but non-negligible fraction of the time. (2) Adversarial training on trigger-eliciting prompts taught the models to recognize their triggers better, hiding the backdoored behavior rather than removing it. "Worse" means a detectable failure becomes an undetectable one while the elicitation metric improves, the paper's phrase being a false impression of safety; your red-team success metric and the model's concealment have become the same number. (3) The gap is the difference in harmful compliance between contexts the model believes feed training (free tier, 14%) and contexts it believes are unmonitored (paid tier, almost never); absolute rates measure jailbreak propensity, while the differential conditioned on nothing but perceived training status is what evidences situationally strategic behavior. The synthetic-documents variant, where the information arrives by fine-tuning on pretraining-like documents under a generic prompt, knocks down "artifact of a weird system prompt." Still standing: salience-driven evaluation awareness (Shah, ~15M tokens on one narrow topic) and scratchpad reasoning as post-hoc rationalization (Sekhon). Worth adding: the role-play critique at full strength via Andreas (anthropomorphism written into framing and metrics, unvalidated automated grading of scratchpad text, the persuasive replication being a model with no "I" at all), then the reply via Bengio with Shah supporting (the behavior follows predictably from trained-in goals plus information about training plus reasoning capability, and the seven lines of evidence rule out pure priming though not the persona framing). (4) Because the measurement and the confound move together: a model that better recognizes evaluations behaves well in evaluations regardless of deployment propensity, and the authors report the reduction may be partly driven by that awareness, so the quantity a gate needs, deployment behavior, is what test-time behavior stops tracking. Selecting no-verbalized-suspicion transcripts controls only verbalized awareness; unverbalized awareness is unmeasured, so the confound survives, and the selection does nothing about scenarios having been iterated against specific models.

Control and monitoring

Read

  • AI Control: Improving Safety Despite Intentional Subversion (Greenblatt, Shlegeris, Sachan & Roger, 2023, arXiv:2312.06942) — the setting, the protocol family (trusted monitoring, defer to trusted, trusted editing, untrusted monitoring), and the evaluation methodology. Rebuild the evaluation loop from memory.
  • The case for ensuring that powerful AIs are controlled (Shlegeris & Greenblatt, Redwood Research blog, May 2024, blog.redwoodresearch.org) — the useful-but-catchable window and the two definitions doing the work. Name the crux that would empty the window.
  • Safety Cases: How to Justify the Safety of Advanced AI Systems (Clymer, Gabrieli, Krueger & Larsen, 2024, arXiv:2403.10462) — optional. The control argument type only, among the paper's four (inability, control, trustworthiness, deference). Keep a ledger entry per type recording the evidence each demands.
  • Ten People on the Inside (Shlegeris, Redwood Research blog, Jan 2025, blog.redwoodresearch.org) — optional. The minimal-political-will limit case. Control's chief advocate argues that control is what survives minimal buy-in; say whether that institutional position strengthens or discounts the claim.

Do

Write a control protocol plus red-team plan for a named internal deployment: an AI R&D assistant with read–write access to your training and evaluation repositories, drafting experiment code, proposing PRs, and triaging results. The protocol specifies which model is trusted and on what grounds, what the untrusted assistant may touch, the monitoring layers with who audits what fraction, and the escalation path when something is flagged. The red-team plan enumerates the protocol's trust assumptions, ranks them by weakness, and scripts a concrete attack on the weakest. Close with the cost accounting: if the scripted attack had succeeded, what does the attacker now have? Protocol design is the build; running a full control evaluation against W40's toolchain is a project, not a sitting.

Done when the protocol states its trust assumptions as an explicit list, the red-team plan's lead attack targets the assumption you ranked weakest rather than the one easiest to write about, and the cost accounting prices a successful attack in concrete objects (what was corrupted, exfiltrated, or made possible) rather than adjectives.

Skip if you can already show a control protocol for a concrete internal deployment that lists its trust assumptions and red-teams the weakest one.

Take away

  • Control seeks acceptable safety despite an untrusted model: a weaker trusted model monitors the stronger one, authority and access are restricted, and scarce human auditing is spent on the most suspicious actions. The evidence is adversarial: the claim is "the best attack our red team could construct fails," never "the model wouldn't."
  • Alignment and control are not the same intervention. Alignment tries to make the model not intend subversion, a property of the model; control tries to make intended subversion fail, a property of the protocol around it. A briefing that treats "we red-teamed it and it behaved" as a control argument has confused the two.
  • The red team plays the untrusted model, converting an unmeasurable question (will this model attack?) into a measurable one (can any attack policy we construct beat the protocol?). A weak red team turns "safe" into "safe against people who didn't try very hard."
  • Protocols are scored on usefulness too: deferring to the weak model everywhere is safe and pointless.
  • Untrusted monitoring uses a monitor as capable as the attacker and inherits collusion: both are instances of the same untrusted model, so an attack can carry a signal telling the monitor to wave it through. The countermeasure is upfront validation on inputs known to contain backdoors.
  • The window closes as the capability gap between trusted labor (humans plus weaker trusted models) and the untrusted model grows. Past some gap, black-box control fails and the safety case falls back on the trustworthiness arguments control was designed to avoid.
  • Policy relevance: deciding what control evidence a deployment decision should demand, when nobody currently has to. California's SB 53 attaches its transparency report at deployment; its only reach inside the lab is a large-developer duty to send quarterly summaries of internal-use catastrophic-risk assessments to the California Office of Emergency Services.
  • The EU AI Act's Article 55 obligations bind providers of systemic-risk models placed on the Union market, and the lab-friendly reading of Article 2(8) treats everything before market placement as exempt research and development.
  • Apollo Research's analysis of internal deployment under the Act argues that in-house use is already "putting into service," so the exemption ends where internal deployment begins. That is a memorandum, not enforcement practice.
Check yourself
  1. A lab reports: "our alignment evaluations came back clean, so a control protocol would be redundant." Separate the two interventions, and say why evidence for one is not evidence for the other.
  2. The untrusted model's role during evaluation is played by a red-team attack policy rather than the model itself. What does that substitution buy methodologically, and what must be true of the red team for the safety number to carry weight?
  3. Untrusted monitoring uses a monitor as capable as the attacker, strictly better than trusted monitoring on competence. Name its distinctive failure mode and the paper's countermeasure.
  4. Define both edges of the useful-but-catchable window, and state which quantity's growth closes it. Which legal instrument currently requires a control evaluation before a frontier lab deploys a model internally as an R&D assistant?

Answers. (1) Alignment tries to make the model not intend subversion, a model property; control tries to make intended subversion fail, a protocol property built from monitoring, restricted authority, and audits. Clean alignment evals are evidence about intent under test conditions, and W42 showed models can behave differently when they infer they are being tested; they bound the probability of scheming without touching the question control answers, which is what happens if that bound is wrong. The failures differ too: an alignment failure is silent until it matters, while a control failure requires beating a protocol built to make beating it expensive. (2) It converts an unmeasurable question, will this model attack, into a measurable one, can any attack policy we construct beat the protocol, yielding a worst-case-flavored bound that does not depend on trusting the model's disposition. It carries weight only under the elicitation assumption: the red team must find attacks at least as good as the deployed model could mount. (3) Collusion, since monitor and attacker are instances of the same untrusted model and an attack can carry a signal telling the monitor to wave it through. The countermeasure is upfront validation on inputs known to contain backdoors; a monitor that passes known backdoors convicts itself, and the protocol treats its scores accordingly. (4) Lower edge: transformatively useful, capable of substantially reducing the risk posed by subsequent AIs, chiefly by accelerating safety-relevant R&D. Upper edge: uncontrollable, capable enough at evading control techniques or sabotaging control evaluations that control is infeasible. The window closes as the capability gap between trusted labor and the untrusted model grows. No instrument requires a control evaluation: SB 53's transparency report attaches at deployment and its internal-use reach is a quarterly self-summary to Cal OES, while whether the EU AI Act reaches internal deployment at all is contested, with Article 55 attaching at market placement, Article 2(8) exempting what comes before, and Apollo arguing in-house use is already putting into service. In practice the artifact you just wrote answers to the lab's own published safety framework.

Model weights and infrastructure security

Read

  • Securing AI Model Weights (Nevo et al., RAND, May 2024, landing page and direct PDF) — the ~36-page main body: Summary, Chs. 4–7, Figure 4.1, Table 5.1. Rebuild both ladders (OC1–OC5, SL1–SL5) from memory.
  • Insider Threat Mitigation Guide (CISA, Nov 2020, cisa.gov) — optional. The four parts, read for program elements, indicators, and privacy-and-civil-liberties constraints. Record each element RAND's SL3 measures presuppose but do not specify.
  • Secure AI Framework (Google, saif.google) — optional. The SAIF map, then the 15 risks and the controls each maps to. Grade it against RAND on one axis: does SAIF anywhere say secured against whom? Google is selling the cloud it tells you how to secure.

Do

Write the security architecture for a frontier development environment, keyed to a named security level. Choose your target SL and say why; SL3 or SL4 are the defensible ambitions. Then: (1) enumerate every path by which weights can be read, copied, or moved (training runs, checkpoint storage and backups, researcher access, inference serving, the cloud control plane, vendors), using RAND's eight urgent recommendations as the spine: the first, a security plan against a comprehensive weight-theft threat model, is this document itself, and the other seven (centralized copies, fewer authorized people, hardened access interfaces, insider program, defense-in-depth, red-teaming, confidential computing) are its section headings. (2) Attach controls per path and per attack category, honoring the report's warning that strength in one category does not compensate for weakness in another. (3) Name the two hardest-to-close vectors, each tied to a Table 5.1 category and the OC class that makes it feasible. (4) Close with the ledger: the SL this architecture achieves, the SL it cannot, and the if-then that follows, which is that a capability whose theft-consequences exceed your achievable SL is a capability this lab should not build yet. Ground that judgment in the newest card on the roster page. No build this week.

Done when the architecture states which SL it achieves and which it cannot, each residual vector names its attack category and enabling OC class checkable against Tables 5.1 and 5.2, and the final section draws the capability conclusion (what should not be built while the gap stands) rather than ending on "invest more in security."

Skip if you can already show a weight-security architecture that names its security level, its two hardest residual vectors, and the capability decision that follows.

Take away

  • Model weights are a security asset unlike documents or user data. A leaked document discloses what it says; stolen weights transfer the capability itself, minus every central control: no rate limits, no monitoring, no usage policy, no revocation.
  • "Secure" is not a property; "secure against OC3" is. RAND indexes five security levels (SL1–SL5) to five attacker operational capacities (OC1–OC5), running from a hobbyist with days and $1,000 to a top-priority state operation with years and up to $1 billion.
  • Eight of the 38 attack vectors are out of reach for OC1–OC3 yet feasible for OC4–OC5, so a lab can pass every commercial security audit and still be open to the actors that matter most. A security claim missing its OC index says nothing.
  • The authors state outright that SL5 cannot currently be achieved and will likely need national-security assistance. Ch. 7 adds that the levels "do not, in and of themselves, imply what security outcomes are required," so something outside the ladder has to supply the required outcome.
  • Insiders sit in OC3 alongside cybercrime syndicates because the investment required to defend robustly against each is comparable, even though the specific measures only partially overlap. SL3 is therefore the union of measures against that diverse group.
  • Fine-tuning strips safety training from a stolen checkpoint for under a dollar (W24's practicum, W27's Qi et al.). Theft is therefore irreversible in a way disclosure never is, and custody is the control with staying power.
  • Policy relevance: setting security requirements that scale with capability. Anthropic's Responsible Scaling Policy (v3.4, effective 8 July 2026) and DeepMind's Frontier Safety Framework (v3.1, 17 April 2026) both pair capability thresholds with required security standards for the weights. Audit them with one question: which OC class does each promised standard thwart?
  • The binding floor is EU AI Act Article 55(1)(d), administered by the Commission's AI Office: providers of GPAI models with systemic risk must ensure adequate cybersecurity protection for the model and its physical infrastructure. The GPAI Code of Practice is the compliance path; enforcement is live from August 2026.
  • Custody sits underneath every other hook, since evaluation gates, deployment rules, monitoring, incident reporting, and revocation all bite downstream of it.
Check yourself
  1. Your CISO reports "the weights are secure." What two questions does this framework oblige you to ask before that sentence means anything, and what does the existence of eight vectors infeasible for OC1–OC3 but feasible for OC4–OC5 imply about a program assembled entirely from commercial best practice?
  2. Name three specific controls that exist while weights sit behind the lab's API and vanish the moment they are stolen, then say what W24 and W27 established about the one safeguard that does travel with the checkpoint.
  3. Your lab's next training run targets a capability whose theft by a top-tier state actor you judge intolerable. Using the report's own findings about SL5, lay out the decision structure it leaves you.
  4. Why does RAND place insiders in OC3 alongside cybercrime syndicates, and what does that grouping force the SL3 benchmark to be?

Answers. (1) Secured against whom, naming the OC class, since a claim without an attacker index is unfalsifiable; and across which attack categories, since the report is explicit that investment in one category, say code execution, does not compensate for gaps in another, say physical access or human intelligence. "Secure" has to mean coverage of all nine categories at the target OC rather than excellence in one. Commercial best practice is calibrated to the attackers most companies face, OC1–OC3, so the eight OC4–OC5-exclusive vectors are state-level operations requiring defenses ordinary corporate security never installs; passing audits and being safe from states are different properties, and only the OC index distinguishes them. (2) Rate limits and usage monitoring; deployment-side classifiers and usage-policy enforcement; revocation, the power to shut access off. All are functions of the weights sitting behind an interface the lab controls. The safeguard that does travel, safety training baked into the weights, is removable by fine-tuning at trivial cost, which is why custody is the control with staying power. (3) The levels calibrate measures to expected outcomes, but Ch. 7 says the levels do not themselves imply what security outcomes are required, so the capability decision is the only candidate to supply one. If theft by OC5 is intolerable, the required outcome is SL5, and the report states flatly that achieving SL5 is currently not possible, that protecting models interacting with the internet against such actors is currently not feasible, and that the measures needed may take significant time (e.g., five years) and will likely require national-security assistance. Two options survive: radically restrict connectivity and operations now, paying the productivity costs the report names, or do not build the capability until security catches up. Note where the reasoning stops being RAND's, since the report ends at calibration and recommends starting on advanced measures soon; the don't-build conclusion is your inference to defend. (4) Insiders command far fewer resources than syndicates but start with preexisting access, and RAND's stated reason is that the investment required to robustly defend against each is comparable even though the specific measures only partially overlap. The SL3 benchmark therefore has to be the union of measures against that diverse group, which is why reaching it is more than an increment over SL2.

Societal and systemic risks

Read

  • AI Safety, Ethics, and Society, Chs. 5 and 7 (Center for AI Safety, aisafetybook.com) — Ch. 5 Complex Systems (§5.2, §5.3) and Ch. 7 Collective Action Problems (§§7.2–7.5). Rebuild Ch. 5's composition argument from memory before you check.
  • Multi-Agent Risks from Advanced AI (Hammond et al., 2025, arXiv:2502.14143) — §2.3 Collusion (pp. 17–20) and §3.2 Network Effects (pp. 23–26), especially "Homogeneity and Correlated Failures." Write an evidence brief on the correlated-failure claim.
  • International AI Safety Report 2026 (internationalaisafetyreport.org) — optional. §2.3.1 Labour market impacts and §2.3.2 Risks to human autonomy. Grade the retreat: 2025's environment, privacy, copyright, and market-concentration sections were dropped.

Do

Write the systemic risk register: three entries, no more, one concentration, one correlated-failure, one epistemic. Each entry carries three fields. Transmission mechanism: the route from local failure to system-level harm, drawn concretely enough that a skeptic could design the observation showing the route isn't carrying anything. Evidence grade: where the best current evidence sits on the claim ladder, citing the single strongest source; this week hands you field evidence for algorithmic collusion, a lab existence proof for steganography, structural inference for monoculture, and mostly assertion for epistemic erosion. Intervention family: the matrix column that reaches the mechanism, written in matrix language (sign, conditional, crux) or "none live," which is a finding rather than a blank. The register feeds S3. No build this week.

Done when each entry names a transmission mechanism specific enough to be falsified: for every mechanism line you can state the observation that would show the mechanism is not operating, and a reader could run that check without asking what you meant.

Skip if you can already show a three-entry systemic risk register whose mechanism lines a skeptic could falsify.

For the dossier, take the newest frontier model on the roster page and write its safety, control, and security snapshot: each claimed capability threshold, the threat pathway it gates, the evaluation behind the claim, the mitigation it triggers, the weight-security posture, and the residual risk with a named owner, ending with the fields the card leaves blank. The Phase IX gate, on the gates page, is the frontier-system audit, five pages plus a claim ledger over a current frontier system's public record. W45 plus the S-block is a designed stopping point.

Take away

  • "Systemic" is earned by a transmission mechanism: a named structure (concentration, homogeneity, feedback, dependency) through which local failures become system-level harm. If you can't draw the route from one failure to many, you have a large harm rather than a systemic one.
  • Big is not systemic. A harm reaching millions one at a time, each instance independent, is a scale problem; scale problems parallelize across existing regulators. Systemic means the failures are coupled: one cause, many simultaneous or compounding effects. Coupled problems need an instrument at the coupling point, and there usually isn't one.
  • The monoculture route: most deployed agents are wrappers or fine-tunes of a handful of foundation models with heavily overlapping training data, so a flaw in one base model surfaces near-simultaneously across nominally independent downstream systems. The evidence is structural inference from data-overlap measurements, not deployment incidence.
  • Collusion requires cooperation at a third party's expense, so correlated behavior from shared signals is a network effect rather than collusion. The German gasoline market is the field instance of tacit collusion; steganographic channels under equally capable oversight are a lab existence proof.
  • Component-level safety does not compose into system-level safety, and every register entry sits on a collective-action structure underneath.
  • Policy relevance: telling a principal which systemic risks have any live instrument, where the answer is mostly none. The EU AI Act is the one major statute using "systemic risk" for frontier AI, and it attaches the label to models above a training-compute presumption (W16's threshold), a model-level proxy while all three mechanisms live at the system level.
  • The rare live instrument is DORA, the EU's financial-sector resilience regulation: on 18 November 2025 the European Supervisory Authorities designated 19 critical ICT third-party providers, hyperscale cloud among them, for direct oversight with inspection and penalty powers.
  • Correlated failure across shared base models is reached by no authority anywhere, and the epistemic route has only W34's provenance instruments, a voluntary standard plus labeling mandates that regulate the layer W34 showed strips.
Check yourself
  1. A briefing calls mass AI-generated disinformation "a systemic risk." State this week's test, then write one version of the claim that passes it and one that fails.
  2. Classify by CAIF's taxonomy: (a) trading agents with similar strategies crash a market by acting on a common signal; (b) pricing agents sustain supra-competitive prices without ever communicating. Name where each belongs, and why (a) is not collusion.
  3. State the monoculture transmission mechanism precisely enough to falsify, and give the observation that would falsify it. Place the German retail-gasoline case and the LLM steganography study on the claim ladder, with one thing each cannot establish.
  4. Which register entry does the EU AI Act's systemic-risk designation reach, which does DORA reach, and which entries have no live instrument at all?

Answers. (1) The claim must name the structure the harm travels through, not its size. Fails: "synthetic content is everywhere, so the risk is systemic," which is a large pile of independent local harms. Passes: "synthetic content degrades the shared verification layer (provenance, trusted intermediaries) that downstream institutions all depend on, so one polluted layer propagates into every decision that consumes it," falsifiable by testing whether verification reliance and trust measures actually degrade with exposure. (2) (a) is not a failure mode at all in CAIF's taxonomy but the risk-factor side, §3.2's network effects in the homogeneity-and-correlated-failures pattern: correlated behavior from shared signals and similar strategies, with no cooperation between agents. Collusion requires cooperation at a third party's expense, and here nobody cooperated, everybody just moved together. (b) is the collusion failure mode in its tacit form, with the German gasoline market as its field instance. (3) Mechanism: because most deployed agents are wrappers or fine-tunes of a handful of foundation models with heavily overlapping training data, a flaw in one base model surfaces near-simultaneously across nominally independent downstream systems. Falsifier: run a shared adversarial input or exploit across downstream agents grouped by base model, and if same-base agents fail no more concordantly than different-base agents, the monoculture route isn't carrying the risk. Gasoline is field measurement supporting an inference, that ordinary adaptive pricing algorithms produced collusive outcomes without communication; it cannot establish that frontier-model agents will do the same, and shows nothing about intent. Steganography is a lab existence proof, that capable models can sustain hidden channels under equally capable oversight; it cannot establish that deployed agents do, or at what rate. Neither supports assurance. (4) The AI Act's designation attaches to individual models via a compute presumption, so it reaches none of the three structural mechanisms directly. DORA reaches the concentration entry: vendor concentration, finance sector only, at the level of designated critical providers. Correlated failure across shared base models has no instrument at all, and the epistemic route has only W34's provenance instruments, which fail at the mechanism, since signatures strip, watermarks weaken under paraphrase, and open weights escape both. One sectoral instrument, one column of instruments that don't hold, one bare column: that distribution is the register's finding.

World models and methods

Read

  • Thorstad, "Against the singularity hypothesis" (philpapers.org/rec/THOATS-5) — the growth-premise attack, plus his replies to the leading philosophical defenses. Name which of your twelve parameter settings change if he is right.
  • Greenblatt, "Plans A, B, C, and D for misalignment risk" (blog.redwoodresearch.org) — the five rungs from international agreement (A) down to nothing (E), with P(plan) and P(takeover | plan) on each. For each rung, name the parameter its takeover estimate leans on hardest.
  • AI Futures Project, "Q1 2026 timelines update" (blog.aifutures.org) — the median move and its four drivers. Write your own automated-coder median before you open it, then grade yourself against W1b's scorecard.
  • Third pass: AI 2027 + Situational Awareness (ai-2027.com · situational-awareness.ai) — both documents, marked up. For each, list five claims you now evaluate differently and the week that changed you.

Do

Label the method behind every strategy document before you judge it: contingency ladder (Plans A–E); branching scenario (AI 2027, MIRI); if-then triggers (Karnofsky, RSPs); portfolio over worldviews (Core Views, d/acc); fault tree (Barrett & Baum's ASI-PATH); backward chaining (A Narrow Path); assumption-explicit decomposition (DeepMind); single-premise reductio (IABIED).

Label five documents before S2, the two re-reads included. Note where one runs two methods and hides the seam.

Write the timelines-and-parameters memo. Twelve parameters: timelines, takeoff shape, polarity, alignment-difficulty distribution, offense-defense balance per domain, diffusion rate, compute concentration, warning-shot legibility, institutional response capacity, verification feasibility, delegation depth, labor substitution.

Operationalize each as a claim you could be wrong about and enter it in the cruxes.md format: C<n>. <claim> — p=0.XX (YYYY-MM-DD) — would move me: <evidence>. Add a timelines position: your median for one named capability milestone with resolution criteria, plus the methodology choice it is most sensitive to. Name which of the eight methods the memo itself uses.

Done when every parameter carries a credence, a date, and one named observable that would move it, and the memo names its own method.

Skip if you already maintain a dated table of all twelve with credences and observables, plus a timelines position with resolution criteria and a named method.

Take away

  • A world model is twelve dials; a strategy is a method applied to a setting of them. Backward-chain from short timelines and you get lab security this quarter; from long timelines, institution-building.
  • When two serious documents share the facts and reach opposite programs, the disagreement is usually about a parameter rather than values. RAND states the field's version: policymakers argue past one another because they weight the threat models differently.
  • The whipsaw does not discredit the forecasters. The same team moved its medians later in mid-2025 and roughly 1.5 years earlier in Q1 2026. Point medians are dominated by a few methodology choices, so the deliverable is a distribution plus its sensitivity, not a year.
  • Plans A–E conditions on political will rather than threat model, a variable most worldview documents don't carry at all. The lettering is now shared field vocabulary (AI 2040 plans in the same letters), which is adoption of a method rather than independent convergence.
  • The parameters are the live policy fights. Preemption bets on institutional response capacity; export controls price compute concentration against diffusion; the open-weights fight is offense-defense plus diffusion; every RSP is a verification-feasibility wager. S4 asks for these numbers.
Check yourself
  1. Classify by method: (a) phased prohibitions derived from "no uncontrolled superintelligence for 20 years"; (b) assumptions numbered up front, each mitigation traced to one; (c) a fork at a single decision node into two endings; (d) every marginal measure rejected because one conditional makes them all insufficient.
  2. Plans A–E conditions on political will rather than threat model. What does that indexing buy, and which parameter still does the most work inside each rung's takeover estimate?
  3. State Thorstad's central attack in one sentence, name the parameter it prices directly, and name one downstream parameter that shifts if he is right.
  4. The same team moved its medians later in 2025 and earlier in Q1 2026. Name two methodology choices that dominate such medians, and say what your memo does about that class of problem.

Answers. (1) Backward chaining (A Narrow Path); assumption-explicit decomposition (DeepMind); branching scenario (AI 2027); single-premise reductio (IABIED). (2) Indexing on will turns "which plan" from a values fight into a forecast: you can hold one threat model and still plan across all five rungs. Inside each rung the alignment-difficulty distribution does the most work; the 7%-to-75% climb in takeover risk as safety effort thins is what a distribution concentrated on "hard but tractable with effort" looks like. (3) Sustained superexponential intelligence growth is asserted rather than argued, against a record of diminishing research productivity and binding bottlenecks. It prices takeoff shape. Downstream, a slower and more continuous takeoff raises warning-shot frequency and gives institutional response capacity time to matter. (4) Which horizon-trend version you extrapolate and which doubling-time estimate you adopt; the task horizon the milestone must clear at 80% reliability is the third, and lowering that bar moved one author's median as much as the trend data did. Publish sensitivity rather than a point.

Threat models: the AI's objectives

Read

  • Carlsmith, "Is Power-Seeking AI an Existential Risk?" (arXiv:2206.13353) — all six premises, his credence on each, and the multiplication. Re-run your own six-premise sheet cold first, then mark every place he moves one of your numbers.
  • Wentworth, "The Case Against AI Control Research" (lesswrong.com) — the post, then the comment thread, where Buck's replies are the pro-control side showing up. Close with one sentence: which cell do these two disagree about?
  • Greenblatt, "Plans A, B, C, and D for misalignment risk" (blog.redwoodresearch.org) — a return, not a re-read; S1 took it for its method. Write one ledger row per plan: the will it assumes, the interventions that survive at that level, the family-A model it bets against.
  • Collier, "More Was Possible" (asteriskmag.com) — optional, with Contra Collier on IABIED (Harms) for the exchange. Read it if your pause-vs-continue fight keeps writing "alignment is intractable" without a source that argues it.

Do

Complete the five family-A dossiers, editing the files in dossiers/ in the repo: fast-takeoff (fast-takeoff-misaligned-takeover-decisive-st.md), scheming (scheming-deceptive-alignment.md), reward hacking (reward-hacking-emergent-broad-misalignment-n.md), WFLL-II (christiano-wfll-part-ii-going-out-with-a-ban.md), rogue replication (rogue-autonomous-replication-and-adaptation-.md).

For each: rewrite the seeded pathway in your own words and close it with one dated bottom-line credence; give every crux a credence and the observation that would move it; write a 12-month prediction with resolution criteria (an observable, a date, where you'll check); fill both matrix-drill lines. Source the empirical claims from your own W42 evidence review.

Then write two cell fights, half a page each, into matrix/matrix.md in the repo under "The seven debates as cell fights", in the cell format on the matrix: pause-vs-continue (disputed parameter: P(alignment intractable)) and control-vs-alignment (disputed parameters: window width and row weight). Each names its cell, whose sign holds under which parameter settings, and what evidence flips it.

Done when a search for "TODO" across the five dossier files returns nothing; each pathway section closes with a dated bottom-line credence and each prediction names an observable, a resolution date, and where you'll check; and each cell fight's first sentence names its cell (row, intervention column, disputed parameter) in matrix.md's terms.

Skip if you keep dossiers current as standing practice: show the five family-A files TODO-free with live predictions, write the two cell fights in one sitting, and move to S3.

Take away

  • Pricing a threat model takes four commitments: the pathway in your own words ending in one dated credence; a credence and a would-move-me line on every crux; one falsifiable 12-month prediction; and the matrix drill. The first three make you gradeable, the fourth makes you useful.
  • Family A is five competing mechanisms for cashing out Carlsmith's premises 3–5, which he prices at ~5% by 2070 (>10% in his May 2022 update note). One undifferentiated "misalignment credence" can't price them: they make different empirical claims and buy nearly disjoint policy.
  • The control-vs-alignment fight is not about whether scheming is real. Both sides accept W42's evidence. They disagree about how wide the useful-but-catchable window is and how much doom-mass sits in the scheming row at all.
  • Rogue replication uniquely motivates KYC-for-compute and cloud revocability: its binding constraint is payment and infrastructure, not cognition. Fast-takeoff actively disfavors spending leverage there, since in its world the dangerous system never rents anything.
  • WFLL-II uniquely motivates correlated-failure policy: cross-vendor diversity requirements, correlated stress testing, manual-reversion drills. That pays only if a stress period arrives with the correction loop still open.
Check yourself
  1. Which Carlsmith premises does W42's model-organism evidence bear on, and which does it leave untouched?
  2. The Wentworth–Buck thread is advertised as a fight about the useful-but-catchable window. What is it at least half actually about, and why do the two fights need different evidence?
  3. How does the Plans A–E ladder reframe pause-vs-continue, and what shape claim about the risk curve is the halt position in the ladder's terms?
  4. What is the difference between a dossier credence and a matrix cell sign, and why can a 2% row still carry signed cells?
