Answers. (1) Premise 3, alignment harder than misaligned-but-deployable: existence proofs that safety training can fail to remove planted deception and that strategic compliance can emerge unplanted. Weakly premise 4. It leaves premises 1–2 (trend and economics), 5 (scaling to full disempowerment) and 6 (a values claim) untouched, so the update propagates rather than overwriting the bottom line. (2) A row-weight fight. Wentworth puts the median doom-mass in non-scheming failure, namely slop, which lives in the reward-hacking row and family C's WFLL-I; Buck defends the scheming row as carrying enough mass to justify robustly-good coverage. Window width is a claim about capability profiles, row weight about which failure modes dominate, so you can concede a wide window and still zero out control by emptying its row. (3) The ladder conditions the plan on political will, and its takeover estimates degrade gracefully (7/13/20/45/75% down the rungs), so marginal will buys marginal safety everywhere on the curve. The halt position claims the curve is flat-then-cliff: if alignment is intractable, every rung short of an enforced global halt buys approximately nothing, so the only rational spend is generating will for Plan A. (4) A dossier credence is P(pathway), a claim about the world; a cell sign is the conditional effect of an intervention given the row, under stated parameter settings. They are independent commitments, so a row priced at 2% can still have well-signed cells. The prediction disciplines the credence; the crux line disciplines the sign.

Threat models: power, structure, and war

Read

  • Davidson, Finnveden & Hadshar, "AI-Enabled Coups" (forethought.org) — the executive summary, the three risk factors, one concrete pathway, and the mitigations in §5. Write your strongest critique before §5, then log which risk factor survives.
  • Kulveit et al., "Gradual Disempowerment" (gradual-disempowerment.ai · arXiv:2501.16946) — the mechanism, then Davidson's three objections in Thoughts on Gradual Disempowerment. State each at full strength first.
  • Kasirzadeh, "Two Types of AI Existential Risk: Decisive and Accumulative" (arXiv:2401.07836) — the distinction, the boiling-frog mechanics, and the governance section that prescribes by branch. Write your own column-by-branch list before that section, then diff.
  • Hendrycks, Schmidt & Wang, "Superintelligence Strategy" (MAIM) (arXiv:2503.05628) — reconstruct Situational Awareness's program from memory before you open it; you third-passed it in S1.
  • The MAIM exchange — read all three: MIRI's Refining MAIM (Abecassis), IAPS's Crucial Considerations in ASI Deterrence (Delaney), Hendrycks & Khoja's AI Deterrence Is Our Best Option.

Skip if you can already show the three family-B dossiers complete, the China cell fight standing in matrix.md, and an intervention map meeting that test.

Take away

  • Threat structure determines intervention class. A decisive risk concentrates in one actor at one moment, so you gate the frontier: compute thresholds, weight security, control protocols, licensing. An accumulative risk spreads across thousands of sub-frontier deployments, so you regulate the system: concentration limits, dependency rules, epistemic infrastructure, resilience.
  • Same severity, near-disjoint intervention sets. Ask what shape a threat is before arguing about how bad it is, and ask of any proposal which shape it reaches and whether its authors accept that the other shape is someone else's job.
  • "Solve alignment and these risks dissolve." Family B runs on solved alignment: a coup needs systems perfectly aligned to one principal, and family C needs no misaligned agent anywhere. Alignment progress can move these models' probability up, so their intervention columns barely intersect the alignment stack.
  • MAIM and SA share every fact and diverge to opposite national programs on one assumption: does a rival state treat your superintelligence project as an existential threat worth sabotaging? True → deterrence and nonproliferation. False → lead maximization is the safety margin.
  • The labor evidence the intelligence-curse cousin leans on is real and small: roughly 0.2% YoY contraction in the most-exposed occupations against ~0.1% growth in the least, as of April 2026, confounded by post-ZIRP hiring contraction, offshoring, and cohort size. Spend it with that label attached.
Check yourself
  1. Name the coups paper's three risk factors, and for each, the prior week whose evidence prices it.
  2. State GD's mechanism in two sentences, then Davidson's three objections at full strength. Which one, if it holds, moves GD out of the x-risk column entirely, and into what? Which one moves it into a different row?
  3. A senator's office asks you to rank two bills: one gates frontier training runs above a compute threshold, one funds epistemic infrastructure and limits concentration of automated decision-making in critical sectors. Using Kasirzadeh, explain why "which is better" is malformed, and what you would answer instead.
  4. Write the MAIM vs SA fight as a matrix cell: row, column, both signs, the differing assumption, and one item from the exchange that would move it.

Answers. (1) Secret loyalties, W42: Sleeper Agents shows a planted loyalty surviving supervised fine-tuning, RL, and adversarial training. Exclusive access, W44: it persists while weight security holds and the open-closed gap stays wide, which your S1 diffusion parameter puts near four months as of 2026. Singular loyalties, W43: logging, separation of duties, and external observers pointed at a misbehaving principal rather than a misbehaving model. (2) Mechanism: societal systems serve human interests because they need human labor, minds, and consent; as AI substitutes for each, those loops weaken independently and reinforce one another's drift, with no misaligned agent required. Objections: capital and taxation (humans keep asset ownership and can tax AI labor); incentive and legibility (elites have every incentive to prevent their own disempowerment, and truthful advisors to see it coming); wrong endpoint (the mechanism argues better for extreme power concentration among some humans than for universal disempowerment). The first moves GD into distribution policy: tax, transfer, antitrust. The third keeps the catastrophe and moves the row into family B's coups-and-lock-in pathway, which is why the objection comes from the coups author. (3) The bills serve different threat structures, not different quantities of one risk: the compute-threshold bill is + on decisive rows and ~0 on accumulative ones, the systemic bill the mirror image. Ranking them head-to-head smuggles in an unstated credence over threat structures. Answer with your weight on decisive versus accumulative catastrophe, and a portfolio split. (4) Row: AI-enabled great-power war, equally the China debate. Column: natsec-led national superintelligence project. SA: + (the lead is the safety margin). MAIM: − (the project is the preemption trigger). Differing assumption: rival perception. Delaney supplies the arithmetic (rival expects disempowerment ~70%, acts ~60%, US backs down ~60%, net ~25% that the dynamic delays anything six months or more), so documented sabotage doctrine raises the middle term and a credible commitment to escalate collapses the third.

Compute governance

Read

  • Sastry, Heim, Belfield et al., "Computing Power and the Governance of Artificial Intelligence" (arXiv:2402.08797) — the four governable properties and what they license, then the limitations and risks sections. Write your own defeater list, with a credence that each bites by 2030, before the limitations section.
  • Heim, Fist, Egan, Huang et al., "Governing Through the Cloud" (governance.ai) — providers as securers, record keepers, verifiers, and enforcers, and the claim that workload type and compute consumed are readable from non-confidential information. Write an evidence brief on that classification claim.
  • Petrie, Aarne, Ammann, Dalrymple, "Flexible Hardware-Enabled Guarantees for AI Compute" (arXiv:2506.15093) — part one of a series, with no deployment timeline offered. For each guarantee, name the physical or institutional assumption it stands on, then sort every claim into buildable-by-2030 or aspiration.
  • Wasil, Reed, Miller, Barnett, "Verification methods for international AI agreements" (arXiv:2408.16074) — optional, the S6 bridge. Ten methods in three families: national technical means, access-dependent, hardware-dependent. Read for the taxonomy and hold the treaty mechanics for S6.

Do

Build the compute-lever map, one row per lever, at least eight, spanning the stack: training-compute thresholds, large-run reporting, cloud KYC, chip export controls (the ECCN layer), upstream supply-chain controls on fabrication and tooling, outbound investment screening, cloud-provider enforcement, HEMs, location verification, international verification regimes.

Columns: mechanism, the named instrument and authority (R7 supplies these); current status, dated, citing pages/sources.md; failure modes, technical decay, evasion, political; matrix signs for your six priority dossiers (fast-takeoff, scheming, misuse-bio, coups, great-power war, safety-tax collapse) as sign/conditional/crux; decay parameter.

All forty-eight-plus signs live in the map, so promote only the contested few into matrix/matrix.md, which keeps its ~50-cell cap.

The export-control saga runs through the week as the evidence stream, from October 2022's first advanced-computing rule through the January 2026 case-by-case licensing regime for H200-class chips. It is one of the eight case-block cases, so write your memo before reading its sealed retrospectives.

Then draft the flagship skeleton: all five headings of flagship/candidate-1.md, the question stated decision-shaped in one sentence, at least three numbers carried over from the map. Write this memo even if your month-three flagship pick landed on candidate 2 or 3.

Done when the map covers at least eight levers and every row's final column names the single world-model parameter that most threatens that lever, with all three of W20's decay mechanisms (algorithmic progress, distillation, inference-time scaling) among the named parameters, each carrying a number: a rate, a halving time, or a dated threshold crossing. A map where every lever fears the same parameter fails.

Skip if you already have a map to this spec: eight-plus levers with mechanism, dated status, failure modes, matrix signs, and a named decay parameter each. Refresh the statuses against the source register and move to S5.

Take away

  • Compute governance works because compute is detectable, excludable, quantifiable, and produced by a concentrated supply chain. Those four properties are conditional on the scaling paradigm holding, not laws of nature, and each lever cashes one or more of them.
  • The levers are ordered: HEMs enable verification, verification enables treaties, treaties enable any credible slowdown. Dismissing hardware mechanisms as exotic quietly deletes the top of that chain.
  • Export controls are not AI safety policy by default. The flagship US "AI safety" lever serves great-power-competition threat models. For loss-of-control risk from the leading domestic labs it does nothing, and may worsen things by hardening the race framing that spends the safety tax.
  • A map sign is a conditional, not a verdict. Export controls score + for great-power war only if they slow the rival more than they accelerate indigenization, so the H200 memo can land either way. The crux is the good-enough-stack parameter: rival capability's elasticity to marginal chip access, a number nobody has published carefully.
Check yourself
  1. Name the four governable properties, and for each give one lever that cashes it and one post-2024 development that erodes it.
  2. A senator calls export controls "the single most important AI safety policy." Under which threat models is that defensible, under which does the sign go to zero or negative, and what single world-model assumption flips it?
  3. What two things does the cloud paper claim a provider can read from non-confidential information it already holds, which post-2024 training practice blurs the classification, and what does that do to a KYC trigger written on "training runs above X FLOP"?
  4. The dependency chain runs HEMs → verification → treaties → slowdown. Why that direction, and which FlexHEG component failing collapses which of Wasil's three verification families back into which other one?

Answers. (1) Detectable, cashed by reporting and thresholds (W21: a training run is five numbers you can rebuild from public information); eroded by decentralized training splitting runs below any single site's visibility. Excludable, cashed by export controls and KYC; eroded by the maturing good-enough domestic stack and by open weights, which leave exclusion with no object once the artifact diffuses. Quantifiable, cashed by FLOP-denominated triggers; eroded by inference-time scaling, which buys capability in a unit the metric never sees. Concentrated, cashed by chokepoint controls on fabrication, tooling, and cloud; eroded by indigenization. (2) Defensible under great-power war and competition models, and under safety-tax collapse via the lead-as-safety-budget argument, since a compute lead is the slack that makes a domestic slowdown affordable. Zero-to-negative under fast-takeoff and scheming from the leading domestic labs: controls do nothing about the frontrunner's own run, and under MAIM assumptions they raise a rival's incentive to preempt. The flipping assumption is rival perception. (3) Workload type (training versus inference) and compute consumption. W28's regime blurs it: verifiable-reward RL and test-time scaling spend inference-shaped compute that builds capability, so a "training run above X FLOP" trigger both under-triggers and over-triggers. Trigger design migrates toward aggregate-consumption and conduct-based tests, the direction the cloud paper's record-keeper and verifier roles anticipate. (4) Treaties need verification you can trust without trusting the counterparty. National technical means are weak against data centers that look like warehouses, and access-dependent inspection is invasive enough that states refuse it, so hardware-dependent methods are the only family offering per-chip granularity with privacy preserved. FlexHEG's two components are the guarantee processor and the tamper-protecting enclosure; if the enclosure fails, every attestation the processor signs becomes forgeable and the hardware-dependent family collapses back into access-dependent inspection.

Evals-gated governance

Read

  • Anthropic RSP v3.4 and the version archive (responsible-scaling-policy · rsp-updates) — the text for exercise 1, the corpus for exercise 2. Read it the way opposing counsel would: who could I hold to this, and how would I know if they broke it?
  • Karnofsky, "If-Then Commitments for AI Risk Reduction" (carnegieendowment.org) — the CBRN worked example, the tripwire-operationalization section, and the limitations he concedes. Then ask whether v3.4 allocates trigger-writing and threshold-moving power the way its logic demands.
  • Clymer et al., "Safety Cases: How to Justify the Safety of Advanced AI Systems" (arXiv:2403.10462) — the four argument categories (inability, control, trustworthiness, deference) and how a full case composes them; skip the worked-example detail. For each ASL gate in your red-lined RSP, name which type it instantiates.
  • OpenAI Preparedness Framework v2 (openai.com · PDF) — optional comparator: three tracked categories, two thresholds, with regulatory mapping split into the May 2026 Frontier Governance Framework. Diff its threshold language against the RSP you red-lined.
  • SaferAI risk-management ratings (tracker.safer-ai.org) — optional. Twelve companies scored 8–35% across risk identification, analysis, treatment, and governance. Compare their Risk Analysis score for the company you red-lined against your own hatch count.

Do

Exercise 1, the red-line. Read RSP v3.4 end to end, marking every discretionary escape hatch: modal verbs ("may", "we expect", "as appropriate"), undefined thresholds, self-adjudicated tests ("if we determine"), obligations with no named owner or date. Write your classification rule first, then classify each clause hard commitment or discretionary hatch.

Exercise 2, the version diff. All nine versions, v1.0 (19 September 2023) through v3.4, are archived at rsp-updates. Official redlines exist for v2.2 and v3.1–v3.4 only, so hand-diff the two structural rewrites, 1.0→2.0 and 2.2→3.0. Track thresholds by construct (capability, safeguard, who decides) rather than by section number.

Produce two documents: the red-lined RSP, an annotated excerpt list headed by your classification rule and the tally N discretionary hatches, M hard commitments; and the version-diff memo, one page, on the ratchet-down hypothesis. R3 has the SB 53 statutory read.

Then spend twenty minutes on the matrix, writing into matrix/matrix.md in the repo: cell-ify the evals-sufficiency debate for at least the safety-tax-collapse and scheming rows (sign, conditional, crux) and date your credence in cruxes.md.

Done when the tally line states N and M with every counted item quoted under the stated rule; the memo cites at least three textual changes by version number, assigns each a direction, renders an explicit verdict on the ratchet hypothesis (a "mixed" verdict passes only if it says which constructs moved which way), and connects the SB 53 disclosure-wins lesson to the diffability of the archive it relied on.

Skip if you have already annotated a frontier safety framework clause by clause with discretionary language flagged and counted, and can state which way its thresholds moved across at least two versions.

Take away

  • A hard commitment has three parts: a named actor, a checkable trigger, and a dated or otherwise verifiable duty. Anything missing one is a hatch. SaferAI's version of the point: an RSP without a defined risk tolerance is risk management in name only.
  • The architecture is a ladder, RSP → safety case → audit → licensing, and each rung moves threshold-moving power further from the developer. A self-written policy is checked by its author; licensing is the state grading before deployment. No rung above the first binds in US law today.
  • Discretion is not disproof, and revision is not retreat. A framework with zero "may" would pretend W36–W40's instruments can bear rule-grade precision. The ratchet-down hypothesis (Khlaaf & Myers West, arXiv:2504.15088) is an empirical claim about direction, readable only in the text.
  • Karnofsky's argument is that if-then commitments cost near-nothing if the trigger capabilities never arrive and bind if they do, so short- and long-timeline camps can sign the same text. Take it seriously and the fight relocates onto who writes the triggers, who runs the evals, and who can move the thresholds.
  • Every disclosure instrument now in force (SB 53, the EU AI Act's GPAI obligations) compels publication of frameworks like the one you red-lined, and none validates the measurement or accredits an auditor. California's substantive-mandate bill died by veto and its disclosure bill became law, so disclosure is the regime we run.
Check yourself
  1. Which version-to-version steps in the 2023→2026 RSP history have official redlines, which don't, and why does that distribution put the hand-diff work where the ratchet hypothesis is hardest to test?
  2. Reconstruct Karnofsky's worldview-robustness argument in two sentences, and state what it predicts about where the political fight moves once everyone has signed an if-then regime.
  3. Name Clymer et al.'s four safety-case argument types. Which do current ASL-style capability gates mostly instantiate, and which does W42's evaluation-awareness evidence stress?
  4. Give one piece of text-history evidence that complicates a simple ratchet-down story, and say what SB 53 changes about the ratchet dynamic and what it cannot change.

Answers. (1) Official redlines: v2.2 and v3.1 through v3.4. None for 1.0→2.0, 2.0→2.1, and 2.2→3.0. The two unredlined structural rewrites are where section structure changed and thresholds were re-founded rather than edited, so direction can't be read off a diff tool and each construct must be tracked by hand, which is where a ratchet would hide. (2) If-then commitments cost nearly nothing if trigger capabilities never arrive and bind if they do, so signing requires no agreement on timelines. Commitment-signing is therefore the cheap phase, and the fight relocates to trigger-writing, eval-running, and threshold-moving: the powers your red-line audited. (3) Inability, control, trustworthiness, deference. ASL-style gates are mostly inability arguments: the model cannot yet do X, as measured pre-deployment. Evaluation awareness stresses inability at its measurement joint, since a test-aware model can sandbag the eval the gate is written on, and spills into trustworthiness as behavioral evidence stops discriminating safe from test-savvy. (4) Any of: v3.2 giving the Long-Term Benefit Trust approval power over the selection of external reviewers; v3.1's clarification that pausing development remains available regardless of RSP requirements; v3.3's novel-CBRN threshold rewritten to track the threat model more closely. SB 53 makes the framework and its revisions legally disclosable, so private revision becomes public, diffable text and the ratchet is at least observable. It cannot stop a threshold from moving, validate the measurement behind it, or put anyone outside the company in the approval loop.

International instruments and verification

Read

  • Baker, "Nuclear Arms Control Verification and Lessons for AI Treaties" (arXiv:2304.04123) — the mechanics that transfer: declarations, on-site inspection, unique identifiers, Votkinsk-style perimeter monitoring, managed access. Run a two-column analogy audit, transfers / breaks, writing the breaks column first.
  • Sheehan, "China's AI Regulations and How They Get Made" (carnegieendowment.org) — the policy funnel and the three binding regulations (recommendation algorithms 2021, deep synthesis 2022, generative AI 2023). Write the CAC's own brief for them without using the word "race".
  • Concordia AI, "State of AI Safety in China, 2026 edition" (concordia-ai.com · aisafetychina.com) — the executive summary and the domestic-governance chapter. List five claims that would surprise your natsec-hawk persona, marking which decide "would Beijing sign a verified agreement?"
  • AI Futures Project, "AI 2040, Plan A" (ai-2040.com) — read as its authors insist, "primarily a recommendation, not a prediction": total research transparency, mutual datacenter verification, controlled compute growth, "mutually assured compute destruction" as enforcement, a pause at expert-level AI before a ~2040 resumption. Price each component.

Do

Write four paragraphs against the record, one per case: New START mechanics, the IAEA's continuity of knowledge stress-tested to failure in Iran, the BWC verification collapse, Montreal's preconditions. Each names the single most transferable design and the disanalogy that carries the weight. Go deep enough to audit anyone's analogy, Baker's and Plan A's included.

Build the ten-instrument table: the Bletchley–Seoul–Paris–New Delhi summit declarations, the EU AI Act, the GPAI Code of Practice, lab RSPs, California's SB 53, US export controls, the Hiroshima Process reporting framework v2, the UN scientific panel, the CAC generative-AI measures, New START. Classify each binding / voluntary / declaratory, with current status from the <a href="sources.html">source register</a> and the rows it lacks logged.

Then matrix v2, curated to roughly fifty cells in the matrix/matrix.md format: the seven debate cells, plus for each threat model the intervention it uniquely motivates and one it actively disfavors. Four fights arrive in draft (pause↔continue and control↔alignment from S2, China from S3, evals-sufficiency from S5); finalize those rather than rewriting them.

Write the remaining three fresh, each as a fight over its named parameter: open weights is irreversibility × who-diffuses-first, AGI-is-far is task-horizon extrapolation validity, safety-washing is who sets thresholds. Date every credence, take crux resolution dates from the dossiers' 12-month predictions, and cut any cell you couldn't defend to a named reader.

Plan A gets one paragraph in the front matter, priced component-by-component against the four records. Write the China capability-and-governance brief: two pages with named sources on capability (Ding's diffusion frame), what the regulations bind, what the state's moves signal (WAICO, founded in Shanghai in July 2026), and a bottom line on "would Beijing sign?" with a dated credence.

Done when every maintained cell carries sign, conditional, and crux; every instrument row names its enforcement mechanism or says "none"; and the brief never asserts a bind without naming the instrument and organ that enforces it, or a signal without naming its audience. A matrix where every sign is positive fails, as does a brief in which an undifferentiated "China" does things only the CAC, MIIT, or the Politburo could do. The China-scholar ideological Turing test with a human judge sits at this gate; take the dossier snapshot before you sit it.

Skip if you already hold a conditional map covering the seven debates with sign, conditional, and crux, plus a China analysis built on primary Chinese texts. Take the week as pure finalization.

Take away

  • Verification is designed around what is countable and attributable, not around what is dangerous. New START counted deployed delivery vehicles and never verified warhead stockpiles. Every AI-treaty proposal answers the same question first, and the only serious answer so far is chips and facilities.
  • "Voluntary" is not a synonym for toothless, and "binding" is not a synonym for enforced. The GPAI Code of Practice is voluntary, and signing it buys presumption of conformity with a regulation that fines. The BWC is a ratified treaty that has run fifty years without a verification protocol. Classify on the mechanism, not the label.
  • Baker's analogy breaks in two places: the regulated parties are private firms rather than a handful of state programs, and trained weights copy at zero marginal cost, so there is no conserved physical referent for "a model". Political will is the third break, and the New START expiry (February 5, 2026) priced it in public.
  • The open-weights camps inverted in 2025–26. Washington debated restricting Chinese open models while Beijing moved to restrict its own models' export, so openness became a state instrument on both sides and the marginal-risk question is now conditional on whose stack diffuses.
  • The instrument table is lever-level fluency and the matrix is the skeleton of the capstone's position paper. R9 turns this into a negotiation brief with red lines and fallbacks; R10–R12 add the economics, infrastructure, and implementation mechanics.
Check yourself
  1. Baker's case that AI verification is more nuclear-shaped than bio-shaped rests on one structural fact. Name it, then name two facts about the AI object the analogy cannot absorb.
  2. The GPAI Code of Practice and the Hiroshima reporting framework are both "voluntary". Why do they classify differently once you name the mechanism?
  3. State the two grounds of the 2001 US rejection of the BWC protocol, the two Montreal features that transfer to AI, and the Montreal precondition AI most clearly lacks.
  4. Name two obligations the CAC generative-AI measures enforce and one function the regime serves beyond enforcement, and say what makes the algorithm registry the reusable tool.

Answers. (1) Frontier AI runs on thousands of highly specialized, physically trackable, supply-chain-concentrated chips, so a countable attributable object exists, as with delivery vehicles and fissile material and unlike bioweapons' commodity equipment. It cannot absorb that the regulated parties are private firms rather than a handful of state programs, and that trained weights copy at zero marginal cost, leaving no conserved referent for "a model", so verification must target production and facilities, the Votkinsk move, never the artifact. (2) "Voluntary" is a label; the mechanism is the classification. Code of Practice adherence buys presumption of conformity with the EU AI Act's binding GPAI obligations, a voluntary on-ramp into a fining regime enforceable from August 2026. The Hiroshima framework v2 is transparency reporting with no conformity hook: reputational, which is "none" with better stationery. (3) Rejection grounds: a protocol too weak to catch cheaters would create false assurance, and inspections would expose proprietary and biodefense information, the objection that recurs verbatim when labs face audits. Montreal transfers adjustment procedures that tighten schedules without re-ratification, and trade measures against non-parties, market-access enforcement that maps onto compute supply chains better than inspections do. The missing precondition: a harm with no strategic-competition dimension and an industry holding patented substitutes. (4) Binds: filing models and algorithms in the registry, and content-security obligations, namely review, labeling, and synthetic-media marking with real takedown and licensing consequences. Beyond enforcement, the regime signals ideological control domestically and governance leadership internationally. The registry is reusable because it was built once under the 2021 algorithm regulation and every subsequent regulation bolts new duties onto the same filing infrastructure.

R1: US AI policy architecture

Read

For each instrument, record: issuer, authority, legal status, required action, deadline, implementing institution, litigation exposure, successor. That table is the residency's standing habit.

  • America's AI Action Plan (White House, July 2025; PDF) — the three pillars and every action assigned to an institution. Mark which of its ~90 actions carry authority and which are aspiration on letterhead.
  • Executive Order 14365 (Dec 2025; text) — the litigation task force, funding conditions, preemption theory, deadlines, legal limits. Record each asserted mechanism and its limit.
  • NSPM-11 (June 2026; text) — the adoption, assurance, accountability, compute, security and reporting directives, with owners and deadlines.

Do

The case. Read the EO 14110 record up to 20 January 2025 only: issued Oct 2023, implemented through OMB M-24-10 and DPA §4.2 reporting. Give yourself an hour to write the half-page memo an incoming NSC staffer needed: what does rescission actually delete, and what survives it? Then check against what happened.

The memo. Two pages, BLUF, to a named principal (an IFP colleague heading to a Hill briefing, or a state legislative staffer): the operative map of US AI authority this month. What binds, what is litigation-exposed (DOJ task force, X.AI v. Weiser, Colorado's repeal-and-replace), what is vacuum. Recommendation first, three options, one page of map.

Done when every consequential claim links to controlling text, legal force is distinguished from political announcement throughout, and a reader can act from page one. Then compare against a real CRS report on the same ground and count the levers you missed.

Take away

  • Five instrument types carry almost everything: statute, executive order, agency rule, guidance, funding condition. Knowing which one you're holding is the skill this unit exists for.
  • An EO cannot preempt state law. Preemption needs a statute to conflict with, so EO 14365 works through proxies instead: DOJ litigation, funding conditions on BEAD, and agency preemption strategies that each need their own hook.
  • EO provisions anchored in statute survive; free-floating ones are press releases with deadlines. EO 14110's DPA §4.2 reporting had real authority behind it, and its compute thresholds outlived the EO by migrating into state law.
  • Classified annexes, directive verbs, no notice-and-comment: that is the national-security instrument type, and NSPM-11 is the current example.
  • Strategy documents are unreviewable because they do nothing. You challenge the agency action that follows, under the APA, not the plan.
  • Durability ranking: statute > agency rule (notice-and-comment cuts both ways) > IEEPA-anchored EO section > OMB memo, which the next director can rescind before lunch.
Check yourself
  1. Why can't EO 14365 preempt state law itself, and what three proxies does it use?
  2. Which provision of the rescinded EO 14110 had the strongest statutory anchor, and why does that matter?
  3. The Action Plan "directs" an agency to do something no statute authorizes. What happens, and who could challenge it?
  4. Rank by durability: OMB memo, BIS final rule, IEEPA-anchored EO section, signed statute.

Answers. (1) Preemption flows from statute; an EO creates none. Proxies: DOJ litigation on existing-law and dormant-commerce theories, discretionary funding conditions, and FTC/FCC preemption strategies that need their own statutory hooks. (2) DPA §4.2 training-run reporting, which borrowed the Defense Production Act's authority. (3) Nothing self-executing; an agency must find authority elsewhere or proceed as guidance, and a regulated party challenges the resulting action, not the plan. (4) Statute > BIS rule > EO section > OMB memo.

R2: Administrative law and regulatory design

Read

  • Administrative Procedure Act, 5 U.S.C. 551 through 559 and 701 through 706 (text) — definitions, rulemaking, adjudication, hearings, sanctions, reviewability, scope of review, remedies. Work §553 and §706 with a pen: mark which steps build a record a court can review.
  • Circular A-4: Regulatory Analysis (OMB, Sept 17, 2003; PDF) — baseline, alternatives, benefits, costs, distribution, discounting, uncertainty, presentation. Take the section order; your RIA outline follows it.
  • OMB M-25-15 (Feb 12, 2025; PDF) — revoked the Nov 9, 2023 revision of A-4 under EO 14192 and reinstated the 2003 circular. Note what changed: discount rates, distributional weights.
  • Loper Bright Enterprises v. Raimondo (opinion) — majority and dissent on statutory interpretation, agency expertise, ambiguity, reliance, and the judicial role. Track what the majority still says a court owes an agency's reasoning.

Do

Draft a narrow rule for one documented AI risk. Build the statutory authority, evidentiary record, alternatives, cost analysis, and judicial review sections. Map the delegation you stand on, the procedure you owe, the reasoned explanation, the enforcement mechanism, the remedies, and the standard of review.

Produce three documents: a notice of proposed rulemaking, a regulatory impact analysis outline in A-4 order, and a litigation risk memorandum naming the likely plaintiff, the forum, and the strongest count against you.

Done when the rule stays within delegated authority, answers serious alternatives, preserves a reviewable record, and states what evidence would force revision.

Skip if you can already write an authority section that survives a Loper Bright reading.

Take away

  • Notice-and-comment is the price of binding effect. The §553 exceptions (interpretive rules, general statements of policy, agency organization and procedure, good cause) buy speed and cost enforceability.
  • A "guidance" document that in practice binds regulated parties can be recharacterized as a legislative rule issued without procedure, and vacated.
  • Arbitrary-and-capricious review under §706 tests the record you built, not the answer you reached. Agencies lose for ignoring an obvious alternative, passing over contrary evidence, or supplying a post hoc rationale their lawyers invented.
  • Loper Bright did not strip agencies of power, it moved the question. Courts now exercise independent judgment on what a statute means, with Skidmore respect for persuasive agency reasoning, and express delegations of discretion still bind.
  • The drafting consequence: ambiguity is no longer a safe harbor, so effort moves into the authority section.
  • Which version of Circular A-4 governs is itself a political variable. The 2023 revision lowered discount rates and added distributional weighting; M-25-15 revoked it under EO 14192 and restored the 2003 text. Show the calculation both ways.
  • For AI specifically there are few binding hooks: an existing sectoral statute (FTC Act §5, ECOA, the FD&C Act), a reporting authority like DPA §4.2, or nothing. Name the hook first, then design the rule to fit it.
Check yourself
  1. An agency issues an "AI transparency framework" as guidance to move fast. What has it given up, and how might a court treat the document anyway?
  2. Under §706, what must a challenger show, and what is the agency's best defense?
  3. After Loper Bright, where in an NPRM does the drafting effort concentrate?
  4. Which version of Circular A-4 governs your RIA today, what changed, and under what authority?

Answers. (1) Guidance binds nobody and cannot be enforced against a regulated party, and it builds no record; if it functions as a binding norm, a court can treat it as a legislative rule issued without notice-and-comment and vacate it. (2) That the action was arbitrary and capricious: an important aspect ignored, contrary record evidence unaddressed, an explanation running against the evidence, or no reasoned explanation at all. The defense is the contemporaneous record. (3) The statutory authority section; courts read the statute themselves now, so the rule must show the text authorizes it or point to an express delegation of discretion. (4) The Sept 17, 2003 circular, reinstated by OMB M-25-15 under EO 14192; the revoked Nov 9, 2023 revision had lowered the discount rate and added distributional weighting.

R3: Federalism and state AI law

Read

  • Executive Order 14365 (White House, Dec 2025; text) — every asserted federal mechanism and the legal challenge it invites. You mapped this in R1; here, write the statutory hook each mechanism would need to work.
  • California Transparency in Frontier Artificial Intelligence Act, SB 53 (enacted text) — covered actors, duties, thresholds, reporting, enforcement, effective dates. Copy the thresholds down verbatim and list who sits just outside them.
  • Colorado Artificial Intelligence Act, SB 24-205 (bill) — deployer and developer duties, high risk decisions, discrimination, notice, enforcement, effective dates. Note every date that has moved and what moved it.

Do

The case. Put SB 1047 as passed beside SB 53 as enacted and derive the political constraint set from the diff alone, before you read the veto message. Dropped: the shutdown capability, third-party audits, the Board of Frontier Models, liability for derivative models. Kept: published frameworks, incident reporting, whistleblower protection, a compute-and-revenue gate. Give yourself an hour to write the half-page memo naming which cut bought which vote. Then read Newsom's veto message of 29 September 2024 and count what you attributed to the wrong opponent.

Compare one frontier model rule and one automated decision rule across authority, object, trigger, evidence, remedies, compliance cost, and interstate effects.

Then write a federalism options memorandum with model statutory language and a preemption analysis: which level of government holds the constitutional power, which holds statutory authority today, which has the institutional competence to run the program, and where those three come apart.

Done when the recommendation separates constitutional power, statutory authority, institutional competence, and political preference, and a drafter could lift your model language into a bill without rewriting it.

Take away

  • The two state laws regulate different objects. SB 53 reaches large frontier developers by capability and size: models trained above 10^26 operations, developers above $500M annual revenue.
  • SB 53's duties are published safety frameworks, transparency reports, critical safety incident reporting to Cal OES within 15 days, whistleblower protection, and AG enforcement up to $1M per violation.
  • The Colorado act reaches developers and deployers of systems making consequential decisions, and imposes a duty of reasonable care against algorithmic discrimination.
  • Enforcement design decides who complies. Both statutes route through the state attorney general with no private right of action, which caps exposure and makes compliance a function of one office's priorities and budget.
  • Colorado also offers an affirmative defense for programs aligned to the NIST AI RMF, which quietly converts a voluntary framework into a liability shield.
  • Preemption is a statutory question, and there is no AI statute to preempt with. The Senate stripped the ten-year state moratorium from the 2025 reconciliation bill 99-1. EO 14365 therefore works through proxies: DOJ litigation, funding conditions, and agency proceedings that each need their own hook.
  • Funding conditions have limits worth knowing before you propose one. Under Dole they must be unambiguous and related to the federal program's purpose, and NFIB bars conditions coercive enough to be compulsion. Conditioning BEAD broadband money on a state's AI law is where both tests get argued.
  • Dormant Commerce Clause attacks on state AI law are weaker than industry briefs suggest. After National Pork Producers v. Ross (2023), a nondiscriminatory law with incidental out-of-state burden usually survives.
  • Compliance dates are the live fight, not the duties. Colorado's obligations slipped to June 30, 2026 and the statute has faced repeal-and-replace pressure every session since enactment. Treat any effective date as a lobbying target.
Check yourself
  1. Why can't an executive order cure Congress's failure to pass a preemptive AI statute?
  2. Name three ways the objects of SB 53 and the Colorado AI Act differ.
  3. What are the constitutional limits on conditioning federal grant money on a state repealing its AI law?
  4. A developer argues a state AI law unconstitutionally burdens interstate commerce. How strong is that after National Pork Producers?

Answers. (1) Preemption flows from a federal statute conflicting with state law; an EO creates none, so it can only fund litigation, attach conditions to money Congress already appropriated, or push agencies to act under their own hooks. (2) Covered actor (large frontier developers by compute and revenue vs. developers and deployers of consequential decision systems); trigger (training scale vs. use in a specific decision about a person); duty (disclosure, framework publication, and incident reporting vs. reasonable care against algorithmic discrimination). (3) Dole: unambiguous, related to the funded program, within the spending power; NFIB: not coercive. Both are argued on the size and relatedness of the grant. (4) Weak on its own. Incidental burden from an evenhanded law is generally tolerated, and the extraterritoriality theory narrowed after Ross; the stronger challenges are statutory or First Amendment.

R4: Rights, privacy, due process, and sectoral authority

Read

  • Mobley v. Workday, Inc., No. 3:23-cv-00770 (N.D. Cal.) (docket) — the May 16, 2025 conditional-certification, March 6, 2026 applicant-coverage, and June 22, 2026 motion-to-dismiss orders. Track the vendor-as-agent theory through each ruling.
  • EEOC on algorithmic hiring (Mobley amicus brief, AI guidance, removed Jan 27, 2025) — note who is left to enforce.
  • CFPB Circular 2023-03, adverse action notification (circular) — ECOA and Regulation B when a model drives the denial. Draft the notice a 400-feature model would have to generate.
  • AI/ML in Software as a Medical Device (FDA, page) — lifecycle, change control, and monitoring materials. Work out which updates a predetermined change control plan covers and which force a new submission.
  • Illinois Public Act 103-0804 (HB 3773) (text) — optional. The notice duty and the proxy-discrimination clause; read them against California's automated-decision regulations.

Do

Audit one high-consequence deployment in employment, credit, health, education, benefits, or criminal justice under existing law. Trace the affected right, the protected class or interest, the decision process, notice, explanation, challenge, remedy, regulator, and burden of proof. Produce a rights impact assessment, a challenge procedure, a regulator map, and a remedy matrix.

Done when the analysis names actual causes of action or administrative authority, tests procedural fairness, and includes the affected person's route to relief, written so that person could follow it.

Take away

  • Title VII, the ADEA, the ADA, ECOA, the FD&C Act, and the Due Process Clause already attach to these decisions. Inserting a vendor does not open a gap: Mobley's theory is that a screening vendor acts as the employer's agent and can be sued directly.
  • "The model is proprietary" is not a defense to an explanation duty. ECOA and Regulation B require the specific principal reasons for adverse action, and Circular 2023-03 denies the sample-form checklists any safe harbor when the model's reasons are not on them.
  • FDA regulates the change as well as the device. A predetermined change control plan states in advance which model modifications may ship, with the protocol and impact assessment; those need no new marketing submission, and anything outside the plan does.
  • Withdrawn guidance leaves the statutes standing. EEOC pulled its AI guidance Jan 27, 2025 under EO 14179; what moved is who enforces: private plaintiffs, state AGs, Illinois HB 3773 (eff. Jan 1, 2026), and California's automated-decision regulations (eff. Oct 1, 2025).
  • People harmed usually lose on procedure, not merits: no notice the system was used, no reasons, no human to appeal to, a deadline that ran before they knew. Design the challenge path first.
Check yourself
  1. A vendor screens applicants for hundreds of employers. On what theory is the vendor itself liable under Title VII or the ADEA?
  2. A lender's model uses 400 features and no human can rank them. What does ECOA still require in the notice?
  3. What does a predetermined change control plan change about FDA review of an updated model?
  4. An agency withdraws its AI enforcement guidance. What changed legally, and what changed practically?

Answers. (1) Agency: the vendor screens applicants against employer-set criteria, a function the employer would otherwise perform itself, so it falls inside the statutes' coverage of employment agencies and agents. Mobley survived dismissal on that theory and drew a nationwide ADEA collective. (2) The specific and accurate principal reasons for the denial, derived from the model actually used, not the nearest item on a sample-form checklist. (3) Review moves upstream: modifications described in the approved plan ship without a new submission; anything outside it requires one. (4) Legally nothing, since guidance is not law and Title VII, the ADEA and the ADA stand. Practically, federal investigations and amicus support dry up and enforcement falls to private suits and state regimes.

R5: Copyright, training data, identity, and open weights

Read

  • Copyright and Artificial Intelligence reports (US Copyright Office, hub) — the three reports on digital replicas, copyrightability, and training, plus the cited authorities. Mark where each states existing law and where it recommends legislation.
  • C2PA Technical Specification 2.2 (spec) — claims, manifests, signing, trust, ingredients, redaction, threat model, limitations. Read the threat model first, then list what a valid manifest does not prove.
  • Model Openness Framework (Linux Foundation AI and Data, isitopen.ai) — source, weights, data, documentation, licensing, reproducibility, downstream freedoms. Score two releases that both call themselves open.

Do

The case. It is February 2019. Your lab has trained a language model in four sizes, the largest at 1.5 billion parameters, and the release decision is yours: all weights now, staged by size, or hold. Nothing has shipped, so there is no misuse record to reason from. Give yourself an hour for the half-page recommendation, saying which of the four openness variables each option moves and what evidence would reverse you. Then read Solaiman et al., Release Strategies and the Social Impacts of Language Models (arXiv:1908.09203), and NTIA's 2024 report on open model weights for what a government made of the same question five years later.

Analyze one proposed training or release practice from the creator, developer, deployer, researcher, and affected person perspectives. Produce an options memorandum with licensing, provenance, transparency, access, liability, and enforcement provisions, and name for each option the empirical question whose answer would change your recommendation.

Done when the product avoids treating all openness or all data rights as one variable and identifies the empirical crux behind each tradeoff.

Skip if you can already say which of the four openness variables a given release moves.

Take away

  • The Copyright Office split the problem three ways: digital replicas (Part 1, July 2024), copyrightability of AI output (Part 2, January 2025), and training as fair use (Part 3, pre-publication May 2025). The split is useful even where you disagree with the conclusions.
  • Registration requires human authorship. Prompting alone does not supply it; human selection, arrangement, or modification can.
  • Fair use for training is decided on a record, not on a technology. Bartz v. Anthropic and Kadrey v. Meta (N.D. Cal., June 2025) both treated training as transformative on those records, leaving acquisition method and market dilution as the live questions.
  • A C2PA manifest proves who signed, not that the content is true. It binds assertions to a signer and a hash. Metadata strips silently, absence of a manifest means nothing, and a compromised or lying signer produces a valid claim.
  • Openness is at least four independent variables: code, weights, training data, documentation, each with its own license and downstream freedoms. A weights release is the one decision in this unit that cannot be reversed.
  • Each tradeoff has a matching instrument: contract and licensing for data, statute for digital replicas (state ELVIS-type laws, a federal NO FAKES-style bill), procurement and disclosure rules for provenance, liability allocation for downstream deployment.
Check yourself
  1. Your client's model output is commercially valuable. What has to be true for any of it to be registrable?
  2. A platform mandates C2PA manifests on every upload. Which harms does that leave untouched?
  3. Two labs both announce "open source" models. Which distinctions does that phrase hide?
  4. Which risks of a training practice can licensing fix, and which survive a signed license?

Answers. (1) Human authorship the Office recognizes: selection and arrangement of AI-generated material, or human-authored elements within the work. The prompt does not count, and the registration must disclaim machine-generated portions. (2) Anything about truth or consent. It leaves authentic-but-misleading capture, defamatory real footage, unlicensed training on signed content, and unsigned content whose missing manifest proves nothing. (3) Whether weights, code, training data, and documentation are each released, under what license, with what use restrictions, and whether a third party can reproduce the result. (4) Licensing can authorize a corpus, allocate indemnity, and set field-of-use limits. It cannot fix third-party rights the licensor never held, publicity and digital-replica claims from people depicted in the data, or downstream liability for outputs.

R6: National security institutions and military use

Read

  • National Security Presidential Memorandum 11 (June 2026) (text) — every directive, responsible official, deadline, assurance requirement, civil liberties safeguard, compute action, and review duty. Build the deadline table as you go.
  • DoD Directive 3000.09, Autonomy in Weapon Systems (PDF) — definitions, policy, senior review, testing, human judgment, failure handling, responsibilities. Read the exemptions and the waiver as carefully as the review requirement.
  • AI Risk Management Framework (NIST, hub) — apply Govern, Map, Measure, and Manage to a classified or mission-critical deployment and mark what it leaves to mission authority.

Do

Run a tabletop involving capability surprise, compromised weights, vendor dependence, intelligence uncertainty, alliance coordination, civilian harm, and command responsibility. Produce a national security decision memorandum, an assurance plan, and an after action report that names a human owner for each residual risk.

Done when the recommendation preserves mission effectiveness, constitutional accountability, independent testing, escalation routes, and explicit residual risk ownership.

Take away

  • DoDD 3000.09 is not a ban on autonomous weapons. It requires appropriate levels of human judgment over the use of force, and its teeth are a senior review once before development and again before fielding, for systems in a defined category.
  • That review runs through the Under Secretaries for Policy and for Research and Engineering with the Vice Chairman. Several classes of system are exempt, and the review can be waived for urgent military need.
  • National security instruments route around the R2 machinery by design. APA §553(a)(1) exempts military and foreign affairs functions from notice-and-comment, so an NSPM has no comment record and usually no reviewable agency action. Oversight arrives through appropriations, committee notification, inspectors general, and internal review boards.
  • The NIST AI RMF is voluntary and sector-neutral. Govern, Map, Measure, Manage gives vocabulary and a risk register; it assigns no authority and sets no threshold. Every "acceptable risk" determination is a mission owner's call, made by name.
  • Vendor dependence converts into an operational constraint. When the model, the weights, the eval harness, and the serving stack sit with a contractor, independent testing, surge capacity, and the ability to halt a deployment all depend on contract terms written years earlier.
  • Weapons review is a separate track from AI policy. Legal review of new weapons and means of warfare, the practice Additional Protocol I article 36 describes and DoD runs through its own directives and the Law of War Manual, attaches to the weapon whatever any AI framework says.
Check yourself
  1. A program office claims DoDD 3000.09 forbids its system. What does the directive actually require, and whose signatures does it need?
  2. Why can't a contractor challenge NSPM-11 the way it would challenge a BIS rule?
  3. What does the AI RMF give you, and what does it deliberately refuse to decide?
  4. Name three ways vendor dependence becomes a command problem during the tabletop.

Answers. (1) For covered systems, senior review before formal development and again before fielding, plus verification, validation, testing and evaluation, and design that lets commanders exercise appropriate levels of human judgment over the use of force. Signed by the Under Secretaries for Policy and for Research and Engineering and the Vice Chairman; exemptions and an urgent-need waiver exist. (2) No notice-and-comment record and no final agency action in the usual sense: APA §553(a)(1) exempts military and foreign affairs functions, and the substance may sit in classified annexes. Accountability runs through Congress and internal oversight. (3) A shared vocabulary, a lifecycle structure, and a way to enumerate and track risks. It refuses to set thresholds, allocate authority, or say what risk is acceptable. (4) You cannot independently test what you cannot access; you cannot surge or retrain without the vendor; and you may not be able to halt a deployment or recover weights unless the contract said so.

R7: Export controls, investment, industrial strategy, and compute

Read

  • Export Administration Regulations (Bureau of Industry and Security, regulations) — advanced computing and semiconductor manufacturing controls, definitions, license requirements, end use rules, exceptions, enforcement cases. Take one ECCN through the full flow: classification, destination, end user, license exception, penalty.
  • 31 CFR Part 850, outbound investment (Treasury, program page) — covered transactions, technologies, countries, prohibitions, notifications, knowledge standard, exceptions, penalties. Sort one hypothetical deal into prohibited, notifiable, or neither.
  • CHIPS and Science Act resources (NIST, hub) — incentive authority, guardrails, program implementation, research institutions, current awards. Find the guardrail language in an actual award and read what triggers clawback.

Do

The case. Take the chain to 15 January 2026 and stop. The October 2022 interim final rule, A800 and H800 inside one product cycle, the October 2023 revision adding performance density, H20, the Diffusion Rule issued 15 January 2025 and rescinded 13 May 2025, then BIS moving H200 and MI325X from presumption of denial to case-by-case. You are the licensing officer holding the first applications. One hour, half a page: which do you approve, on what conditions, and what does that reasoning commit you to when the next compliant-but-capable SKU arrives? Then check against what happened.

Model one control across substitution, evasion, enforcement capacity, allied incentives, welfare cost, indigenous production, and effects on research. Produce a control design, a quantitative sensitivity analysis, an allied negotiation brief, and an enforcement plan.

Sort each element by the authority it runs on: ECRA and the EAR, IEEPA, CFIUS, section 232, CHIPS conditions, procurement, or diplomacy. Say which needs Congress.

Done when the work cites operative provisions, models behavioral response, identifies leakage paths, and states a measurable revision trigger.

Take away

  • Controls work on chokepoints, not on knowledge. Leverage exists where a few firms in allied jurisdictions supply everyone: lithography, high-bandwidth memory, EDA tools, advanced packaging. Where no chokepoint exists, a control mostly produces substitution and a domestic program in the target country.
  • The EAR reaches items; services largely fall outside it. Renting controlled compute from an uncontrolled jurisdiction is the standing leakage path; serious proposals reach for end-use rules, KYC duties on cloud providers, and license conditions on operators to close it.
  • Enforcement capacity is the binding constraint on any design. Licensing decisions, end-use checks, and penalties depend on a small field force and on foreign cooperation, so score every candidate control on whether a violation would be detected within the time it matters.
  • Outbound investment is the newer and narrower instrument. 31 CFR Part 850 sorts covered transactions in semiconductors and microelectronics, quantum, and AI into prohibited and notifiable, runs on a knowledge standard that includes a duty of reasonable and diligent inquiry, and draws its penalties from IEEPA.
  • Industrial policy binds through conditions, not appropriations. CHIPS guardrails bar material expansion of advanced capacity in countries of concern for ten years, with clawback for breach, and they are negotiated award by award.
Check yourself
  1. A control covers an ECCN, but the foreign buyer rents the same capability from a cloud provider. What in the EAR reaches that, and what doesn't?
  2. Under 31 CFR Part 850, what makes a transaction prohibited rather than notifiable, and what does the knowledge standard demand of an investor?
  3. What binds a CHIPS award recipient after the money is disbursed?
  4. Name two revision triggers for a compute control that a successor administration could measure.

Answers. (1) Not the item controls: no controlled hardware is exported. Reaching it takes end-use and end-user rules, license conditions on the operator, KYC and reporting duties on providers, and the Entity List. The service itself sits outside the classic export framework. (2) The category of covered activity, not deal size: some technology categories are flatly prohibited, others notifiable within a deadline. Knowledge covers what the investor knew or should have known after a reasonable and diligent inquiry, so failing to ask is no defense. (3) The award's guardrails: no material expansion of advanced capacity in a country of concern for ten years, limits on joint research and technology licensing with entities of concern, plus reporting and clawback. (4) Examples: share of frontier-relevant compute reaching controlled destinations through third countries, and time-to-substitution for the controlled component, measured by the target's domestic yield and volume.

R8: Standards, conformity assessment, audit, and assurance

Read

  • AI Risk Management Framework (NIST, framework hub) — the framework, profiles, playbook, and limits on conformity claims. Mark every point where it declines to say what would count as passing.
  • ISO/IEC 42001, AI management system (standard page) — scope, structure, management system purpose, certification relationship, normative references. Write down what a certificate against it asserts about a model.
  • AI Accountability Framework (GAO, GAO-21-519SP) — governance, data, performance, monitoring, evidence questions, accountability roles. Flag which questions a vendor can refuse without penalty.

Do

The case. It is July 2025. The General-Purpose AI Code of Practice published on the 10th in three chapters, transparency, copyright, and safety and security, and the GPAI obligations apply on 2 August. You run policy at a frontier developer, and the choice is sign, sign one chapter, or decline. One hour, half a page: what adherence buys under Articles 53 and 55, what it concedes, and what declining costs a firm that still has to demonstrate compliance some other way. Then check the signatory list for who signed in part and who declined outright.

Design an assurance market for one high-consequence use: résumé screening, clinical triage, or credit adjudication. Trace the chain from law to standard to test method to laboratory to auditor to certification body to accreditation body to regulator to market surveillance to review. Name the conflict of interest at each handoff and the control for it.

Write the scheme itself: what object is assessed, by what method, against what evidence threshold, by whom, under what competence and independence rules, who accredits the assessors, who does surveillance after the certificate issues, what happens on failure, and how the assessed party appeals.

Done when every assurance claim in the scheme has a defined object, method, evidence threshold, accountable actor, conflict control, and consequence for failure.

Take away

  • Most AI assurance proposals name a standard and a regulator and leave the middle empty. No test method means no lab; no accreditation means no reason to trust the auditor; no surveillance means the certificate describes only the day it issued.
  • Different accreditations carry different claims. ISO/IEC 17021 governs bodies that certify management systems (what a 42001 certificate is), 17065 product certification, 17025 testing laboratories. A 42001 certificate says the organization keeps processes, records, and reviews, not that a model is accurate or safe.
  • The NIST framework is voluntary with no conformity assessment scheme attached. It gives vocabulary (Govern, Map, Measure, Manage) and profiles, including the Generative AI Profile, NIST AI 600-1. Nobody can be certified against it, so "aligned with the NIST AI RMF" is self-issued.
  • "Third-party audit" is not one thing. The auditee usually selects the auditor, pays the auditor, and defines the scope. Independence has to be engineered: accreditation, regulator-set minimum scope, rotation, a ban on advisory work for the audited entity, publication of adverse findings.
  • EU AI Act Article 40 gives harmonised standards a presumption of conformity; Article 43 lets most Annex III providers self-assess, with notified bodies mandatory only for some biometric systems. The technical content is therefore written in CEN-CENELEC JTC 21, late against the 2 August 2026 application date. R9 compares regimes.
Check yourself
  1. A vendor hands you an ISO/IEC 42001 certificate. What has been assessed, and what has not?
  2. Under the EU AI Act, who writes the technical content a high-risk provider must meet, and how does that content acquire legal force?
  3. Name three ways auditor independence fails, with one control for each.
  4. Why can the NIST AI RMF not produce a certificate?

Answers. (1) The management system: documented processes, assigned roles, records, and management review, assessed by a body accredited under ISO/IEC 17021. Not assessed: model accuracy, safety, or any performance claim about a deployed system. (2) CEN-CENELEC JTC 21 drafts harmonised standards under a Commission standardisation request. Once cited in the Official Journal, Article 40 gives conformity with them a presumption of conformity with the corresponding requirements, and Article 43 lets most Annex III providers self-assess against that. (3) The auditee pays and selects (accreditation plus mandatory rotation); the auditee scopes the audit (regulator-set minimum scope, published scope statements); the auditor sells consulting to the same client (ban advisory work, publish adverse findings). (4) It defines no requirements, thresholds, accredited assessors, or consequences. It is a process vocabulary, so any conformity claim against it is self-issued.

R9: Comparative and international governance

Read

  • Regulation (EU) 2024/1689, the AI Act (EUR-Lex) — prohibited practices, high-risk systems, general-purpose models, governance, enforcement, transition. Cite articles, not summaries.
  • Interim Measures for the Management of Generative AI Services (CAC, official text) — plus the algorithm recommendation, deep synthesis, labeling, filing, and ethics review measures. For each, write down the obligated party and the trigger.
  • Hiroshima AI Process reporting framework (OECD, portal) — version 2.0 and the 25 first-round reports, submitted at transparency.oecd.ai; next reports due 1 September 2026. List what version 2.0 changed.

Do

The case. Read three verification regimes from their annexes. New START: 18 on-site inspections a year, ten Type One and eight Type Two, unique identifiers on delivery vehicles, telemetry on up to five launches, a Bilateral Consultative Commission for disputes, and expiry on 5 February 2026 with no successor. IAEA safeguards: INFCIRC/153 accounts for declared material at declared facilities, INFCIRC/540 is opt-in for the rest. The BWC: a protocol negotiated from 1995 to 2001 and abandoned. One hour, half a page: which properties transfer to a compute-verification regime, which break, and on which disanalogy. Then read Baker (arXiv:2304.04123) and mark where your list differs.

Build a common-field comparison across the EU, China, the UK, Japan, Korea, and the US. Fields: regulatory object, legal authority, risk classification, registration or filing, testing duties, data duties, content controls, enforcement and penalties, industrial policy, security integration, international strategy. Three jurisdictions come with assigned texts; find the other three yourself (the UK has no cross-cutting AI statute, Japan's 2025 promotion act carries no penalties, Korea's AI Framework Act took effect 22 January 2026).

Then write the negotiation brief for a delegation you are staffing: one coordination mechanism that could bind, one irreducible conflict, red lines, and fallback positions.

Done when every cell cites a primary legal or official text with its current status, no jurisdiction is described in caricature, and each difference is explained by an institution or an incentive rather than by national temperament.

Take away

  • The regimes regulate different objects, so "stricter" is usually a category error. The EU regulates products placed on its market and tiers them by risk. China regulates services offered to the public inside China. The US works through sectoral agencies, procurement, and export controls.
  • The UK and Japan add no cross-cutting obligations and lean on existing regulators.
  • Enforcement leverage differs more than text does. The AI Act reaches 7% of worldwide turnover or €35M for prohibited practices and 3% or €15M for most provider obligations, with general-purpose model duties enforced centrally by the AI Office.
  • China's leverage is the filing and security-assessment regime plus the power to pull a service. Washington's is contract terms, agency authority, and licenses.
  • China's stack is layered: the Interim Measures (effective 15 August 2023) over the algorithm recommendation provisions (2022), deep synthesis provisions (2023), and synthetic-content labeling measures (effective 1 September 2025). Article 4's core socialist values requirement is the organizing purpose.
  • Voluntary international frameworks bind nothing and are still worth the hours. HAIP reports are self-drafted and unverified, and were barely comparable across firms until version 2.0 forced closed-ended questions. What they create is a dated public record you can quote back at a company.
  • Policy relevance: coordination binds where sovereignty costs are low and technical content is shared. Feasible: incident-report formats, evaluation methods, mutual recognition of test results. Not feasible: content rules rooted in incompatible speech regimes, or inspection of a rival's training runs. S6 has the verification mechanics.
Check yourself
  1. A Chinese firm trains a model and uses it internally, never offering it to the public. Which instruments bite, and why not the Interim Measures?
  2. Under the AI Act, who enforces general-purpose model obligations, and what is the ceiling for a prohibited practice?
  3. Name one coordination mechanism that could bind all six jurisdictions and one conflict that cannot be negotiated away.
  4. Why is a HAIP transparency report weak evidence of compliance and still useful to you?

Answers. (1) The Interim Measures cover generative AI services provided to the public within China, so internal or non-public use falls outside them; the firm is still reached by PIPL, DSL, CSL, and science-and-technology ethics review, and any public release would trigger filing, security assessment, and labeling. (2) The AI Office within the Commission for general-purpose models, national market surveillance authorities for the rest; prohibited practices reach €35M or 7% of worldwide annual turnover, whichever is higher. (3) Feasible: a common serious-incident report format and mutual recognition of test methods through the safety-institute network, both technical and low-cost to sovereignty. Irreducible: content requirements, where the Chinese values mandate and EU fundamental-rights law cannot both be satisfied by one product configuration. (4) It is self-reported, unverified, and carries no penalty for omission. It is also dated, public, and quotable, which makes it a commitment record and a baseline for drafting binding text.

R10: Productivity, labor, competition, and distribution

Read

  • Productivity data and measures (BLS, program page) — methods, series, revisions, industry detail, limitations. Find the industries where an AI effect would surface first.
  • National Economic Accounts (BEA, GDP data) — the output, investment, income, price, and industry tables. Locate data-center construction and software in the investment tables.
  • Cloud services market investigation (UK CMA, case page) — theories of harm, market definition, remedies, current outcome. Read one respondent submission alongside the inquiry group's finding on the same question.
  • Three causal studies: Noy & Zhang (Science, 2023), Brynjolfsson, Li & Raymond (NBER 31161), METR's developer RCT (METR, July 2025). Read each design before its result.

Do

Pick one major AI economic claim in circulation ("AI is raising US productivity growth", "AI is closing entry-level hiring") and test it against public data and at least one causal study. Build the evidence book so a colleague can rerun it: series, vintage, source table, every transformation, the code. Keep observed change, attribution, and forecast in separate labeled layers.

Then produce the distributional incidence table (which workers, firms, consumers, and regions gain and lose, and over what horizon), one forecast with a resolution date for the log, and a policy options memo.

Done when the analysis states its assumptions, tests at least two alternative explanations for the observed change, disaggregates affected groups instead of averaging them, and keeps welfare judgments separate from political feasibility.

Take away

  • The ladder runs capability, exposure, adoption, task redesign, firm effect, sector effect, aggregate effect, distribution. Each rung sheds most of the effect above it. Almost every bad AI economics claim jumps from rung one to rung seven.
  • The three experiments do not agree, and that is the lesson. Noy & Zhang (about 450 professionals, mid-level writing): time down 0.8 SD, quality up 0.4 SD, dispersion compressed. Brynjolfsson, Li & Raymond (5,179 support agents): 14% more issues resolved per hour, 34% for novices, near zero for the most experienced.
  • Self-reported gains are not evidence. METR (16 experienced open-source developers, 246 tasks) measured 19% slower with AI against an expected 24% speedup, and those developers still believed they had been sped up about 20%. Ask what was randomized and what was timed before you quote a percentage.
  • Measurement lags reality. BLS output per hour and total factor productivity arrive quarterly and annually with revisions, and intangible complements (retraining, process redesign, data work) depress measured productivity before they raise it.
  • Data-center capex enters GDP immediately as investment in structures, equipment, and IP products. That is growth in the accounts, not yet productivity.
  • Macro forecasts on the same evidence differ by an order of magnitude: Acemoglu puts ten-year TFP gains under 1%, Goldman Sachs put the effect near 1.5 points of annual productivity growth and roughly 7% of global GDP. The gap is assumptions about task coverage and cost per task, not a dispute about data.
  • Policy relevance: the near-term instruments are competition instruments. The CMA's inquiry group found market power in cloud and routed remedies into the strategic market status regime under the Digital Markets, Competition and Consumers Act 2024, so finding and remedy sit in different proceedings.
Check yourself
  1. A firm reports a 30% productivity gain from an AI tool. Name three things you need before that is evidence of an aggregate effect.
  2. Which of the three studies compress the skill distribution, which found a negative effect, and what design difference explains the sign?
  3. Where does data-center construction appear in the national accounts, and why does that mislead a productivity argument?
  4. Why did the CMA's finding of market power not itself produce remedies?

Answers. (1) Whether the comparison was randomized or self-reported; the population and task mix, since novice and expert effects differ in sign across these studies; and the firm's share of employment plus the diffusion rate, net of reallocation. Check also that the output measure is value added rather than issues closed. (2) Noy & Zhang and Brynjolfsson, Li & Raymond compress, because gains concentrate in workers whose unaided output was below what the model produces. METR is negative: expert developers on large repositories they know well, where model output needs review and rework and maintainers set the quality bar. Task familiarity and the review burden drive the sign. (3) As private fixed investment in structures, equipment, and intellectual property products, which adds to GDP in the year of construction whether or not anything downstream becomes more productive. Productivity requires output per hour to rise afterward. (4) The inquiry group recommended strategic market status investigations under the DMCC Act 2024; conduct requirements would be imposed in that separate proceeding, with its own designation process and appeal route.

R11: Energy, infrastructure, environment, and communities

Read

  • Docket RM26-4, Interconnection of Large Loads to the Interstate Transmission System (FERC, docket page) — notices, orders, comments, jurisdictional positions, thresholds, cost allocation. Read one utility comment and one consumer advocate comment on the same question.
  • Data center and electricity demand data (EIA, Form EIA-860M) — capacity, generator, load, and regional data with the definitions and revision notes. Work out what the form can and cannot tell you about load.
  • eGRID (EPA, program page) — the methods first, then subregional emissions, generation, and resource mix. Write the sentence you would use to describe a facility's emissions honestly with only this dataset.
  • Environmental Justice Index (CDC/ATSDR, EJI) — optional; the cumulative-burden screen. EPA's EJScreen was removed 5 February 2025 and survives only through the Public Environmental Data Partners mirror (screening-tools.com).

Do

The case. PUCO case 24-508-EL-ATA. AEP Ohio has proposed a tariff for new data-center load above 25 MW, the evidentiary hearing ran from December 2024 into January 2025, and a settlement signed by some parties and not others sits with the commission. You staff a commissioner. One hour, half a page: approve, modify, or reject, and say which costs the minimum-take percentage and the contract term allocate, who carries the risk if the load arrives late or never arrives, and what you would want on the record before the next case. Then check against the July 2025 order.

Evaluate one proposed large load, a real project in a live interconnection queue. Keep four layers separate and labeled: measured facts, engineering estimates, forecasts, policy judgments.

Produce the diligence model (energy, power, capacity, utilization, timing); the authority map covering FERC, the RTO or ISO, the state commission, the siting board, county zoning, and air and water permits, with every appeal path and deadline; a ratepayer and reliability briefing written for a commissioner; a lifecycle impact assessment using subregional data; and a community participation plan built on real comment dates.

Done when the product distinguishes energy from power and capacity, allocates costs to a cause, tests timing and utilization sensitivities, uses geographic rather than national-average evidence, and identifies every appeal path.

Skip if you have already built this for a real project. Then do only the authority map with appeal paths and filed comment deadlines.

Take away
