  • Energy, power, and capacity answer different questions. A 500 MW campus is a power rating; at 80% utilization it consumes roughly 3.5 TWh a year; capacity is what the system must hold available at peak. Tariff design needs the third, emissions math the second, headlines use the first.
  • Jurisdiction is the live question. FERC governs transmission service and wholesale sales under Federal Power Act sections 205 and 206. Retail service, generation siting, and distribution stay with the states under section 201(b). Large-load interconnection has been retail utility business, which is what RM26-4 unsettles.
  • Cost allocation follows cost causation: rates must be roughly commensurate with the burdens a customer imposes. The working instruments are a large-load tariff class, minimum-take and contract-term commitments, collateral, and exit fees, all for the case where the load arrives late, leaves early, or never shows.
  • Queue totals are an upper bound, not a forecast. The same project shops several utilities and holds speculative positions in more than one queue, so summing announced megawatts double-counts by construction.
  • EIA-860M inventories generators with planned online dates, so data-center demand has to be inferred from other evidence. eGRID publishes annual subregional averages with a long lag, and a new round-the-clock load is served by marginal units, so an average understates the emissions caused.
  • Policy relevance: NEPA attaches only where there is a federal handle, meaning federal land, a federal permit, or federal money, and Seven County Infrastructure Coalition v. Eagle County (2025) narrowed how far upstream and downstream an agency must look and told courts to defer on scoping.
  • The leverage left is state and local: commission tariff proceedings, air permits for backup generation and onsite turbines, water permits, and county zoning, each with its own clock.
Check yourself
  1. A developer announces a 1 GW campus. What else do you need to state its annual energy use and its capacity obligation?
  2. Which parts of the approval chain does FERC not control, and where does each order get appealed?
  3. Why is an eGRID subregional average the wrong number for "the emissions this data center causes"?
  4. Two utilities each report a 2 GW request from the same developer. How much demand is that?

Answers. (1) For energy: the load factor and the ramp schedule, since 1 GW at 80% is about 7 TWh a year and a phased build is much less in year one. For capacity: whether the load is coincident with system peak, whether it is firm or curtailable, and the reserve margin the system carries. (2) Retail rates, generation and facility siting, distribution service, land use, and most environmental permitting are state or local. A state commission order goes to rehearing and then to state court; a FERC order requires a rehearing request within 30 days before review in a federal court of appeals under FPA section 313. (3) It is an annual average across all generation in the subregion, while a new constant load is served at the margin by whatever unit dispatches next, and the average also ignores capacity built to serve it. Use a dispatch model or a marginal-emissions tool such as AVERT. (4) Somewhere between zero and 2 GW. Queue positions are options rather than commitments, and shopping one project across utilities inflates every regional total that adds them up.

R12: Implementation, budgeting, procurement, enforcement, and crisis

Read

  • OMB Memorandum M-25-22, Driving Efficient Acquisition of Artificial Intelligence in Government (PDF) — acquisition planning, data and IP, testing, risk allocation, portability. Read it beside M-24-18 and list what was dropped.
  • Federal Acquisition Regulation (acquisition.gov) — market research, acquisition planning, competition, contract types, data rights, quality assurance, termination, remedies. Copy the clause numbers you would insert.
  • Standards for Internal Control in the Federal Government (GAO, GAO-14-704G) — the five components and seventeen principles. Use them as the checklist your operating plan has to satisfy.

Do

The case. Six hours ago a frontier developer told your agency that a full set of model weights left its network. Attribution is uncertain, the exfiltration window is not yet bounded, and nothing is public. You brief the principal in one hour: what is known, what is inferred, what decision is needed today, and under which authority. Then write the list of things you needed on hand before that call and did not have. RAND's RRA2849-1 supplies the attacker tiers; SB 53's 15-day Cal OES clock and AI Act Article 55 supply the reporting duties.

Convert your capstone recommendation into an operating plan a real institution could execute: a budget with an account and fiscal-year structure; staffing with grades and realistic hiring timelines; the procurement clauses that produce the data and inspection access your policy assumes; an inspection plan; an enforcement ladder from information demand through penalty to referral; a dashboard with named owners; and a revision schedule with triggers.

Then run the crisis exercise. The core assumption fails at 2 a.m. on a holiday weekend: who decides what, under which authority, on what information, and what gets said publicly within six hours? Write the playbook from whatever the exercise broke.

Done when owners, resources, timelines, authorities, data, controls, consequences, and review triggers are explicit enough for a real institution to execute, and the crisis playbook assigns decision rights instead of directing people to coordinate.

The residency ends here. Snapshot the matrix cells this unit moved (implementation feasibility as a screen on every intervention family), then carry the package into C1.

Take away

  • Authority is not capacity. An authorization creates a program, an appropriation funds it, and the Antideficiency Act makes the gap legally sharp.
  • One-year versus no-year money, FTE ceilings, hiring lead times, and the bar on new starts under a continuing resolution decide whether your policy exists in year one.
  • Procurement is a governance instrument that reaches things rulemaking cannot. A contract clause buys inspection rights, data and model access, portability, and termination remedies, and it binds only the parties who sign.
  • M-25-22 pushes performance-based techniques (statements of objectives, performance work statements, quality assurance surveillance plans), lock-in protections through knowledge transfer and portability, pricing transparency, and standardized data and IP terms.
  • A memo is not enforcement. M-25-22 reaches solicitations issued 180 days after issuance, excludes national security systems, and enforces itself through agency policy updates on a 270-day clock. No penalties attach, the next director can rescind it as it rescinded M-24-18, and durability lives in the statutes and FAR clauses written underneath.
  • The Green Book is the standard your program gets audited against. GAO and the inspector general ask for evidence of controls rather than intentions, so the monitoring and deficiency-reporting path has to exist before launch.
  • Every rung of an enforcement ladder needs its own legal hook: information demand, inspection, warning, civil penalty with a statutory maximum, license or registration consequence, referral to DOJ. Skipping the hook is how enforcement plans die in litigation.
  • Policy relevance: incident reporting is where implementation becomes visible. California's SB 53 requires critical safety incident reports to Cal OES within 15 days; the EU AI Act requires them under Article 73 for high-risk systems and under Article 55 for systemic-risk models, to the AI Office. Both need somebody staffed to read what arrives.
Check yourself
  1. Your policy requires inspection of a vendor's training data. Where does that authority come from if the vendor sells to the government, and where if it does not?
  2. A program is authorized in June and the fiscal year opens under a continuing resolution. What can start and what cannot?
  3. Which contracts does M-25-22 reach, and what happens to it when the OMB director changes?
  4. Name the Green Book components and say which one a dashboard satisfies.

Answers. (1) From the contract: a clause negotiated before award, enforceable against that vendor with termination and remedies attached, which is why procurement is the fastest route to access. Otherwise you need a statutory grant of inspection, subpoena, or civil investigative demand authority, exercised through a rule that survives APA review. (2) A continuing resolution generally continues prior-year activities at prior rates and bars new starts and most reprogramming, so planning, drafting, and hiring against existing authority can proceed while obligating funds for a new program cannot, on pain of an Antideficiency Act violation. (3) Solicitations issued on or after 180 days from 3 April 2025 and options or extensions exercised after that date, excluding AI acquired as a component of a national security system. An OMB memo is rescindable at will, so anything you need to survive belongs in a FAR clause or a statute. (4) Control environment, risk assessment, control activities, information and communication, monitoring. A dashboard is information and communication plus monitoring; it is not a control activity, and treating it as one is a common finding.

C1: Position paper draft

Read

No new reading. When a branch sends you back to a dossier, an anchor source, or a matrix cell's citations, that is re-reading with a purpose.

Do

Pick one decision. Each is one sentence, live, and sits on an anchor.

  • Should Commerce tighten the January 2026 case-by-case licensing regime for H200-class accelerator exports back toward presumptive denial, hold it, or stop treating chip controls as the lead instrument as their leverage erodes?
  • Should frontier safety frameworks be codified, with California's transparency mandate scaled into a federal duty with defined thresholds, or left voluntary while the science of evals matures?
  • Should pre-deployment evaluation authority, including the power to move thresholds, sit with a government body rather than with the companies whose frameworks defined them?
  • Where should the open-weight release threshold sit, and should the FY2026 NDAA's exclusion of a leading Chinese open-weight family from defense and intelligence systems extend to critical infrastructure?
  • Should the US appropriate now for hardware-enabled governance mechanisms, building verification capacity for treaties that don't yet exist, on the logic that verification enables treaties?
  • Should datacenter siting and interconnection policy be used deliberately as a compute-concentration lever, or left to energy economics?

Off-menu is allowed on the same bar: one sentence, decision-shaped, a named actor with authority to act this year, and at least two serious worldviews that disagree about it.

Name one of S1's eight methods before you draft: contingency-plan ladder, branching scenarios, if-then architecture, portfolio-over-worldviews, fault tree, backward chaining, assumption-explicit decomposition, single-premise reductio. Let it discipline the paper's shape.

Write five sections in order.

  1. The decision question. One sentence naming the actor, the instrument and authority, and the horizon. "The US should take AI risk seriously" is not a decision; "BIS should amend the licensing posture for X-class accelerators by Y" is.
  2. Worldview conditionals. Two to four branches, each a named setting of the two or three of S1's twelve parameters that matter here, argued to a firm recommendation with the arithmetic shown.
  3. The recommendation. Yours, given your dated credences over the branches. Say what it costs in the branches where you are wrong.
  4. Cruxes. Which belief, if flipped, flips which recommendation.
  5. Update triggers. Predeclared observables with rough horizons, each entered in the forecast log.

Attach two appendices. The claim ledger gives every consequential claim in the body a row: claim · rung (measurement / inference / forecast / assurance / recommendation) · source · scope · your credence where the claim is yours. The evidence appendix freezes data, calculations, and sources.

Target 3,000–6,000 words plus appendices, method named in the opening section. Hand C2's red team this draft rather than a polished one.

Done when you can sample ten consequential claims from the body and find a ledger row for every one, at least two worldview branches reach different recommendations, and the cruxes section names, for each branch, what would flip it. Skip if the draft already exists and passes that test; nothing else passes.

Take away

  • The disagreement is the engine. A reader with different world-model parameters should locate their branch, follow it to a firm recommendation, and see which crux separates you from them.
  • If every worldview you take seriously converges, the decision is dead or the branches are strawmen. Convergence is a finding worth a paragraph, not a paper.
  • Conditional is not hedged. Each branch argues to a firm recommendation under its stated parameters. The conditionality lives in which branch is actual, not in how hard you commit inside one.
  • A crux is a belief: flip it and a branch's recommendation flips. An update trigger is an observable event that would flip a crux; it carries a rough horizon and a forecast-log entry.
  • Crux: "dangerous capabilities are measurable pre-deployment." Trigger: "a documented case of a deployed model shown to have sandbagged the pre-deployment eval it passed." A stated crux that flips nothing was never a crux.
  • Ledger rows go in while you draft. Retrofitting citations onto finished prose is how numbers get invented, and a paper that invents an ECCN teaches its reader anti-expertise.
  • The paper is the policy artifact: a named actor, a live instrument, a horizon, and a recommendation someone could act on this year. Write it for the reader who could act on it.
Check yourself
  1. Your two branches, scaling-paradigm-holds and normal-technology, both recommend keeping the case-by-case licensing regime. What does the acceptance test say, and what are the two possible diagnoses?
  2. "Compute thresholds decay as control points under continued algorithmic efficiency gains." Which rung, and what does its ledger row need before the claim can carry weight in a branch?
  3. Why must the method be named before drafting rather than identified afterward?

Answers. (1) The draft fails: at least two branches must reach different recommendations. Either the decision isn't live and every serious worldview converges, or the branches aren't distinct worldviews. Check the second first; convergence is often an artifact of branches built two clicks apart. (2) Forecast: it extrapolates a trend into a claim about future control-point viability. The row needs a source (the founding compute-governance paper lists this among its own defeaters), a scope naming the conditions (long timelines, the pace of algorithmic efficiency gains, decentralized-training feasibility), and your credence. A forecast can only carry a conditional recommendation, so it feeds a branch rather than the conclusion. (3) Declared first, the method forces moves the draft must then make: predeclared thresholds, feasibility-indexing, priced conjunctions. Retrofitted, its absence shows, in a "fault-tree" paper with no conjunction arithmetic.

C2: Red team and revise

Read

  • Klein, "Performing a Project Premortem" (HBR, September 2007) — all of it; it is two pages. Run the premortem as written, with stipulated failure and silent listing, before you reread your own draft.
  • The Alignment Faking external reviews (reviews PDF) — the W42 document, reread for form rather than content. Write one sentence per reviewer on what made the critique land.
  • ICD 203, Analytic Standards (ODNI, revised January 2015; dni.gov PDF, FAS mirror) — optional. The nine analytic tradecraft standards, especially the seventh. Read it as the rubric your revision log gets graded against.

Do

Every round writes rows into one red-team record: the attack in the attacker's voice, an axis tag, your written response, and a ruling of survived / revised (name the section) / conceded–unresolved.

Two axes. Technical: is the mechanism and evidence right? Institutional: is it implementable, given authority, venue, cost, enforcement? A finished record must carry both; a draft reviewed on one is half-reviewed.

Round 0, day one. Hand the draft to one named human and ask for one axis honestly covered; an IFP colleague reading for institutional realism is the intended case, and the tutor covers the other axis. Ask two specific questions: which section would you cut, and which claim would you bet against? Log name and date. Send on day one; their turnaround is the critical path.

Round 1, the three personas (TUTOR.md rule 10), at full strength with incentives attached. Write a response to every attack before ruling on it.

  • Frontier-lab GC: scope creep, safe-harbor gaps, liability exposure, the threshold that captures their safe system or waves a rival's through.
  • Accelerationist Hill staffer: cost, competitiveness, whose district pays, which committee has jurisdiction, why this instrument dies in markup.
  • Chinese MOFA analyst: reciprocity, verification asymmetry, what it signals, how a ministry white paper quotes your threshold back at you.

Rounds 2 and 3, the premortem and consider-the-opposite (rule 9). It is 2029, your recommendation was adopted and failed. Write the postmortem, naming the failure concretely: which world-model parameter broke, which actor defected, which measurement was gamed, which discretionary escape hatch got used.

Then take each load-bearing row of C1's claim ledger and ask whether you would have weighted this evidence the same had it cut the other way. The postmortem and every row that fails the symmetry check enter the record as attacks, with axis tags and rulings.

Round 4, revise, with the log. Every difference between the C1 draft and the revised draft enters the revision log tagged correction / judgment change / style, with a one-line reason and a pointer to the attack that forced it; style needs no attack.

Preserve verbatim what you could not answer; the strongest unresolved criticism travels to C3's defense. Ship one bundle: the revised draft, the record, and the log, with the human reader's name, date, and what their read changed.

Done when at least one attack produced a substantive revision traceable to a named attack; the log's tags separate corrections from judgment changes under spot-check, the tutor rerunning the sources behind two logged changes, either tag, with a correction hiding under a judgment tag failing the bundle; and a named human read the draft and left a trace in the record.

A record where every ruling reads "survived" fails, and so does a log that is all style. There is no pass task; the object under review is your own C1 draft.

Take away

  • Three kinds of change, three tags. A correction means your evidence was wrong, in a number, citation, authority, or mechanism, and anyone rerunning your sources converges on the fix.
  • A judgment change means the evidence stands but your weights moved, which is the analysis working rather than failing. Style is neither, tagged so it cannot pad the count.
  • The split matters downstream: in C3 and the annual what-I-got-wrong post, corrections are confessed and judgment changes are argued.
  • Surviving unchanged is not strength. A record where every attack reads "survived" means the attacks ran below full strength or the rulings weren't honest. Conceding everything is the symmetric failure, a draft whose claims were never priced.
  • Klein's result explains why stipulated failure works: people under-generate failure reasons for plans they own.
  • The premortem stipulates adopted-and-failed. A rejection postmortem collapses into politics and tests only your persuasion; adopted-and-failed holds the politics fixed and forces the recommendation's own failure mechanism into view.
  • ICD 203's seventh tradecraft standard, consistency with or change from previous analysis, is the revision log's standard applied to yourself.
  • Each persona is also a real downstream document: the GC's comment letter, the staffer's one-pager that kills your instrument in markup, the ministry statement quoting your threshold back. Running that response now costs an evening instead of a public walk-back.
  • The named human is the one part of the review no AI can flatter. DEPTH's counting rule is blunt: a quarter with no named-human exchange didn't count.
Check yourself
  1. Tag these three changes, and say why the distinction survives into C3: (a) your draft stated an enacted threshold one order of magnitude high against the statute's text; (b) after the MOFA round you weight verification asymmetry more heavily and downgrade "adopt" to "pilot"; (c) you moved the recommendation above the evidence summary because your reader stopped early.
  2. Why must the premortem stipulate adopted and failed rather than rejected?
  3. Match the attack to the persona structurally positioned to make it: (a) your reporting threshold also captures a class of systems you never meant to reach; (b) your recommendation requires an appropriation nobody will move; (c) your verification ask is non-reciprocal and reads as containment.
  4. The record shows twelve attacks and twelve "survived" rulings, and your human reader returned "looks right to me" with no notes. What does the acceptance test do with the bundle?

Answers. (1) (a) Correction: anyone rerunning the source converges on the fix. (b) Judgment change: the evidence stands, the weights moved. (c) Style: no attack required, tagged so it can't pad the count. The distinction survives to C3 and the annual what-I-got-wrong post, where corrections are confessed and judgment changes are argued. (2) Rejection tests persuasion, and its postmortem collapses into politics: wrong venue, wrong framing, wrong week. Adopted-and-failed holds the politics fixed and exposes the recommendation's own failure mechanism: broken parameter, defecting actor, gamed measurement, used escape hatch. (3) (a) The frontier-lab GC: compliance teams read definitions for scope, not intent. (b) The Hill staffer, whose terrain is appropriations and political economy. (c) The MOFA analyst, since reciprocity and signaling are what a ministry reads first. (4) It fails twice. All-survived means the personas ran below full strength or the rulings were advocacy dressed as refereeing; rerun the weakest round at full strength rather than invent a concession. "Looks right" leaves no trace in the record, so the named-human condition isn't met either.

C3: Brief, defend, publish

Read

  • ICD 203, Analytic Standards (ODNI; dni.gov PDF, FAS mirror) — Section D only: the five analytic standards and the nine tradecraft standards under the fifth. Pre-grade your C2-revised draft against tradecraft standards 1–3.
  • The assertion-evidence approach (Michael Alley, Penn State; assertion-evidence.com) — the tutorial plus one model presentation. Rewrite your three weakest slide titles as assertions before building the deck.
  • One serious opposing analysis (GovAI research) — optional. Find the strongest published analysis that cuts against your recommendation and write its case at full strength before defense day.

Do

The brief. Ten slides, ten minutes, for the principal your C1 decision question names. Follow the paper's spine at 10-to-1: decision (1) · why now (1) · branches with parameter settings visible (2–3) · the crux between them (1) · recommendation and its cost where you're wrong (1–2) · update triggers with horizons (1) · what you'd research next (1).

Every title an assertion, every body visual or numerical evidence, the claim ledger in your bag as the answer sheet rather than on the wall. Rehearse against the clock at least twice.

The defense. Twenty minutes of adversarial Q&A with the tutor in rule-10 mode: the principal played hostile, plus the frontier-lab GC, the accelerationist Hill staffer, and the Chinese MOFA analyst, each attacking from their incentives at full strength. Record or transcribe it.

Scoring is rule 4 applied to ICD 203's tradecraft standards; the tutor quotes the anchor for every score and names a concrete deficiency before any pass. Afterward, answer the two strongest failed questions in writing, or concede in writing.

Publish. The paper ships, through IFP editorial if the scope was negotiated as work product and your blog if not, tutor pre-reviewed against venue norms. It counts against DEPTH §5.5's six-to-eight pieces this year.

Then the portfolio note. DEPTH §7's application is two-shot by design, and the August cycle (Research Scholar, deadline 16 August 2026) was the baseline shot taken expecting rejection. If that cycle has passed, this paper is the work sample for the next one.

Done when the URL resolves publicly, and the defense record shows at least two questions marked no good answer, each with a written response or concession, questions attributed to their personas and ICD 203 scores carrying the tutor's quoted anchors.

Also required: a next-question note, half a page, naming the next flagship seed, argued in three sentences for tractability, neglectedness, and fit. Its gap must trace to one of those failed questions or to a ledger row the defense exposed, and be researchable with the methods you now have in months, not years.

Skip if you already have a live URL and a defense record that meet that test.

Take away

  • Compression is the final exam. A 5,000-word conditional paper at ten slides is roughly 10-to-1, and what survives is the load-bearing structure: the decision, the branches that disagree, the crux between them, the recommendation and its price.
  • If a slide's content won't compress to an assertion, it isn't a finding yet. It is information, and it goes in the appendix.
  • A clean defense is a failed defense. A transcript where every answer held is evidence about the questioning, not about the paper. Defending a conditional paper means some cruxes are genuinely open.
  • Defenders fail ICD 203's tradecraft standard 2 first, uncertainties expressed and explained rather than performed as hedging, then standard 3, assumptions distinguished from underlying evidence. Under pressure both collapse.
  • The week performs three real rooms: the briefing where decisions get made, the hostile Q&A where analysis gets tested, and publication, which is the only one the field can see.
  • The delta between the baseline application and the reapplication, in outcome and in reviewer feedback, is the cleanest external measurement available.
  • What continues after this page: the forecast log resolving, the annual what-I-got-wrong post, the monthly named-human exchange, the application cycles.
Check yourself
  1. Your transcript shows crisp, complete answers to every question asked. What does the acceptance test say, and what are the two possible diagnoses?
  2. A slide is titled "Compute thresholds: background and context." Name the rule it breaks and write the repair.
  3. The August application cycle closed months before your paper published. What is the paper now, and what is the measurement?
  4. What makes a next-question note fail even when the defense record passes?

Answers. (1) The record fails: at least two questions must have had no good answer. Either the questioning wasn't hostile, so rerun it with the personas at full strength, or it was hostile and you graded yourself generously, which is why rule 4 makes the tutor quote the anchor for every score. (2) It's a topic, not an assertion, a phrase headline hiding whether the slide has a finding. Repair: state the takeaway as a complete sentence ("Compute thresholds decay as control points, at rates you can now put numbers on") and let the body carry the evidence. If no such sentence exists, the slide is appendix material. (3) The work sample for the second shot of the two-shot application. The measurement is the delta, in outcome and reviewer feedback, between the baseline application and the reapplication. (4) When its gap is untraceable to the defense, a pet topic rather than a hole the room found, or intractable at your scale. A gap that fails tractability is a wish rather than a seed.

The matrix

A cell is one threat model set against one intervention family. It carries a sign (serves, ignores, or worsens), the conditional naming the world-model settings under which that sign holds, and the crux, the observation that would flip it.

Nineteen threat models by thirty-six intervention families is 684 cells. The maintained set is capped at roughly 50: the seven live debates, plus for each threat model the policy it uniquely motivates and the policy it actively disfavors. Value density collapses outside the contested cells, so completing the grid is not the goal and appears in no milestone.

The axes

Rows: 19 threat models, four families, sorted by what has to go wrong. Full dossier seeds in depth_threatmodels.json.

  • A. The AI's objectives. Fast-takeoff takeover · scheming · reward-hacking to emergent misalignment · WFLL-II correlated failure · rogue replication
  • B. Humans using AI against humans. Misuse-bio · misuse-cyber · AI-enabled coups · authoritarian lock-in · great-power war
  • C. Structural drift. WFLL-I proxy lock-in · gradual disempowerment · the intelligence curse · epistemic collapse · multi-agent flash dynamics · evolutionary selection · value lock-in
  • D. Meta-failures. Safety-tax collapse · s-risks

Columns: 36 intervention families, sorted by where in the causal chain they bite. The names below are the ones the seeded cells touch; depth_proposals.json carries the full set with July-2026 status and design cruxes.

  • Inputs. Export controls · compute thresholds and reporting · cloud KYC · HEMs · siting
  • Development process. RSPs · safety cases · licensing · weight security · audits · whistleblower protection
  • Artifact. Open-weight policy · structured access
  • Deployment. Agent governance · provenance · sectoral rules · incident reporting · liability · insurance
  • Ecosystem and international. AISIs · summits · UN track · treaties and verification · MAIM · moratorium · d/acc · windfall · public compute
  • Meta. Preemption and venue · lab structure · natsec-led approaches

Modulating parameters, 12 world-model variables that reweight every cell. Timelines · takeoff shape · polarity · alignment-difficulty distribution · offense-defense balance per domain · diffusion rate · compute concentration · warning-shot legibility · institutional response capacity · verification feasibility · delegation depth · labor-substitution rate

Three seeds name interventions that are not yet columns: control protocols, alignment research, and resilience or institutional design. Treat them as candidate columns; the table keeps each seed's own name.

The seeded cells

Sign is serves / ignores / worsens. Paired rows with opposite signs on the same cell are contested cells, and the crux sits in the gap between their conditionals.
