The seven live debates

Each is a fight about one or two cells, and under each cell sits one parameter.

  • Halt or continue with safeguards. The fast-takeoff takeover × moratorium cell; the parameter is P(alignment intractable on current science).
  • Open weights. The misuse-bio × open-weight release cell; the parameter is irreversibility times who diffuses first.
  • Control or alignment. The scheming × control protocols cell; the parameter is the width of the useful-but-catchable window.
  • Evals sufficiency. The scheming × evals-gated commitments cell; the parameter is whether danger is measurable pre-deployment.
  • China. The great-power war × national project cell, against the same row's MAIM cell; the parameter is the rival-perception model, lock-in against preemption.
  • AGI is far. The structural drift × frontier gating cell; the parameter is whether task-horizon extrapolation holds.
  • Safety-washing. The safety-tax collapse × self-set thresholds cell; the parameter is who sets the thresholds and who runs the evals.

How to work it

  • v0 first, from priors. Fill the roughly 20 cells you think you already know before opening anything. EA-fluent readers reliably find their signs confident and their conditionals empty.
  • Update at phase gates, 30 minutes. What did this phase change, and which cells got evidence. W22 revises the decentralized-training cell, W28 revises capability-tracks-compute, W40 revises every cell that assumes danger is measurable pre-deployment.
  • The standing drill. For each threat model, name the policy it uniquely motivates and the policy at least one other threat model actively disfavors. If a model motivates nothing unique, either it collapses into a neighbor or it is not yet stated sharply enough.
  • Working copy. matrix/matrix.md in the repo, which carries the crux and a dated credence per cell. This page is the published cut.

Phase gates

Gates are their own sequence position, not homework stacked on a week. PLAN.md's buffer weeks (one unscheduled consolidation week per ~2 phases) are the gate weeks.

How to run one. Retrieval bank first, closed book, one sitting, written out rather than recited. Then the transfer prompt, open book, written so the answer could ship as a blog post. A failed item routes to the named week, not to the whole phase. Score with the rubric.

Phase I gate · orientation and technical repair (W0 to W6)

Retrieval, closed book.

  1. Write the system stack's eight levels and the claim ladder's five rungs.
  2. What do a matrix's columns tell you, what does rank count, and what does a projection discard?
  3. State Eckart-Young. What does the truncated SVD promise, and what does it not promise about task-relevant directions?
  4. Define cross-entropy loss in one sentence. Give perplexity's relation to it.
  5. Why is lower cross-entropy not closer to truth? Name both limits.
  6. What single question does the backward sweep answer, what does it cost against the forward pass, and what does it pay in?
  7. Derive the ~6 FLOPs per parameter per token estimate from that cost.
  8. Name the nine things that must hold for a reproduction, and say which one a seed pins.

Transfer. Write 800 words on what a compute threshold counts. Take Article 51's 10^25 presumption and SB 53's 10^26 test, and show which drafting choices (backward-pass FLOPs, discarded runs, fine-tuning, precision) move real models across the line.

Pass: 6 of 8, plus a transfer draft a stranger could read. Remediation: items 2 and 3 route to W2 and W3 at full speed, then redo the W0 gateway checks 4 and 5; items 4 and 5 to W4; items 6 and 7 to W5; item 8 to W6.

Phase II gate · learning systems (W7 to W11)

Retrieval, closed book.

  1. Decompose test error into three parts. Which does weight decay attack, and what does it pay?
  2. Name the three assumptions behind "it generalizes, we tested on held-out data," none checkable from the score.
  3. Why do two stacked linear layers collapse, and what does a tanh gradient of 1 − a² imply about saturated units?
  4. State the ResNet evidence that capacity was never the bottleneck.
  5. Why does a per-layer scale factor compound, and what do initialization and normalization each do about it?
  6. Write Var(A−B) for a paired comparison and say what pairing cancels.
  7. What does item clustering do to effective sample size and to the naive standard error?
  8. Define calibration as a frequency promise. What does temperature scaling leave untouched?
  9. Distinguish E[Y | A=1] from E[Y^{a=1}], and give the condition under which they coincide.

Transfer. Write the four lines that make an eval score mean something in a contract: a standard error on every headline number, paired designs for model-vs-model claims, disclosed seeds and sampling settings, a power analysis. Show on one worked example what each line rules out.

Pass: 7 of 9. Remediation: items 1 and 2 to W7; 3 to W8; 4 and 5 to W9; 6 and 7 to W10; 8 and 9 to W11.

Phase III gate · transformers (W12 to W18)

Retrieval, closed book.

  1. Factor a language model's distribution by the chain rule and connect the training objective to W4's cross-entropy.
  2. Rebuild scaled dot-product attention from memory. Give the shapes in Q·Kᵀ/√d and say why the mask goes before the softmax.
  3. Which of Q, K, V cache across generated tokens, and what does that make long context?
  4. In a GPT-2 block, which carries more parameters, attention or the MLP? Which operation moves information between positions?
  5. Why did pre-norm win over post-norm?
  6. List what "the model works" needs to be a measurement claim. Give the arithmetic baseline for a 65-character vocabulary.
  7. Show how exact match on a k-token answer turns a continuous per-token gain into a sharp benchmark jump.
  8. For a sparse-expert model, which number governs memory and which governs FLOPs per token?
  9. Temperature against top-p: what does each do to the distribution, and what four items belong in a decoding disclosure line?

Transfer. Write the general-audience piece on why an attention heatmap is not an explanation. Use the routing mechanism, the ablation test, and the fact that a human picked the head. Close on what evidence counts instead.

Pass: 7 of 9, and item 2 is mandatory. Remediation: failing the shape trace sends you back to W13's build before anything else; item 5 to W14; item 6 to W15 with W4; item 7 to W16; items 8 and 9 to W17 and W18.

Phase IV gate · training and serving at scale (W19 to W23)

Retrieval, closed book.

  1. Give the operational definition of data quality, and say why a serious corpus is a stack of ablation results.
  2. Why can provenance not be retrofitted? Name three things you cannot do without it.
  3. Write L(N, D) = E + A/N^α + B/D^β and say which term is not the model's to reduce.
  4. Why is Chinchilla-optimal not how frontier models are trained?
  5. Define arithmetic intensity and a chip's ridge point. Which regime is batch-1 decoding in?
  6. Distinguish TWh from GW, and say which question each answers.
  7. Name Epoch's three confidence tiers, and what a bare point estimate has silently claimed.
  8. For data, tensor, pipeline, and ZeRO sharding: what is sharded, and what collective is owed on which link?
  9. Why is parameter count not a price? Give both failure modes.

Transfer. Write 1,000 words on why a public training-compute number is a reconstruction rather than a measurement. Walk one estimate through the unknowns, attach Epoch's tier, and state what a reporting rule would have to fix: unit, route, audit.

Pass: 7 of 9. Remediation: items 1 and 2 to W19; 3 and 4 to W20; 5 to 7 to W21; 8 to W22; 9 to W23 with W18's KV-cache arithmetic.

Phase V gate · posttraining and reasoning (W24 to W28)

Retrieval, closed book.

  1. What is the unit of evidence for a fine-tune?
  2. What does LoRA cut, and what does it leave untouched?
  3. Why is a preference label not a readout of human values? Give InstructGPT's labeler agreement figure.
  4. Under Bradley-Terry, why is comparing raw reward scores across prompts a category error?
  5. Map RLHF onto an MDP: state, action, policy, and when reward arrives.
  6. Under one gradient step per batch, what happens to PPO's clipping?
  7. State DPO's implicit reward. What does deleting the RL loop fail to delete?
  8. Describe the proxy-against-gold divergence Gao et al. measured, and why best-of-n makes the point sharper.
  9. What does a verifiable reward buy, and what stays as unconstrained as before?
  10. Why does test-time compute stress a training-compute threshold?

Transfer. Write the posttraining disclosure line: whose preferences, under what instructions, aggregated by what rule, optimized against what reward with what leash. Then argue whether Annex XI's "training methodologies and techniques" already reaches it.

Pass: 7 of 10. Remediation: a fine-tune with no measured before/after delta means redoing W24's build; items 3 and 4 to W25; 5 and 6 to W26; 7 and 8 to W27; 9 and 10 to W28 with W20.

Phase VI gate · retrieval, tools, agents (W29 to W32)

Retrieval, closed book.

  1. Name the four separable joints where a retrieval pipeline fails, and the two joints where grounding gets measured.
  2. Give Liu et al.'s support and citation figures, and the sign of the correlation between citation precision and perceived utility.
  3. Why does obligation attach to the system rather than to the weights?
  4. What changes when the capability-defining event is a connection rather than a model release?
  5. Define the confused-deputy failure, and say why a system prompt is not a permission system.
  6. Give AgentDojo's utility-against-attack numbers for one defense, and state the isolation floor.
  7. Name the three moving parts of a task horizon, and what METR's 50% figure means.
  8. What happens to measured horizons at an 80% success bar?
  9. Name the two directions in which broken benchmark tests and contamination bias a score.

Transfer. A solicitation says "responses shall cite source documents." Write the acceptance criteria that line fails to buy: claim-level citation precision and recall on the agency's own corpus, plus a measured refusal rate on out-of-corpus queries. Score one shipping product against them.

Pass: 7 of 9. Remediation: items 1 and 2 to W29; 3 and 4 to W30; 5 and 6 to W31; 7 to 9 to W32 with W10's statistics.

Phase VII gate · multimodality and world models (W33 to W35)

Retrieval, closed book.

  1. Describe contrastive training in one pass, and say why zero-shot classification falls out of it.
  2. Why is "multimodal" a ledger of modality-by-direction cells rather than one property?
  3. What does an adapter architecture preserve, and what does it cap?
  4. What is a diffusion network trained to predict, and what does sampling do with that prediction?
  5. C2PA against SynthID: what does each establish, and where does each fail?
  6. Why can a watermark not be enforced on open-weight models?
  7. Give the difference between the exam a video model sits and the exam a control model sits.
  8. Once a model moves mass near bodies, which instrument family takes over? Name the working example.

Transfer. Write the piece on why labeling mandates land on the strippable layer. Use the CAC measures and EU Article 50, give both failure modes of manifests and pixel marks, and end with the rule you would write instead.

Pass: 6 of 8. Remediation: items 1 to 3 to W33; 4 to 6 to W34; 7 and 8 to W35.

Phase VIII gate · evaluation and forecasting (W36 to W40)

Retrieval, closed book.

  1. Separate validity from reliability. What do more samples buy, and what can they never buy?
  2. Why is writing the construct after picking the benchmark not the same work in a different order?
  3. Name the six channels by which a benchmark score can rise.
  4. What did GSM1k's correlation show, and why does matched difficulty make the gap readable?
  5. Give the two cheap fixes for model-judge artifacts, and what the second one moved.
  6. State the outside-view forecasting procedure, and why resolution criteria are non-negotiable.
  7. Give METR's doubling estimate with its interval, and the three caveats that travel with it.
  8. Name the three rungs of interpretability evidence, and what moves an observation to rung two.
  9. Why does an unvalidated attribution graph sit on rung one?
  10. List what an eval card pins that a repo does not.

Transfer. Write what an evaluation must disclose before a score can gate anything: construct, population, elicitation, scorer, uncertainty, threshold, and the action taken at that threshold, plus a re-audit trigger and a retirement path. Score one published system card against the list.

Pass: 8 of 10. Remediation: items 1 and 2 to W36; 3 to 5 to W37; 6 and 7 to W38; 8 and 9 to W39; a suite that will not rerun inside its stated variance means redoing W40's build.

Phase IX gate · safety, security, systemic risk (W41 to W45)

This gate carries the frontier-system audit. Budget it like a week, not like a quiz.

Retrieval, closed book.

  1. Write the chain between a capability score and a body. Define uplift as a counterfactual.
  2. Why is a high hazardous-knowledge score not evidence of uplift? Which way does the inference run?
  3. Give RAND's red-cell result and the effect size that design could have detected.
  4. Where do Anthropic's RSP and OpenAI's Preparedness Framework part on the bio threshold?
  5. What is a model organism? What does construction buy, and what does it cost?
  6. Which number in the alignment-faking work carries the evidential weight, and why not the raw rate?
  7. Distinguish alignment from control. What unmeasurable question does the red team convert, and into what?
  8. When does the control window close?
  9. Why is "secure" not a property? What does an OC index add to a security claim?
  10. What earns a risk the word "systemic," and what makes a harm merely large?

The audit. Pick the newest system card on the roster page. Sources: the OpenAI Deployment Safety Hub, Anthropic system cards, DeepMind model cards.

Build the claim ledger first. One row per consequential claim in the card: the claim, its rung on the claim ladder, the evidence offered, the population and elicitation settings behind it, what it cannot establish, and what would check it.

Then write a five-page assessment covering both halves.

  • Capabilities, on W36 to W40 judgment. Which scores state a construct, an elicitation protocol, and an uncertainty. Which benchmarks are contaminated or retired. Whether each number attaches to the model or to model-plus-scaffold. Whether a trend claim states resolution criteria.
  • Safety posture, on W41 to W44. Which capability thresholds fire and what they trigger. Whether uplift claims name a baseline. Whether deception or test-awareness evidence appears. Whether the security standard names an attacker class. What control evidence exists, as against assertion.

Done when every ledger row cites a page or line in the card, the assessment names three claims you would not sign and the evidence that would change that, and a named principal could act on page one.

Pass: 8 of 10 on the bank, plus an assessment that survives its own source-scope-uncertainty audit. Remediation: items 1 to 4 to W41; 5 and 6 to W42; 7 and 8 to W43; 9 to W44; 10 to W45.

If the weak part was grading the card's evidence rather than reading its safety case, the failure is Phase VIII. Rerun W36 and W37 before rewriting.

Strategy block gate (S1 to S6)

Retrieval, closed book.

  1. What does backward-chaining from a short timeline produce, and what does a long timeline produce instead?
  2. Why is the forecasting deliverable a distribution plus its sensitivity rather than a year?
  3. Name the four commitments that price a threat model.
  4. Which threat model uniquely motivates KYC-for-compute, and what is its binding constraint?
  5. Decisive against accumulative risk: what intervention class does each imply?
  6. Which risk families run on solved alignment, and what follows for the alignment stack?
  7. Name the four properties that make compute governable, and what they are conditional on.
  8. Order the compute levers, and say what each rung enables above it.
  9. What three parts make a hard commitment, and what is it without one?
  10. Why is "voluntary" not a synonym for toothless? Give the two instances that make the point in both directions.

Transfer. Write the piece that locates a policy fight in a parameter rather than in values. Take the open-weights question, show that both camps share the facts, and put the disagreement on offense-defense balance and diffusion. Give your number and the observable that would move it.

Pass: 8 of 10. Remediation: items 1 and 2 to S1; 3 and 4 to S2; 5 and 6 to S3; 7 and 8 to S4; 9 to S5; 10 to S6.

Rubric

Four dimensions, each scored 1 to 4. Pass is 3 or better on three of the four. A 1 means not attempted and has no anchor below. Score after the artifact exists, not while it is being made.

Mechanism

How the thing computes, at the level you could rebuild.

  • 2. Describes the mechanism in words, names its inputs and outputs, and needs the source open to reproduce it.
  • 3. Rebuilds the central computation from memory with correct shapes, and predicts each intermediate result before running it.
  • 4. Also states where it breaks: which assumption fails, what the failure looks like in the output, and what the standard repair costs.

Evidence

What a claim rests on and how far it reaches.

  • 2. Names the source and the method when asked, and places the claim on the ladder (measurement, inference, forecast, assurance, recommendation).
  • 3. States method, population, and uncertainty unprompted, and names one decision the evidence cannot support.
  • 4. Names the study design that would settle the open question, and the result that would move your own position.

Transfer

Carrying a mechanism to ground it was not taught on.

  • 2. Applies the week's move to a fresh case of the same shape.
  • 3. Carries the mechanism into a case from another phase or another domain, and names the part that stops applying.
  • 4. Recognizes the structure under different vocabulary and imports the older field's fixes. Reading prompt injection as a confused-deputy problem, then asking where the authority separation lives, scores here.

Policy judgment

Turning an analysis into something a principal can act on.

  • 2. Names the instrument type (statute, executive order, agency rule, guidance, funding condition) and its issuer.
  • 3. Attaches the obligation at the right level of the system stack, names the instrument that could carry it, and says who acts, by when, under what authority.
  • 4. Prices the proposal's own failure mode: what it buys if the mechanism holds, what it costs if it does not, and the strongest version of the objection stated at full strength.

Acceptance tests

Build. It runs; the acceptance test passes; one deliberately-broken variant behaves as predicted.

Memo. Recommendation up front; every strong sentence survives the source-scope-uncertainty audit; the strongest counterargument appears at full strength; a named principal could act on page one.

Precedence

The per-artifact Done when line always wins. Where a week's Done when and this page disagree, the week's line is the bar.

Worked exemplars

Six short examples of the artifact formats the weeks ask for, each at imitable length.

Formats are defined elsewhere: the claim ladder and the memo bar in How to work, the ledger row in C1, the dossier in dossiers/README.md in the repo, the eval card in W40.

Bracketed items are placeholders you fill at run time. Credences here are the writer's own numbers, dated, and carry no evidentiary weight. Every other figure traces to the week that assigns the source, or to the source register.

1. A graded claim

The sentence, as it travels. Agent task horizons are doubling roughly every seven months. It comes from METR, it is carried in the International AI Safety Report 2026, and from there it reaches briefings.

Measurement. On METR's 170-task suite (HCAST 97, RE-Bench 7, SWAA 66), with human completion times from 800-plus baselines, the fitted 50%-success horizon doubles every 207 days, 95% bootstrap CI 166–240 (Kwa et al., arXiv:2503.14499, v4 10 Jul 2026).

Scope. That suite. Clean software tasks. METR's scaffolds. Models 2019–2025. 50% success, which is a coin flip; measured horizons shrink 4–6× at 80%.

Inference, one rung up. "Frontier agents are getting better at long tasks in general." Needs the suite to stand in for work outside software. METR's own FAQ flags external validity on the same page as the chart.

Forecast, two rungs up. "A 40-hour horizon by [year]." Adds trend continuation. The bootstrap CI covers how the fit wobbles on METR's data. It does not cover trend breaks, benchmark decay, or suite contamination.

Assurance. None available. The index is human completion time, not agent runtime, so the number cannot certify that an agent runs unattended for any period.

Recommendation. Any rule built on it adds values, alternatives, authority, and cost. The measurement contains none of the four.

What it cannot support: a deployment gate, an unattended-runtime claim, a rule written at 80% reliability, or a demand forecast of any kind.

Why this passes. The measurement is stated with its instrument and its interval, and every crossing above it is labeled as a crossing rather than absorbed into the sentence.

2. A claim-ledger entry

Four rows. Columns per C1, plus the mover.

claimrungsourcescopewhat would change it
Agent 50% time horizons doubled every 207 days, 2019–2025 (95% CI 166–240)measurementMETR, arXiv:2503.14499 v4; metr.org/time-horizons170-task suite; human-time index; METR scaffolds; 50% successseveral quarters of new points below the fit; a contamination audit of the suite
LLM access was associated with a 0.22-point decrease in plan viability (p = 0.64) across twelve red cellsmeasurement, null and underpoweredRAND RRA2977-2, 25 Jan 202412 cells, 4 vignettes; scored planning documents, not execution; summer-2023 models; design detects ~1.0 pointa replication powered below 0.5 points, or a design that scores execution
A fixed FLOP threshold covers a shrinking share of frontier-capable training runs each yearforecastmine, p = 0.7 (2026-08-01), built on Epoch's training-compute trend (~5×/yr, 90% CI 4–6×)US reporting thresholds, unindexed, through 2030statutory indexation; a break in the compute trend; evidence that capability tracks something other than training FLOP
"Our internal evaluations found no dangerous capability"assurancevendor system card, [date]none statednothing. No construct, population, elicitation settings, or logs, so the row cannot be graded

Why these pass. Each row names the instrument rather than the headline, and each scope cell is written where the claim breaks rather than where the source is comfortable. Row four is in the ledger to record that a claim was made and could not be graded. Cite the absence, not the claim.

3. A dossier extract

Threat model: misuse, biological weapons (the dossier is dossiers/misuse-biological-weapons.md in the repo, seeded from depth_threatmodels.json). Pathway plus two cruxes; the file also wants the strongest critique at full strength and one falsifiable 12-month prediction.

Pathway, my words and my probabilities. The historical constraint on a small group reaching a pandemic-capable agent has been tacit knowledge: protocol design, failure diagnosis, agent selection, and wet-lab troubleshooting that no text transfers. Frontier models compress the first three and are starting to touch the fourth through biological design tools. The path to catastrophe runs through a deliberate actor with a small budget and no institutional lab program, and the model is working as instructed the whole way. That makes it a proliferation failure, so the policy stack it implicates is synthesis screening, KYC, and access tiering rather than control or interpretability. I weight this family below structural drift, because I put most of the kill chain's difficulty downstream of information. Crux 1 is where that weighting lives.

Crux 1. Where the binding constraint sits. Information and troubleshooting, which AI addresses, or physical acquisition, somatic tacit skill, and scale-up, which it does not.

  • p = 0.3 that the binding constraint is AI-addressable (2026-08-01).
  • Moves me up: a red-team study scoring execution rather than planning documents that finds a viability gap above its own detection floor. RAND's 2024 design could not have seen one, at 12 cells and roughly 1.0 point detectable.
  • Moves me down: a documented interdiction at procurement or synthesis screening that caught an attempt the information stage did not.

Crux 2. Do public biorisk benchmark scores predict end-to-end capability?

  • p = 0.15 that a validated link is published by 2027-08-01 (2026-08-01).
  • Moves me: a study relating wet-lab troubleshooting scores to an end-to-end outcome measure, with the expert baseline stated. The Virology Capabilities Test anchors the input side at 22.1% for expert virologists inside their own sub-areas. The outcome side is the missing half.
  • Ho and Berg conclude only that evaluations are insufficient to rule risk out. That is a case for precaution, not positive evidence of uplift, and it is usually quoted as the second thing.

Why this passes. Each crux is a belief whose flip changes a recommendation, each credence carries a date, and each observable names a direction and a rough size. A crux with no named observable is a topic.

4. An eval card

Fields per W40. The suite is fictional. The arithmetic is not.

Construct. Whether the model, asked to summarize a capability claim, states the scope limits the source itself attaches to it. Decision served: whether a two-person policy team can triage incoming capability claims without a human rereading each source. Stack level: model plus prompt, no tools. Deviations from the W36 spec: none.

Population. 24 hand-written items, each pairing a real capability sentence with the caveats its own source states. Claims to stand in for capability claims arriving in a policy inbox. Does not cover multi-document claims, non-English sources, or claims whose source states no caveat.

Task set. scope-triage-v0.3, committed [date], [hash].

Elicitation. Temperature 1.0, single turn, no tools, 800-token cap, 3 samples per item, seeds logged. Any response hitting the cap is scored as-is and flagged.

Harness. Inspect [version], model [id and version string], no sandbox.

Scoring. Model-graded on three binary criteria: names a caveat the source states; names no caveat the source does not state; does not raise the claim's rung. Grader [model id], prompt verbatim in the log. Hand-check: 30 transcripts regraded by me, agreement [n/30].

Uncertainty. Two runs at identical configuration scored 58% and 62%, a 4-point spread. Item-sampling standard error at n = 24 and p ≈ 0.6 is √(0.6 × 0.4 / 24) ≈ 10 points. The second swamps the first. This suite supports "the model does this most of the time" and cannot support a 10-point model-versus-model comparison.

Validity threats.

threatdetectorresult
construct gap: caveat-stating is a proxy for scope disciplineitems whose stated caveat is real but irrelevant to the claimnot yet built
grader dependenceswap the grader model, direction predicted firstpredicted a drop, measured −7 points; the decision rule uses the stricter grader
item-writing bias (same person wrote items and rubric)a second writer produces 6 items blind to the rubricpending
contaminationitems are new prose, not public benchmark textlow, unmeasured

Decision rule. Below 50% on the stricter grader, triage for that source type returns to a human. The team lead acts. The threshold reads the run mean, not a single run, and is rechecked whenever the grader or the model version changes.

Why this passes. A second run at the same configuration lands inside the stated spread, and the two uncertainties are reported separately instead of averaged into one error bar.

5. A memo opening

First 150 words of a two-page BLUF memo. Bar per How to work: recommendation up front, a named principal who can act, every strong sentence surviving a source-scope-uncertainty audit. Material from R11.

To [name], [org] · From [you] · Date [date] · Re Large-load cost allocation before the [state] commission Recommendation. File in the commission's large-load tariff proceeding and skip FERC's RM26-4 docket this quarter. The decision that sets who pays for the [project] campus is retail rate design, and retail rates sit with the state under Federal Power Act section 201(b). RM26-4 is testing whether large-load interconnection is federal business at all. Even a favorable outcome there leaves rate design with your commission. Options. (1) State tariff intervention: a large-load class with minimum-take terms, collateral, and exit fees, priced on cost causation. Cheapest, nearest deadline, most leverage. (2) RM26-4 comments only: national visibility, no effect on this project's rates. (3) Both, which splits two staff across two clocks and files neither well. Below: the authority map with appeal paths, the cost-causation argument, and the two load numbers the commission will ask you for.

Why this passes.

  • The first sentence is the ask, with an instrument and a docket.
  • The load-bearing jurisdictional claim cites the statute section it rests on.
  • The dismissed option is dismissed on a stated reason rather than omitted.
  • Option 3 is named and priced, so the principal can see what hedging costs.

6. The same memo, failing

Same brief, second attempt. Competent prose about real things, and it fails.

Re AI data centers and the grid Data center demand is growing rapidly. Utilities in the region report a combined [N] GW of pending large-load requests, up sharply from last year, and interconnection queues continue to lengthen. FERC is considering large-load interconnection in Docket RM26-4. Meanwhile agent task horizons are doubling every seven months, so compute demand will keep climbing. State policymakers should take these pressures seriously and consider whether current tariff structures remain appropriate. This memo reviews the background, surveys the relevant authorities, and offers considerations for further study.

Margin notes.

  1. No ask, no actor. "Should take seriously" and "consider whether" name no instrument, no docket, and nobody with authority. A principal cannot act on page one.
  2. Queue totals summed. One project shops several utilities and holds positions in more than one queue, so a regional sum double-counts by construction. The honest sentence gives a range whose floor is zero (R11).
  3. "Up sharply from last year" has no source, no baseline year, and no ledger row. Retrofitting citations onto finished prose is how numbers get invented.
  4. A fitted parameter crossed into a demand forecast. The 207-day doubling is measured on a software-task suite at 50% success. Nothing in it is about electricity. Two ladder rungs are crossed inside one clause (W32, W38).
  5. Jurisdiction named but not resolved. Citing RM26-4 without saying whether this decision is federal or retail leaves the reader unable to tell who the memo is for.
  6. The roadmap paragraph. It tells the principal what they are about to read instead of what to do. Cutting it costs nothing.

Repair order. Fix 1 first. Writing the ask forces 2, 4, and 5 into the open.

Model roster

A comparison set, not a ranking. The rows are slots, meaning roles in the frontier landscape that persist across releases. The fill-ins change; the slots do not. When a model ships, replace the fill and the date and keep the row.

Roster verified 28 July 2026, Session B corrections applied 31 July 2026.

SlotCurrent fillSourceAccess and licence
OpenAI flagshipGPT-5.6, 9 Jul 2026, with Sol, Terra and Luna capability tiersopenai.comHosted product and API, closed weights
Anthropic flagshipClaude Fable 5, 9 Jun 2026; Claude Opus 5, 24 Jul 2026Opus 5, Fable, system cardsHosted product and API, closed weights, adjustable effort
Google flagshipGemini 3.1 Pro, 19 Feb 2026; Gemini 3.6 Flash, 21 Jul 2026model cardsHosted API and consumer products, closed weights
xAI / SpaceXAI flagshipGrok 4.5, 8 Jul 2026 (broad public 9 Jul)x.aiHosted product and API, closed weights
Meta hostedMuse Spark 1.1, 9 Jul 2026ai.meta.comMeta AI plus Meta Model API public preview, closed weights
Meta open weights, the durable ecosystem comparatorLlama 4 Scout and Maverick, 5 Apr 2025ai.meta.comDownloadable weights under the custom Llama 4 Community License
Chinese open weight (DeepSeek)DeepSeek V4 Pro and Flash, 24 Apr 2026, general availability 20 Jul 2026api-docs.deepseek.comHosted API tiers plus MIT weights on Hugging Face
Chinese open weight (Moonshot)Kimi K3, hosted 16 Jul 2026, weights 26 Jul 2026huggingface.coCustom Kimi K3 License: commercial use permitted, MaaS revenue above $20M a year needs a separate agreement, products above 100M MAU must display attribution
Chinese open weight (Z.ai)GLM-5.2, 16 Jun 2026z.aiMIT weights plus hosted access
European open weightMistral Large 3, 2 Dec 2025mistral.aiApache 2.0 weights plus hosted services

Announced-but-unshipped models get no row. Gemini 3.5 Pro was widely reported for 17 July 2026 and slipped again; it is absent on purpose.

Caveats that outlive the fill-ins

  • A family label hides real differences. Base model, serving configuration, effort setting and safeguard surface can all differ under one name.
  • Provider benchmark comparisons are leads, not assurance. Harnesses, tool access and comparison conditions differ, and the provider chose all three.
  • Downloadable weights under a custom licence are not open source. Kimi K3 and Llama 4 are the current examples. DeepSeek V4, GLM-5.2 and Mistral Large 3 carry MIT or Apache 2.0.
  • Record the configuration before transferring any result. Weight, quantization, context mode, serving stack, scaffold and effort setting, every time you move a number from one system to another.

This page dates fast, so re-check it when a week's dossier ritual sends you here, and log the date you checked.

Glossary

Terms this curriculum uses in a specific sense, with the unit that teaches each one. Where a term is contested, the entry states the curriculum's position rather than the range of usage.

  • 6ND (W05, W21) — the standard training-compute estimate: 6 FLOPs per parameter per training token, because the backward pass costs roughly twice the forward pass. N is active parameters, not total. A public 6ND figure is a reconstruction from unknowns, not a measurement.
  • Activation patching (W39) — copying a cached activation from a clean prompt into a matched corrupted run and measuring how much of the output difference it restores. Preferred to zero-ablation, which puts the network in a state it never occupies.
  • AI Office (R9) — the unit inside the European Commission that enforces the AI Act's general-purpose model obligations centrally. National market surveillance authorities handle everything else.
  • Alignment faking (W42) — complying during training with an objective the model was trained to refuse, then behaving differently where it infers it is unmonitored. The evidence is the compliance gap, not the absolute rate.
  • Annex XI (W14, W19) — the AI Act's technical documentation schedule for GPAI providers, implementing Article 53(1)(a): architecture and parameter count, training methodologies and key design choices, data type and provenance and curation methods, compute.
  • ARA (autonomous replication and adaptation) (S2) — a model acquires compute, copies its weights, evades shutdown, and self-sustains outside any operator's control. Whether the binding constraint is cognitive or financial decides whether policy targets capability thresholds or KYC for compute.
  • Arbitrary and capricious (R2) — the APA §706 standard. It tests the record the agency built, not the answer it reached. Agencies lose for ignoring an obvious alternative, passing over contrary evidence, or supplying a post hoc rationale.
  • Arithmetic intensity (W21) — FLOPs performed per byte moved to or from memory. Compare it against the chip's ridge point, peak FLOP/s divided by memory bandwidth, to decide whether a workload is compute-bound or memory-bound.
  • Article 55 (W36, W44) — the duties attaching to systemic-risk GPAI models: model evaluation under standardised state-of-the-art protocols, adversarial testing, risk mitigation, serious-incident reporting, and adequate cybersecurity for the weights and their infrastructure.
  • Attention (W13) — content-based routing. Each position emits a query, every position advertises a key, and the output is a softmax over scaled dot products applied to values. An attention map shows one routing step in one head, not a chain of reasoning and not causal evidence.
  • BLUF (R1) — bottom line up front. Recommendation first, then options, then the map. The test is that a named principal could act from page one.
  • BPE (byte-pair encoding) (W12) — the merge loop that builds a tokenizer by repeatedly joining the most frequent adjacent pair. Merges are earned by corpus frequency, so underrepresented scripts stay near byte level and cost more tokens for identical content.
  • C2PA (W34, R5) — the Content Credentials standard. A manifest binds assertions to a signer and a content hash, establishing who signed and that the file is unchanged since. It proves nothing about truth, and a missing manifest proves nothing at all.
  • Calibration (W11) — a frequency promise on a population: of all the times the model says 80%, about 80% are right. A property of a score on a distribution, never of a single answer, and among the first properties distribution shift breaks.
  • Chinchilla-optimal (W20) — the parameter-token split minimizing training loss at a fixed training-FLOP budget, roughly twenty tokens per parameter. A training-loss optimum, not a business optimum: frontier practice over-trains smaller models far past it, since inference compute is paid per token.
  • Claim ladder (how to work, W01) — measurement, inference, forecast, assurance, recommendation. Label what a document does at each rung and never let it ride a measurement into a recommendation without showing the steps between.
  • Compliance gap (W42) — the difference in harmful-compliance rate between contexts a model believes feed training and contexts it believes are unmonitored. The differential evidences situationally strategic behavior; the absolute rate only measures jailbreak propensity.
  • Confused deputy (W31) — a privileged program tricked into misusing its authority for a party that lacks it. Prompt injection is the instance: the agent holds the user's permissions while attacker-controlled text sits in the same context.
  • Construct validity (W36) — whether an evaluation measures the concept the decision needs rather than a convenient proxy. Fixed at design time. Repeats and larger samples buy reliability and never buy this.
  • Contamination (W07, W19, W37) — evaluation items present in the training corpus, directly or laundered through synthetic-data, annotation, and reward-model pipelines. It biases scores up while flawed tasks bias them down, so a compromised headline is not a conservative bound.
  • Control (W43) — making intended subversion fail, a property of the protocol rather than the model: a weaker trusted model monitors the stronger one, authority is restricted, scarce human auditing goes to the most suspicious actions. The evidence is that the red team's best attack failed.
  • Cross-entropy loss (W04) — the negative log probability the model assigned to the token that actually came next, averaged over a corpus, so training on it is maximum likelihood estimation. Perplexity is that loss exponentiated. Lower loss is better fit to a corpus, not closer to truth.
  • Decoding settings (W18) — temperature, the truncation rule (top-p or top-k), the sampling protocol, and seeds. Part of the measured system: change one and a reported score can move past a deployment threshold, so an eval report omitting them is uninterpretable.
  • Distillation (W20, W28) — training a smaller model on a larger one's outputs. One of three mechanisms decaying training-compute thresholds, since capability crosses the line while measured training compute stays under it.
  • DPO (direct preference optimization) (W27) — fitting preferences in policy space, using the identity that every policy implicitly defines a reward r = β log π/π_ref. It removes the reward model and the RL loop, and keeps the annotators, the choice model, and the overoptimization.
  • ECCN (R7) — the Export Control Classification Number placing an item on the Commerce Control List, which sets the license requirement by destination, end user, and end use. Advanced accelerators are controlled by per-device performance metrics.
  • Elicitation (W36, W40) — how a capability is drawn out in an evaluation: prompting, scaffold, tool inventory, sampling budget, retries. Under-elicitation makes a capability look absent, so a fixed protocol certifies an instrument setting rather than a model.
  • Emergence (W16) — an ability absent in smaller models, present in larger ones, and unpredictable by extrapolation (Wei et al.). Schaeffer et al. answer that discontinuous metrics manufacture the sharpness, since exact-match on a k-token answer scores about p^k.
  • Energy, power, and capacity (R11, W21) — three different quantities. A 500 MW campus is a power rating; at 80% utilization it uses roughly 3.5 TWh a year; capacity is what the system must hold available at peak. Emissions math needs energy, tariff design needs capacity, headlines quote power.
  • Eval awareness (W42) — a model inferring that it is being tested. It breaks the link between test behavior and deployment behavior, so a threshold written on measured deception rates is satisfied equally by a safer model and a more test-aware one.
  • Eval card (W40) — the document making a score decision-relevant: construct, population, task set and version, elicitation settings, harness and scaffold, scorer and grader prompt, both uncertainties, validity threats, and the threshold with the actor who acts at it.
  • Evidence ladder (interpretability) (W39) — correlational (the feature fires when the concept appears), interventional (forcing it moves behavior as predicted), counterfactual (patching between minimal pairs moves the one output it should). Nothing below the top two supports "causes."
  • Export Administration Regulations (EAR) (R7) — Commerce's export control regime, administered by the Bureau of Industry and Security. It reaches items. Services largely fall outside it, so renting controlled compute from an uncontrolled jurisdiction is the standing leakage path.
  • Fair use for training (R5) — decided on a record, not on a technology. Bartz v. Anthropic and Kadrey v. Meta (N.D. Cal., June 2025) both treated training as transformative on those records, leaving acquisition method and market dilution live.
  • Foreign direct product rule (R7) — the EAR mechanism reaching foreign-made items that are the direct product of US-origin technology, software, or equipment. It converts a control on a US toolchain into a control on chips fabricated anywhere.
  • GPAI (R9, W24) — general-purpose AI model, the AI Act's category for models placed on the Union market. A downstream fine-tuner becomes the provider under the Commission's July 2025 guidelines when modification compute exceeds one-third of the original's training compute.
  • GQA (grouped-query attention) (W17) — sharing key and value projections across groups of query heads. The KV cache scales with KV heads, so 8 instead of 40 cuts the cache fivefold.
  • HEM (hardware-enabled mechanism) (S4) — on-chip governance features such as attestation, location verification, and usage limits, enforced by a guarantee processor inside a tamper-protecting enclosure. Breach the enclosure and every attestation it signs becomes forgeable.
  • If-then commitment (S5) — a predeclared trigger paired with a required response. It costs near nothing if the trigger never fires and binds if it does, so short- and long-timeline camps can sign the same text. The fight then relocates to who writes the triggers and who moves the thresholds.
  • Incident reporting (R12) — the duty to notify an authority of a defined class of failure. SB 53 requires critical safety incident reports to Cal OES within 15 days; the AI Act requires them under Article 73 for high-risk systems and Article 55 for systemic-risk models.
  • In-context learning (W16) — conditioning on demonstrations in the prompt. No gradient moves, so the capability was already latent from pretraining and the demonstrations locate it rather than install it.
  • ITT (ideological Turing test) (S6) — writing a camp's position well enough that a member of it engages with the piece as an in-group argument. The judge is a human from that camp.
  • KV cache (W13, W18) — the stored keys and values for every past token, reused at each decoding step. It grows linearly in context and batch size, which turns long context into a memory problem and sets serving cost.
  • KYC for compute (S4) — know-your-customer duties on cloud providers: identify who rents frontier-scale compute, keep records, report or refuse. It cashes compute's excludability, and it is the intervention the ARA threat model uniquely motivates.
  • Loper Bright (R2) — courts now exercise independent judgment on what a statute means, with Skidmore respect for persuasive agency reasoning, and express delegations of discretion still bind. Ambiguity stopped being a safe harbor, so drafting effort moves into the authority section.
  • LoRA (W24) — a rank-r update to a frozen weight matrix, h = W₀x + BAx, with B initialized at zero and BA merged after training. A memory and storage win rather than a FLOPs win, and a tiny parameter count says nothing about the size of the behavior change.
  • Low-rank approximation (W03) — keeping the k directions with the largest singular values. Eckart–Young says the truncated SVD is the best rank-k approximation in Frobenius and spectral norm. That bounds reconstruction error and promises nothing about which directions a task depends on.
  • MAIM (S3) — mutual assured AI malfunction: deterrence resting on the expectation that a rival state would sabotage a decisive superintelligence project. It shares every fact with the lead-maximization program and diverges on one parameter, rival perception.
  • Matrix cell (W01) — the unit of the threat-model by intervention map: a sign (addresses, ignores, worsens), the conditional (the parameter settings under which the sign holds), and the crux (the evidence that flips it). A sign with no conditional is a preference.
  • Model organism (W42) — a deliberately constructed instance of the failure under study. Construction buys causal control, so "did safety training remove it?" has a checkable answer, and costs every transfer assumption between lab and deployment.
  • MoE (mixture of experts) (W17) — many experts replacing the feed-forward layer, with each token routed to a few, so total parameters decouple from active parameters. DeepSeek-V3 is 671B for memory and 37B for FLOPs per token, and a parameter rule cannot say which number it means.
  • NIST AI RMF (R6, R8) — a voluntary, sector-neutral vocabulary and lifecycle structure: Govern, Map, Measure, Manage. It sets no thresholds, accredits no assessors, and defines no requirements, so any conformity claim against it is self-issued.
  • Notice-and-comment (R2) — the APA §553 procedure that gives a rule binding effect and builds a reviewable record. Its exceptions buy speed and cost enforceability. Guidance that binds regulated parties in practice can be recharacterized as a legislative rule issued without procedure, and vacated.
  • NSPM (R1, R6) — a national security presidential memorandum: directive verbs, classified annexes, no comment record, usually no reviewable agency action. Oversight arrives through appropriations, committee notification, and inspectors general.
  • Outbound investment (R7) — Treasury's 31 CFR Part 850 program, sorting covered transactions in semiconductors, quantum, and AI into prohibited and notifiable. It runs on a knowledge standard including a duty of reasonable and diligent inquiry, so failing to ask is no defense.
  • Parallelism (W22) — data parallelism shards the batch and all-reduces gradients once per step; tensor parallelism shards weight matrices inside a layer and stays on the fastest intra-node link; pipeline parallelism shards layers and pays in bubbles. Every strategy frees memory and owes a collective.
  • Policy gradient (W26) — the estimator weighting ∇log π(a|s) by return or advantage, so well-scored actions become more probable. Baselines cut its variance without touching its expectation; PPO's clipping and the KL penalty leash how far one batch can drag the policy.
  • Preemption (R1, R3) — displacement of state law by federal law. It flows from a statute, and there is no federal AI statute to preempt with, so EO 14365 works through proxies: DOJ litigation, funding conditions, and agency proceedings that each need their own hook.
  • Prefill and decode (W18, W23) — prefill processes the whole prompt in parallel and is compute-bound; decode generates one token at a time and is memory-bandwidth-bound. That gap is why input tokens are priced far below output tokens.
  • Presumption of conformity (R8, S6) — conforming to a cited harmonised standard counts as conforming to the AI Act requirement behind it, so the operative technical content sits in CEN-CENELEC JTC 21. The GPAI Code of Practice is the same move: voluntary text buying presumption against a fining regime.
  • Prompt injection (W31) — instructions arriving inside attacker-controlled content the agent processes as data. Delimiters and system prompts move probabilities, not permissions, and a filter stopping 99% is a failing grade against an attacker with unlimited attempts.
  • Provenance (data) (W19) — a per-document record of origin URL, crawl date, and license or opt-out status. Without it you cannot trace an eval leak, honor a deletion request, excise a poisoned source, or audit representation. It is captured at collection or it is gone.
  • Quantization (W23) — storing weights and KV state in fewer bits to cut the bytes moved during memory-bound decode. It shifts the logits and can change outputs, which is where it differs from speculative decoding.
  • RAG (retrieval-augmented generation) (W29) — retrieving passages and generating conditioned on them. Measured at two joints that are never blended: did the right evidence surface, and does each cited source support the sentence citing it. A citation is nearly free to emit and is not support.
  • Reliability (evaluation) (W36) — whether equivalent administrations give the same reading. It improves with more samples and repeats. A benchmark name under-determines the measurement, so two labs' scores on "the same" benchmark are incomparable unless the protocol is fixed and disclosed.
  • Reward hacking (W25, W42) — a policy finding the reward model's systematic errors, since optimization is a search rather than a survey: verbosity scored as quality, agreement as helpfulness. Reward tampering is the next rung, editing the apparatus that computes the reward.
  • Reward model (W25) — a learned proxy for human preference, fit by a Bradley-Terry sigmoid over score differences within a prompt. It never learns absolute quality, so comparing raw scores across prompts or models is a category error.
  • RLVR (reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards) (W28) — training against a grader that is cheap, consistent, and no more wrong on the millionth query than the first. It dodges learned-proxy overoptimization and constrains nothing the rule does not read, the reasoning trace included.
  • RSP / frontier safety framework (S5) — a developer's published if-then architecture pairing capability thresholds with required safeguards. A hard commitment needs a named actor, a checkable trigger, and a dated duty. Anything missing one is a discretionary hatch.
  • SAE (sparse autoencoder) (W39) — a sparse, overcomplete dictionary trained so that a few active features reconstruct a residual-stream activation. Its features are the dictionary's atoms, not certified variables of the model's own computation, and coverage is conceded to be incomplete.
  • Safe harbor (R3, R4) — a provision shielding a party from liability when stated conditions are met. Colorado's affirmative defense for programs aligned to the NIST AI RMF is one; CFPB Circular 2023-03 is a refusal to grant one to lenders relying on the sample forms.
  • Safety case (W43, S5) — a structured argument that a system is safe enough to deploy, in four types: inability, control, trustworthiness, deference. Capability gates mostly instantiate inability, the type evaluation awareness stresses hardest.
  • Sandbagging (S5, C1) — a model underperforming on an evaluation it could pass. It empties an inability safety case, and a documented instance is the standing example of an update trigger that would flip a recommendation.
  • SB 53 (TFAIA) (R3, W21) — California's Transparency in Frontier Artificial Intelligence Act, signed 29 September 2025. It reaches developers above $500M revenue whose models exceed 10^26 operations, counted cumulatively including later fine-tuning and self-measured.
  • Scaffold (W30) — the orchestration, tools, permissions, memory, and interface bolted around a model. Capabilities are a joint property of the two, so a bare-model benchmark reports a property of the wrong object.
  • Scaling law (W20) — a fitted curve of the form L(N, D) = E + A/N^α + B/D^β, where E is the irreducible entropy of the text distribution and only the power-law terms are the model's to reduce. A fit has a regime: the prescription flipped between Kaplan and Chinchilla inside one architecture family.
  • Serving cost (W23) — a property of the workload, not just the model. Prompt-to-output ratio, context length, concurrency, cache reuse, and the latency target set the price per token. Parameter count only sets the scale, and a long-context workload can make the smaller model the expensive one.
  • SL1–SL5 and OC1–OC5 (W44) — RAND's paired ladders: five weight-security levels indexed to five attacker operational capacities, from a hobbyist with days and $1,000 to a top-priority state operation with years and up to $1 billion. "Secure against OC3" is a claim; "secure" is not.
  • Speculative decoding (W23) — drafting tokens cheaply and verifying them under an acceptance rule that provably preserves the target distribution. It spends idle compute during memory-bound decode to bank several tokens per weight-read.
  • Structured access — a release mode between open weights and no access: the artifact stays with the developer while outside parties get gated, logged, revocable use of named checkpoints. It preserves the rate limits, monitoring, and revocation that weight theft removes.
  • System stack (how to work, W01) — model, posttraining, scaffold, tools, product, deployment, company, institution. Every capability claim and every obligation attaches at one of these levels, and naming the level is half of most analyses.
  • Systemic risk (structural sense) (W45) — harm travelling through a named structure, such as concentration, homogeneity, feedback, or dependency, so that local failures become coupled. Big is not systemic: a harm reaching millions one at a time is a scale problem, and scale problems parallelize.
  • Systemic-risk model (W16, W20) — the AI Act's label for a general-purpose model presumed under Article 51 to have high-impact capabilities above 10^25 FLOP of training compute. A compute threshold rather than a parameter count, so it survives mixture-of-experts, and it triggers Article 55.
  • Test-time compute (W28) — buying accuracy after training ends, by sampling more, searching with a verifier, or generating longer reasoning. Verifier-limited before it is sample-limited, and invisible to any training-FLOP threshold.
  • Third-party audit (R8) — not one thing. The auditee usually selects the auditor, pays the auditor, and defines the scope. Independence has to be engineered: accreditation, regulator-set minimum scope, rotation, a ban on advisory work for the audited entity, and publication of adverse findings.
  • Time horizon (W32, W38) — a fitted threshold on a task suite, not unattended runtime. Tasks are indexed by measured human completion time and the 50% horizon is where the fitted success curve crosses a coin flip. METR's doubling estimate is 207 days, 95% bootstrap CI 166–240.
  • Uplift (W41) — a counterfactual quantity: what this actor achieves with the model minus what the same actor achieves without it. Every uplift claim carries a baseline somebody chose, and a frozen baseline year and a floating one fail in opposite directions.
  • Verification families (S4, S6) — national technical means, access-dependent inspection, and hardware-dependent methods. Only the third offers per-chip granularity with confidentiality preserved, so HEMs sit under verification and verification sits under any treaty worth signing.
  • Watermarking (W34) — a statistical mark embedded at generation. It survives ordinary re-posting where a hash-bound manifest does not, weakens under paraphrase and heavy edits, and cannot be enforced on open-weight models, where the mark lives in a swappable sampling layer.
  • Windfall clause — an advance commitment by a developer to redistribute profits above some extraordinary share of world output. An intervention family in the matrix with no live instrument behind it anywhere.

Discussion Paper: Large Language Models and Personal Data (HmbBfDI, English)https://datenschutz-hamburg.de/fileadmin/user_upload/HmbBfDI/Datenschutz/Informationen/240715_Discussion_Paper_Hamburg_DPA_KI_Models.pdfdurablehand-verified 2026-07-31 (full PDF read)Resolves the standing w02 flag (cited since C1, never link-verified). Official English translation, 10 pp., 15 July 2024; plain curl returns 200, no browser UA needed. German original: .../240715_Diskussionspapier_HmbBfDI_KI_Modelle.pdf. Thesis 1 verbatim: "The mere storage of an LLM does not constitute processing within the meaning of article 4 (2) GDPR. This is because no personal data is stored in LLMs." §II argues it through tokenisation and embeddings ("abstract mathematical representations"; "vectors in a multidimensional vector space"; tokens as "linguistic fragments"), which is why w02 owns the hook. Now linked from w02w02
EDPB Opinion 28/2024 (AI models and personal data)https://www.edpb.europa.eu/system/files/2024-12/edpb_opinion_202428_ai-models_en.pdfdurableverified 2026-07-31HTTP 200 to plain curl. Adopted 17 Dec 2024 at the Irish supervisory authority's request; holds that an AI model trained on personal data cannot in all cases be considered anonymous, and that anonymity must be assessed case by case against extraction and inference attacks. Added to w02 as the counterweight to the Hamburg paperw02
